4Centered bounds and the continuum limit

4.1Centered cell budgets imply finite-cutoff control

This section proves the quantitative step used before taking any continuum limit. The estimates are conditional on a centered cell bound along one fixed finite-volume trajectory. They do not produce that bound from the renormalisation-group flow; the latter is a separate and substantially stronger input recorded in the blueprint. The point of the argument here is to show in detail how a bound on one observable centered in one cell controls arbitrary separated tuples, and how the same bound controls changes of lattice positions.

Write \(a_N\) for the lattice spacing at mesh index \(N\), and \(\ell_{N, p}\) for the number of sites in one coordinate direction of the period-\(p\) torus. Thus its physical linear period is \(P_p = a_N \ell_{N, p}\), independent of \(N\) when the schedule is embedded. A configuration of \(q\) marked points is a point of \(E_q = (\mathbb{R}^4)^q\). Let \(\text{Conf}_q\) be the set of configurations with no coincident points, and let \(\text{Gen}_q\) consist of configurations for which each coordinate projection is injective on the marked points. For period \(p\), let \(\text{Cyc}_p\) be the set where every pairwise coordinate difference avoids \(P_p \mathbb{Z}\), and let \(\text{NoHit}_p\) be the set where each pair is nonperiodic in at least one coordinate. Thus \(\text{Gen}_q \subset \text{Conf}_q\), while \(\text{NoHit}_p\) permits coordinatewise ties. We use the sup norm on each spatial coordinate when discussing separations. A tuple is cyclically separated at scale \(r\) if it lies in \(\text{Cyc}_p\) and, for each pair and coordinate, its coordinate difference has distance at least \(r\) from every multiple of \(P_p\). It is separated in some coordinate at scale \(r\) if for each pair at least one coordinate has this property. No condition is imposed on tuples with coincident marked points when we assert a compact-set bound only away from collisions.

For a local observable \(O\) (that is, one of the tokens in the fixed observable register of the finite-volume family) and a lattice site \(x\), let \(O_N (x)\) be its normalized finite-volume readout. For a finite list \(\mathbf{O}= (O_0, \ldots, O_{q - 1})\) and sites \(\mathbf{x}= (x_0, \ldots, x_{q - 1})\), write

\(\displaystyle F_{N, p, \mathbf{O}} (\mathbf{x}) =\mathbb{E}_{N, p} \hspace{-0.17em} \left[ \prod_{i = 0}^{q - 1} {O_i}_N (x_i) \right] .\)

The same notation is used for the lifted lattice function on \(E_q\). The list is allowed to contain any fixed local observables, including reflected or conjugated observables. A centered cell of physical width comparable to \(a_m\) is the centered link tiling at depth \(m \leqslant N\), with its first chamber centered at the chosen site. Call a complex observable chamber-admissible if it is gauge invariant, measurable, uniformly bounded on the finite-volume configuration space, and depends only on link variables whose two endpoints lie in that chamber. Admissibility does not mean that the observable is pointwise nonnegative; reflection positivity is a property of the expectation and the reflected product.

Definition 4.1. (Centered budget) For an observable \(O\), a nonnegative number \(A\), and indices \(m \leqslant N\), a centered budget \(\textsf{CB} (O ; A, m, N, p)\) means: (i) the depth of \(O\) is represented at volume \(N\); (ii) at every torus site \(x\), the readout \(O_N (x)\) is chamber-admissible for the home chamber of the centered tiling of depth \(m\) based at \(x\); and (iii) its centered root test is at most \(A\). The root test is the \(M\)th root of the absolute normalized expectation of the product of the observable copied, with the prescribed reflected/conjugated orientation, to all \(M\) cells of that tiling. The reflected copies and the root test use the finite-volume reflection-positive transfer structure.

For a centered tiling \(\mathcal{T}\) with \(M\) cells, write

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{T}} (F) := \left| \mathbb{E}_{N, p} \left[ \prod_{C \in \text{Cell} (\mathcal{T})} F^{(C)} \right] \right|^{1 / M},\)

where \(F^{(C)}\) is the prescribed translated copy of \(F\), reflected in each axis whose cell coordinate is odd and conjugated exactly when an odd number of axes are reflected. This notation is used only when the observable is admissible in the home chamber and the normalized expectation is defined.

Proposition 4.2. (Finite-cell dissemination from positive seams) Fix one finite torus, one normalized probability law \(\mu\) on its configurations, and one tiling \(\mathcal{T}\). Write its side as \(2 h \ell\), where \(h, \ell \geqslant 1\) are integers, and index its cells by \(\mathcal{C}= (\mathbb{Z}/ (2 h)\mathbb{Z})^4\), so \(M = (2 h)^4 > 0\). All seams have the same kind: put \(\delta = 1\) for link seams and \(\delta = 0\) for site seams. For a fixed base site \(b\), cell \(v\) starts at \(b + \ell v\). Its strict chamber consists of the sites whose relative coordinates \(r_i\) satisfy \(0 \leqslant r_i < \ell\) for link seams, and \(0 < r_i < \ell\) for site seams. Observables are complex, bounded, measurable and gauge invariant, and depend only on links with both endpoints in the indicated strict chamber.

For \(v \in \mathcal{C}\), define the affine signed map \(\phi_v\) by

\(\displaystyle (\phi_v (x))_i = b_i + \ell v_i + \left\{ \begin{array}{ll} x_i - b_i, & v_i \text{ even},\\ \ell - \delta - (x_i - b_i), & v_i \text{ odd}, \end{array} \right. \qquad \pmod{2 h \ell} .\) (4.1)

Parity is well defined modulo \(2 h\). Let \(P_{\phi_v}\) be the pullback of configurations along this map, including reversal of oriented links. The copy \(F^{(v)}\) is \(F \circ P_{\phi_v}\), conjugated exactly when an odd number of the coordinates \(v_i\) are odd. Use these same copies in \(\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{T}}\).

For every \(p \in \mathcal{C}\) and coordinate \(a\), assume the law is invariant under the configuration pullback \(R_{p, a}\) of the site reflection

\(\displaystyle x_a \longmapsto 2 (b_a + \ell p_a) - \delta - x_a, \qquad x_i \longmapsto x_i \quad (i \ne a) .\)

Assume also seam positivity on its positive strict half torus: for every bounded measurable gauge-invariant \(H\) depending only on links with both endpoints in that half,

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E}_{\mu} [H \hspace{0.17em} \overline{H \circ R_{p, a}}] \in [0, \infty) .\) (4.2)

The positive half has relative \(a\)-coordinate in \([0, h \ell)\) for link seams, and in \((0, h \ell)\) for site seams. Then for every family of home-chamber observables \((F_v)_{v \in \mathcal{C}}\),

\(\displaystyle \left| \mathbb{E}_{\mu} \prod_{v \in \mathcal{C}} F_v^{(v)} \right| \leqslant \prod_{v \in \mathcal{C}} \mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{T}} (F_v) .\) (4.3)

For every finite set \(I\), injection \(c : I \to \mathcal{C}\) and such observables \(F_i\), it follows that

\(\displaystyle \left| \mathbb{E}_{\mu} \prod_{i \in I} F_i^{(c (i))} \right| \leqslant \prod_{i \in I} \mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{T}} (F_i) .\) (4.4)

These statements include zero root tests and the empty planted family. There is no power-of-two restriction on \(2 h\).

This is the chessboard principle; compare [Bis09, §5.3, Theorem 5.8]. That statement uses real block functions and site reflections. We give the complex strict-chamber argument, including both seam kinds and vanishing roots, explicitly.

Proof. All products below are bounded measurable functions, hence integrable for the same probability law. The maps in (4.1) carry the strict home chamber into the indicated strict chamber: the reversed relative coordinate is \(\ell - 1 - r\) in the link case and \(\ell - r\) in the site case. Both preserve the stated strict ranges. Their pullbacks therefore preserve locality in the transported chamber; signed link permutations preserve measurability and transport gauge transformations. Conjugation preserves all these properties.

Fix a seam \((p, a)\). On cell indices define the involution \(m\) by

\(\displaystyle (mv)_a = 2 p_a - 1 - v_a, \qquad (mv)_i = v_i (i \ne a),\)

and put \(S = \{v : (v_a - p_a) \bmod 2 h < h\}\), using residues in \(\{0, \ldots, 2 h - 1\}\). If that residue is \(t\), its mate has residue \(2 h - 1 - t\), so \(m\) interchanges \(S\) and its complement. It reverses the parity of coordinate \(a\) and preserves the other parities. Substitution in (4.1), in each of the two parity cases, gives \(\phi_{mv} = r_{p, a} \circ \phi_v\), where \(r_{p, a}\) is the site reflection defining \(R_{p, a}\). Indeed the changed coordinate is \(2 (b_a + \ell p_a) - \delta - (\phi_v (x))_a\); all other coordinates are unchanged. Composition of configuration pullbacks and the parity change now give the exact identity

\(\displaystyle F^{(mv)} = \overline{F^{(v)} \circ R_{p, a}} .\) (4.5)

There is exactly one extra conjugation, even when other coordinates were already reflected.

Each chamber with index in \(S\) lies in the positive half torus: its relative coordinate there is \(\ell t + r_a\) with \(0 \leqslant t < h\). This is in \([0, h \ell)\) for link seams and in \((0, h \ell)\) for site seams. Thus finite products of copies from \(S\) are admissible for (4.2); in particular, no link crossing a seam is introduced.

For positive-half observables set \(B (P, Q) =\mathbb{E}_{\mu} [P \hspace{0.17em} \overline{Q \circ R_{p, a}}]\). Invariance of \(\mu\), the involution \(R_{p, a}^2 = 1\), and conjugation of integrals show \(B (P, Q) = \overline{B (Q, P)}\). The form is linear in its first variable and conjugate-linear in its second, and its diagonal is nonnegative by assumption. Here is the Cauchy–Schwarz argument including a zero diagonal. Write \(u = B (P, P)\), \(v = B (Q, Q)\) and \(z = B (P, Q)\). Positivity of \(P - tQ\) gives

\(\displaystyle 0 \leqslant u - \bar{t} z - t \bar{z} + |t|^2 v \qquad (t \in \mathbb{C}) .\)

If \(v > 0\), take \(t = z / v\) to get \(|z|^2 \leqslant uv\). If \(v = 0\) and \(z \ne 0\), taking \(t = Rz\) with a sufficiently large positive real \(R\) makes the right side negative. Thus \(z = 0\), and the same bound holds also in this case.

For two assignments \(A, D\) of home observables to cells, define

\(\displaystyle [A, D] (v) = \left\{ \begin{array}{ll} A (v), & v \in S,\\ D (mv), & v \not\in S, \end{array} \right. \quad P_A = \prod_{v \in S} A (v)^{(v)}, \quad V (A) = \left| \mathbb{E}_{\mu} \prod_v A (v)^{(v)} \right| .\)

Reindexing the complementary half by \(m\) and using (4.5) proves the pointwise identity

\(\displaystyle \prod_v [A, D] (v)^{(v)} = P_A \hspace{0.17em} \overline{P_D \circ R_{p, a}} .\)

Consequently Cauchy–Schwarz proves, rather than assumes, the fold bound

\(\displaystyle V ([A, D])^2 \leqslant V ([A, A]) V ([D, D]) .\) (4.6)

The operation \(A \mapsto [A, A]\) retains the positive half and reflects its labels into the negative half, with the copy orientations fixed.

Choose any nonempty finite palette of home observables. There are only finitely many assignments from this palette to \(\mathcal{C}\), so \(V\) has a maximum \(K \geqslant 0\). Every fold of a maximizing assignment still maximizes \(V\). To prove this, for a maximizing \(A\) put \(D = A \circ m\). Then \([A, D] = A\), since \(m^2 = 1\). Both folds in (4.6) have value at most \(K\), and

\(\displaystyle K^2 \leqslant V ([A, A]) V ([D, D]) \leqslant K^2 .\)

If \(K = 0\) every assignment maximizes. If \(K > 0\), either factor being strictly less than \(K\) would make their product less than \(K^2\). Thus in both cases each fold is a maximizer, for every translated seam.

We next show in finitely many folds that some maximizing assignment is constant. The following argument applies to any property preserved by all these folds. Suppose an assignment is already independent of the coordinates in a set \(E\), and choose an axis \(a \not\in E\). A strip of \(k\) equal slices starting at \(p_a\) means that whenever \(x, y\) have the same coordinates off \(a\) and their \(a\)-residues relative to \(p_a\) lie in \(\{0, \ldots, k - 1\}\), their labels agree. A strip of length one always has this property.

For \(1 \leqslant k < h\), fold at the seam starting at \(p_a\). In the new strip starting at \(p_a - 1\) of length \(k + 1\), the first slice is reflected to \(p_a\), whereas the remaining slices \(p_a, \ldots, p_a + k - 1\) are kept. All these slices therefore acquire the same labels for each fixed choice of the other coordinates. More explicitly, a new-strip residue \(t = 0\) is sent by the fold to old residue zero; a residue \(1 \leqslant t \leqslant k\) is kept and has old residue \(t - 1 < k \leqslant h\). This proves the extension and checks that the kept slices really are in the positive half. Repeating \(h - 1\) times yields a strip of \(h\) equal slices. Fold once more at its starting seam. Every cell is then either in that half or mapped into it by \(m\), and the map changes no other coordinate. The resulting assignment is independent of \(a\) on the entire torus.

No earlier independence is lost: for \(b \in E\), two cells differing only in coordinate \(b\) lie on the same side of an \(a\)-seam. If kept, their old labels agree; if reflected, their images still differ only in \(b\), so their old labels also agree. Thus all folds just used preserve independence of every coordinate in \(E\). This also covers \(h = 1\), when only the final half-to-whole fold is needed. Performing this construction for the four coordinates makes the assignment independent of all of them. Changing coordinates one at a time then shows it is constant. Since all folds preserve maximality, the original maximum \(K\) is attained by a constant assignment from the same palette. This finite argument uses no divisibility beyond the even cell period.

Finally fix \((F_v)\) and \(\varepsilon > 0\), and abbreviate \(\rho (F) =\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{T}} (F)\) and \(r_v = \rho (F_v) + \varepsilon > 0\). Apply the maximum conclusion to the finite palette \(X_v = F_v / r_v\). Positive real scaling commutes with every copy, including its conjugation. A constant assignment \(X_v\) has value

\(\displaystyle V (\text{constant } X_v) = \frac{\rho (F_v)^M}{r_v^M} \leqslant 1.\)

Hence every assignment from this palette has value at most one, in particular the assignment putting \(X_v\) at \(v\). Pulling its positive real constants out of the product and integral gives

\(\displaystyle V ((F_v)) \leqslant \prod_v (\rho (F_v) + \varepsilon) .\)

The right side is a finite product of continuous functions of \(\varepsilon\). Letting \(\varepsilon \downarrow 0\) proves (4.3), without dividing by a possibly zero root. This scaling argument uses only the definition of the root test, not its triangle inequality.

The constant function \(1\) is admissible, every copy of it is \(1\), and normalization gives \(\rho (1) = |\mathbb{E}_{\mu} 1|^{1 / M} = 1\). For an injective \(c\), fill cell \(c (i)\) with \(F_i\) and every unoccupied cell with \(1\). The full-cell inequality is exactly (4.4); injectivity makes the assignment unambiguous. For \(I = \varnothing\) both sides are \(1\).\(\Box\)

In particular, Proposition 4.2 supplies the full-cell premise of Lemma 4.3 below for this same law, tiling and copy convention. Its seam positivity is an upstream assumption, not a consequence of the root-seminorm argument.

Lemma 4.3. (Seminorm from the planted inequality) Fix one normalized finite-volume Wilson law, one centered tiling \(\mathcal{T}\), and its home chamber, with \(M = | \text{Cell} (\mathcal{T}) | \geqslant 1\). Let \(\mathcal{A}\) be the complex chamber-admissible class defined above, with dependence only on links whose two endpoints lie in the same strict home chamber. Keep the prescribed copy maps and their conjugation choices fixed. Assume the full-cell case of the planted inequality used in Theorem 4.5: for every family \((H_C)_{C \in \text{Cell} (\mathcal{T})}\) in \(\mathcal{A}\),

\(\displaystyle \left| \mathbb{E}_{N, p} \prod_{C \in \text{Cell} (\mathcal{T})} H_C^{(C)} \right| \leqslant \prod_{C \in \text{Cell} (\mathcal{T})} \mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{T}} (H_C) .\) (4.7)

Then \(\mathcal{A}\) is a complex vector space closed under pointwise conjugation, and \(\rho =\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{T}}\) is a finite-valued complex seminorm on it, invariant under conjugation. Explicitly, for \(F, G \in \mathcal{A}\) and \(z \in \mathbb{C}\),

\(\displaystyle \rho (0) = 0, \qquad \rho (F) \geqslant 0, \qquad \rho (zF) = |z| \rho (F), \qquad \rho (F + G) \leqslant \rho (F) + \rho (G), \qquad \rho (\bar{F}) = \rho (F) .\)

Consequently, for every finite index set \(I\), complex coefficients \(z_i\) and \(F_i \in \mathcal{A}\),

\(\displaystyle \sum_{i \in I} z_i F_i \in \mathcal{A}, \qquad \rho \hspace{-0.17em} \left( \sum_{i \in I} z_i F_i \right) \leqslant \sum_{i \in I} |z_i | \rho (F_i) .\) (4.8)

In particular, if \(\rho (F_i) \leqslant A_i\) with \(A_i \geqslant 0\), the right side may be replaced by \(\sum_i |z_i | A_i\), including zero budgets and the empty sum. No positive-definiteness assertion is made for \(\rho\).

Proof. The zero function is admissible. Gauge invariance and dependence only on the specified links are preserved by addition, scalar multiplication and conjugation: configurations agreeing on those links give equal values before and after any of these operations. Measurability is preserved by the same operations on complex-valued functions. If \(|F| \leqslant B\) and \(|G| \leqslant D\) with finite nonnegative bounds, then \(|F + G| \leqslant B + D\), \(|zF| \leqslant |z| B\) and \(| \bar{F} | \leqslant B\). This proves all the stated closure properties without enlarging the chamber or allowing links across its boundary.

For each cell \(C\), write \(P_C\) for its fixed pullback on configurations and \(\epsilon_C \in \{0, 1\}\) for its conjugation choice. Thus \(F^{(C)} = F \circ P_C\) if \(\epsilon_C = 0\), and \(F^{(C)} = \overline{F \circ P_C}\) if \(\epsilon_C = 1\). These geometric pullbacks are measurable; copied functions and all their finite products are therefore bounded and measurable. They are integrable for the fixed probability law. All expectations below are thus ordinary finite integrals. The definition gives finiteness and nonnegativity of \(\rho\). Since \(M \geqslant 1\), the product of the copies of zero is zero, so \(\rho (0) = 0\).

Put \(w_C = z\) when \(\epsilon_C = 0\) and \(w_C = \bar{z}\) otherwise. Before taking any absolute value, one has \((zF)^{(C)} = w_C F^{(C)}\). Linearity of the expectation and \(| \prod_C w_C | = |z|^M\) give

\(\displaystyle \rho (zF)^M = \left| \left( \prod_C w_C \right) \mathbb{E}_{N, p} \prod_C F^{(C)} \right| = |z|^M \rho (F)^M .\)

Taking the unique nonnegative \(M\)th root proves absolute homogeneity, also when \(z = 0\). No equality between the numbers of conjugated and unconjugated cells is needed.

Each copy is additive, including when it conjugates. For every subset \(S \subseteq \text{Cell} (\mathcal{T})\) set \(H_{S, C} = F\) if \(C \in S\) and \(H_{S, C} = G\) otherwise. Finite distributivity gives the pointwise identity

\(\displaystyle \prod_C (F + G)^{(C)} = \sum_{S \subseteq \text{Cell} (\mathcal{T})} \prod_C H_{S, C}^{(C)} .\)

Integrating this finite sum, applying the complex triangle inequality to its expectations, and then applying (4.7) separately to each assignment yields

\(\displaystyle \begin{aligned} \rho (F + G)^M & \leqslant \sum_{S \subseteq \text{Cell} (\mathcal{T})} \left| \mathbb{E}_{N, p} \prod_C H_{S, C}^{(C)} \right|\\ & \leqslant \sum_{S \subseteq \text{Cell} (\mathcal{T})} \prod_{C \in S} \rho (F) \prod_{C \not\in S} \rho (G) = \prod_C (\rho (F) + \rho (G)) = (\rho (F) + \rho (G))^M . \end{aligned}\)

The last equality is again finite distributivity. All factors are nonnegative, so the increasing nonnegative \(M\)th-root function proves the triangle inequality. This argument divides by neither root budget; it includes \(\rho (F) = 0\) or \(\rho (G) = 0\) and every positive finite \(M\).

Both choices of \(\epsilon_C\) give \((\bar{F})^{(C)} = \overline{F^{(C)}}\). Conjugation commutes with finite products and with integration against the real probability measure. Consequently

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E}_{N, p} \prod_C (\bar{F})^{(C)} = \overline{\mathbb{E}_{N, p} \prod_C F^{(C)}} .\)

Absolute values and nonnegative roots give conjugation invariance. Finally induction on \(|I|\), using closure, the triangle inequality and absolute homogeneity, proves (4.8). For \(I = \varnothing\) both sides are zero; replacing each nonnegative root budget by its upper bound proves the final assertion.\(\Box\)

For example, if \(U, U' \in \mathcal{A}\) and \(\bar{U} = U\), the real projection \(V = (U' + \overline{U'}) / 2\) belongs to \(\mathcal{A}\) and satisfies

\(\displaystyle \rho (V - U) \leqslant \tfrac{1}{2} \rho (U' - U) + \tfrac{1}{2} \rho (\overline{U' - U}) = \rho (U' - U) .\)

This uses one law and one chamber for both errors. The lemma proves only a consequence of the planted inequality. Proposition 4.2 supplies that inequality from its explicit seam hypotheses on the same tiling; neither result constructs a centered budget along a selected trajectory.

The quantitative assumption has two distinct orders of quantifiers. For every fixed observable \(O\) and period index \(p\), there is a tail of depths \(m\) such that for each such \(m\) one can choose \(A = A (O, p, m) \geqslant 0\) and a mesh tail on which \(\textsf{CB} (O ; A, m, N, p)\) holds. In addition, for each \(O\) there is a tail of depths \(m\) such that one can choose \(A = A (O, m) \geqslant 0\) for which there is an outer period-index tail and, for each period in it, an inner mesh-index tail on which the budget holds. The constants may depend on the displayed fixed data. This is the precise centered-budget hypothesis used in the next result.

Lemma 4.4. (Finite families share one budget) Suppose each observable in a finite list has the fixed-period centered-budget property (respectively, the expanding-period property). Then, after increasing the depth tail, there is one nonnegative budget for the whole list and one common eventuality on which all the individual centered budgets hold. The same conclusion applies to any finite family of reflected or conjugated observables that belong to the register and have the corresponding centered-budget property.

Proof. For the empty list take budget zero. Otherwise, for each member \(O_i\), choose its depth threshold \(m_i\), and let \(m_{\ast} = \max_i m_i\). Fix any \(m \geqslant m_{\ast}\). At this particular depth, the hypotheses give a possibly depth-dependent budget \(A_i (m)\) and an eventuality for \(O_i\). Take \(A (m) = \max_i A_i (m)\) and intersect the finitely many mesh tails (or the finitely many ordered \((N, p)\) tails). The result is a common eventuality and a single budget for the entire list at this same depth \(m\). Since this works for every \(m \geqslant m_{\ast}\), it proves the asserted eventual-in-depth statement. No monotonicity of the centered budgets as depth changes is used or claimed. There are finitely many orientations of each fixed observable: take all compositions of the four coordinate reflections, with or without conjugation. Whenever these are registered observables with the required property, include the entire finite family in the same list. This argument does not claim a bound uniform over all observables or over an uncountable test class.\(\Box\)

Theorem 4.5. (Centered budgets give local moduli and compact bounds) Assume the finite-volume Wilson family has the following properties, as well as the centered-budget property above. Its fixed schedule satisfies \(a_N \to 0\), \(P_p \to \infty\) along the period schedule, and the nested lattice scales and centered tilings have the stated integer alignment; the finite-volume side is divisible into the \(4 s\) equal chambers used by the centered transfer word. The observable register is closed under the coordinate reflections and conjugation used below. Each registered observable has a fixed finite stencil, its readout is continuous and covariant under translations and coordinate reflections, and conjugation is implemented by the reflected token. Every coordinate cut has a positive seam and the associated continuous transfer representation: a compact metric slice space \(X\), a finite Borel measure \(\mu\), a continuous positive-semidefinite heat kernel \(T\), and continuous kernels \(B_i\) for local slab observables. If a cyclic word consists of bricks \(B_i\) of common width \(w\) and nonnegative integer free gaps \(H_i\), with \(qw + \sum_i H_i = L\), the unnormalized finite-volume expectation is the cyclic kernel integral

\(\displaystyle \text{Cl}_{\mu} (K_1, \ldots, K_r) := \int_{X^r} \prod_{j = 1}^r K_j (x_j, x_{j + 1}) \hspace{0.17em} d \mu^r (x_1, \ldots, x_r), \qquad x_{r + 1} = x_1,\)

for the collapsed cyclic list of \(q\) brick kernels and the individual unit heat steps, which has \(r = q + \sum_i H_i\) kernel factors. The physical tour length is \(L = qw + \sum_i H_i\). Division by the \(L\)-step partition trace \(Z = \text{Cl}_{\mu} (T, \ldots, T) > 0\) gives the normalized Wilson expectation. The finite-volume representation identifies a change of one marked position by \(l\) steps with replacing two free gaps \(H_i, H_j\) by \(H_i - l, H_j + l\). Finally, assume each cut tiling and this same normalized law satisfy the geometric, reflection-invariance and seam-positivity hypotheses of Proposition 4.2, with its copy convention. That proposition then gives, for every such tiling \(\mathcal{T}\), finite index set \(I\), injection \(c : I \to \text{Cell} (\mathcal{T})\), and home-chamber-admissible functions \(F_i\), the planted inequality

\(\displaystyle \left| \mathbb{E}_{N, p} \hspace{-0.17em} \left[ \prod_{i \in I} F_i^{(c (i))} \right] \right| \leqslant \prod_{i \in I} \mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{T}} (F_i)\)

holds. These are the precise locality, transfer, symmetry, reality, seam, and cell-packing properties used below. Then the following four conclusions hold.

  1. For each fixed period \(p\), each compact set \(K \subset \text{Cyc}_p\), each finite list \(\mathbf{O}\), and every \(\varepsilon > 0\), there exist \(\delta > 0\) and a mesh threshold \(N_0\) such that, for \(N \geqslant N_0\), any two lattice tuples \(\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{y}\) whose physical embeddings lie in \(K\) and satisfy \(|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}| < \delta\) obey

    \(\displaystyle |F_{N, p, \mathbf{O}} (\mathbf{x}) - F_{N, p, \mathbf{O}} (\mathbf{y}) | < \varepsilon .\)

  2. For each compact \(K \subset \text{Gen}_q\), each finite list \(\mathbf{O}\), and every \(\varepsilon > 0\), there are \(\delta > 0\) and an outer period-index threshold such that for every period index in that tail the same mesh modulus holds eventually in the inner mesh index. The mesh threshold may depend on the period, while \(\delta\) does not.

  3. For each compact \(K \subset \text{Conf}_q\) and each finite list \(\mathbf{O}\), there are \(D < \infty\) and an outer period-index threshold such that for every period index in that tail the same finite-volume functions have absolute value at most \(D\) eventually in the inner mesh index on lattice tuples embedded in \(K\).

  4. For each fixed period \(p\), each compact \(K \subset \text{NoHit}_p\), and each finite list \(\mathbf{O}\), there is \(D_p < \infty\) such that \(|F_{N, p, \mathbf{O}} (\mathbf{x}) | \leqslant D_p\) for all lattice tuples embedded in \(K\), eventually in the mesh limit. This hypothesis permits coordinatewise ties and applies even when the points are not separated in every coordinate.

The first modulus may depend on the fixed period; the second modulus is uniform in the outer period tail. The third bound has one constant in that tail, with a mesh threshold that may depend on the period. The fourth bound is only for a fixed period. No assertion extends a bound through point collisions.

Proof. If the compact set \(K\) in any assertion is empty, that assertion is immediate. We therefore take \(K\) nonempty when using compact minima below. For the modulus assertions, \(q = 0\) gives a constant empty product and \(q = 1\) gives a position-independent correlation by translation covariance. We now derive the directional estimate for \(q \geqslant 2\). Fix a compact set \(K\) in the fixed-period cyclic patch. Since that patch is open, there is a compact neighborhood \(K'\) of \(K\) still contained in it. Compactness and continuity of the finitely many periodic coordinate-distance functions give a number \(\rho > 0\) such that every tuple in \(K'\) has, for every pair of marks and every axis, distance at least \(4 \rho\) from the period lattice. Choose a centered-budget depth \(m\) with \(a_m < \rho\), and choose one common budget \(A\) for the finite list consisting of the requested observables and their axis-reflected/conjugated versions. For sufficiently fine mesh \(N\), the stencil fit conditions hold and the mesh is small enough for the following integer choices.

Move one marked site by \(l \geqslant 0\) lattice steps in an axis \(\alpha\), where \(la_N \leqslant \rho\). For a negative displacement, reverse the endpoints and apply the same estimate along the reversed segment; the enlarged compact neighborhood keeps both paths admissible. Let \(R = \rho / a_N\) be the corresponding radius in lattice units. The coordinate gaps in the cyclic order exceed the common brick width plus \(2 R\): the compact separation gives at least \(4 \rho / a_N\) lattice steps, whereas the brick has width at most \(2 a_m / a_N < 2 R\). Subtracting that width leaves free gaps greater than \(2 R\). The move changes two free gaps from \((H_0, H_{q - 1})\) to \((H_0 - l, H_{q - 1} + l)\); all other gaps remain fixed, and total tour length is unchanged.

The centered root bound supplies the column estimate needed for this tour. Cut a marked observable at the transfer plane through its centered cell. Locality identifies it with a continuous one-dimensional slab observable, represented by a brick of width \(w = 2 a_m / a_N\) in lattice units. The reflected cut gives a second slab function, obtained from the reflected token; both are admissible in the relevant home chamber and have centered root test at most \(A\). The centered tiling has \(4 s\) cells along that axis. Put the \(2 s\) alternating original and reflected slab functions in the distinct even-indexed cells; their spacing is two cells, and the planted inequality applies to this injection. The planted inequality bounds the absolute expectation of their product by \(A^{2 s}\). Translation and reflection identify that product with the \(s\)-fold reflected column word of the brick. Thus the normalized column moment to power \(\theta = 1 / (2 s)\) is at most \(A\).

We now justify the transfer estimate for the continuous slice kernels; they are not finite matrices. Fix a compact slice space \(X\), finite measure \(\mu\), continuous kernels \(T, B_0, \ldots, B_{q - 1}\), and the positive integer word length \(L = 2 sw\). Choose finite Borel partitions \(\{C_u^{(j)} \}_u\) of \(X\) whose maximum cell diameter tends to zero, and choose a tag \(\xi_u^{(j)} \in C_u^{(j)}\) in each nonempty cell. Form the sampled matrix

\(\displaystyle (K_j)_{uv} = \mu (C_u^{(j)})^{1 / 2} K (\xi_u^{(j)}, \xi_v^{(j)}) \mu (C_v^{(j)})^{1 / 2} .\)

If \(T\) is a positive-semidefinite kernel, \(T_j\) is a positive-semidefinite matrix: it is the Gram matrix of the tagged kernel, conjugated by the diagonal matrix of square-root cell masses. For any fixed cyclic list \(K_1, \ldots, K_r\), multiplication and taking the matrix trace gives the exact finite sum

\(\displaystyle \text{Tr} ((K_1)_j \cdots (K_r)_j) = \sum_{u_1, \ldots, u_r} \prod_{t = 1}^r \mu (C_{u_t}^{(j)}) K_t (\xi_{u_t}^{(j)}, \xi_{u_{t + 1}}^{(j)}), \qquad u_{r + 1} = u_1 .\)

The integrand \(\prod_t K_t (x_t, x_{t + 1})\) is continuous on compact \(X^r\). Since the cell diameters tend to zero, this tagged sum converges to its integral, the cyclic kernel integral \(\text{Cl}_{\mu} (K_1, \ldots, K_r)\). The same quadrature sequence works for the partition word, each shifted tour word, and each fixed replicated column word \(((B_i)^{\ast} B_i)^s\), since these are finitely many continuous cyclic integrands. In particular, if \(Z = \text{Cl}_{\mu} (T, \ldots, T) > 0\) uses \(L\) copies, then \(Z_j = \text{Tr} (T_j^L) \to Z\) and \(Z_j > 0\) eventually. Define the adjoint kernel by \(B_i^{\ast} (x, y) = \overline{B_i (y, x)}\). The column quantities \(f_{i, j}\) converge to

\(\displaystyle f_i = | \text{Cl}_{\mu} (B_i^{\ast}, B_i, \ldots, B_i^{\ast}, B_i) |^{1 / (2 s)},\)

and the centered root bound just proved says \(f_i \leqslant AZ^{1 / (2 s)}\).

For completeness, the finite-matrix estimate applied to these sampled matrices is an immediate Schatten–Hölder bound. For a finite matrix \(A\), write \(\|A\|_r = (\text{Tr} |A|^r)^{1 / r}\) for \(1 \leqslant r < \infty\) and use the operator norm for \(r = \infty\). The two-factor Schatten Hölder inequality is \(\|AB\|_r \leqslant \|A\|_u \|B\|_v\) when \(1 / r = 1 / u + 1 / v \leqslant 1\). It follows from Tropp's generalized unitarily invariant norm Hölder inequality [Tro22, Theorem 6.32, p. 52]: take the unitarily invariant norm to be the Schatten \(r\) norm and its conjugate powers to be \(u / r\) and \(v / r\). The endpoint cases follow by continuity of finite-dimensional Schatten norms as an exponent tends to infinity; when both tend to infinity this is the operator norm inequality. Iterating this two-factor statement with partial sums of reciprocal exponents, and finishing with \(| \text{Tr} \hspace{0.17em} A| \leqslant \|A\|_1\), gives \(| \text{Tr} (A_1 \cdots A_k) | \leqslant \prod_j \|A_j \|_{r_j}\) whenever \(\sum_j r_j^{- 1} = 1\). Put \(Z_j = \text{Tr} (T_j^L)\) and \(\theta = 1 / (2 s)\). For each insertion \(B_{i, j}\) use exponent \(2 s\), so its Schatten norm is

\(\displaystyle f_{i, j} = (\text{Tr} ((B_{i, j}^{\ast} B_{i, j})^s))^{1 / (2 s)} .\)

For a heat factor \(T_j^{h_i}\) use exponent \(L / \text{Re} \hspace{0.17em} h_i\); if \(\text{Re} \hspace{0.17em} h_i = 0\), use \(\infty\). The spectral theorem gives \(\|T_j^{h_i} \|_{L / \text{Re} \hspace{0.17em} h_i} = Z_j^{\text{Re} \hspace{0.17em} h_i / L}\), and \(\|T_j^{h_i} \|_{\infty} \leqslant 1\) on the imaginary boundary after the zero-eigenvalue convention. The reciprocal exponents sum to one because \(q / (2 s) + \sum_i \text{Re} \hspace{0.17em} h_i / L = 1\). Hence

\(\displaystyle \left| \text{Tr} \prod_i B_{i, j} T_j^{h_i} \right| \leqslant \left( \prod_i f_{i, j} \right) Z_j^{1 - q \theta} .\)

Now take \(h_0 = H_0 - z\), \(h_{q - 1} = H_{q - 1} + z\), and \(h_i = H_i\) otherwise. Their sum is constant; each real part is nonnegative for \(|z| \leqslant 2 R\) because every free gap exceeds \(2 R\). The matrix powers are entire in \(z\) by spectral calculus, with the zero eigenspace assigned the constant-zero value. Thus this trace is an entire function \(\Phi_j (z)\) satisfying

\(\displaystyle | \Phi_j (z) | \leqslant \left( \prod_i f_{i, j} \right) Z_j^{1 - q \theta} .\)

For any real \(t \in [0, l]\), the radius-\(R\) circle around \(t\) is inside \(|z| \leqslant 2 R\), so Cauchy's estimate gives \(| \Phi_j' (t) | \leqslant (\prod_i f_{i, j}) Z_j^{1 - q \theta} / R\). Integrate from \(0\) to \(l\). Taking \(j \to \infty\) in the resulting endpoint inequality is justified by the cyclic quadrature convergence above. It gives the continuous-kernel estimate

\(\displaystyle |F_{N, p, \mathbf{O}} (\mathbf{x}+ la_N e_{k, \alpha}) - F_{N, p, \mathbf{O}} (\mathbf{x}) | \leqslant \frac{l}{R} \hspace{0.17em} \frac{(\prod_i f_i) Z^{1 - q \theta}}{Z} \leqslant \frac{A^q}{\rho} \hspace{0.17em} la_N .\)

This is the required one-coordinate bound. It derives the continuous transfer estimate from finite sampled matrices and explicitly passes the traces, denominator, and all column norms to the limit; no finite-dimensional representation of the Wilson measure is assumed.

To compare arbitrary lattice tuples whose embeddings lie in \(K\), change their \(4 q\) scalar coordinates one at a time. Intermediate tuples can lie outside \(K\); by first choosing \(K'\) as above and then taking \(\delta\) smaller than its neighborhood margin, every intermediate tuple remains in \(K'\). The same \(A, \rho\) work at all stages. The sum of the \(4 q\) coordinate displacements is at most \(4 q\) times the sup distance, so

\(\displaystyle |F_{N, p, \mathbf{O}} (\mathbf{x}) - F_{N, p, \mathbf{O}} (\mathbf{y}) | \leqslant 4 q \hspace{0.17em} \frac{A^q}{\rho} \hspace{0.17em} |\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}| .\)

Choosing \(\delta < \varepsilon \rho / (4 qA^q)\), with any sufficiently small neighborhood margin if \(A = 0\), proves the fixed-period modulus.

For the expanding-period modulus, let \(K\) be compact in the generic set, where each coordinate projection separates distinct points. Choose a compact neighborhood still inside that open set. Since its coordinate differences are bounded and the physical period tends to infinity, for every sufficiently large period all pairs are uniformly separated from every periodic image; the same positive \(\rho\) works for all those periods. Choose the centered-budget depth \(m\) with \(a_m < \rho\). The expanding clause gives a common budget \(A\) eventually in period, then eventually in mesh for each such period. The preceding coordinate telescope uses the same \(\rho, A, \delta\) for all these periods, with the inner mesh threshold allowed to depend on the period. This is exactly the expanding modulus. Genericity, rather than absence of full point collisions alone, is needed here because all four coordinate gaps are used by the directional telescope.

We next prove both compact-bound assertions. The cases \(q = 0, 1\) are immediate: the empty correlation is \(1\), while for one observable the planted inequality with one occupied chamber bounds its expectation by its centered root test, at most \(A\); translation covariance makes this uniform in its position. Hence assume \(q \geqslant 2\). Let \(K\) be compact with no full collisions. Define for each pair \(i \ne j\)

\(\displaystyle d_{ij} (x) = \max_{\alpha = 1, \ldots, 4} |x_i^{\alpha} - x_j^{\alpha} | .\)

Its minimum over the finite set of pairs and compact \(K\) is a positive number. For sufficiently large periods, every coordinate difference has absolute value less than half the physical period; hence its distance to the period lattice is its absolute value. It follows that there is \(\rho_0 > 0\) such that every pair in every tuple of \(K\) is separated in at least one torus coordinate by \(\rho_0\), uniformly on a period tail. The same conclusion for a fixed period holds under the weaker NoHit hypothesis: use instead the continuous positive function

\(\displaystyle d_{ij, p} (x) = \max_{\alpha = 1, \ldots, 4} \text{dist} (x_i^{\alpha} - x_j^{\alpha}, P_p \mathbb{Z}) .\)

NoHit says precisely that \(d_{ij, p} (x) > 0\) for every pair; compactness and finiteness of the pair set give \(\min_{x \in K, i \ne j} d_{ij, p} (x) > 0\). This maximum over coordinates is essential: taking the minimum over coordinates would incorrectly rule out coordinatewise ties.

Here is the packing argument, with the two scales distinguished. Choose a coarse depth \(m\) with \(a_m < \rho_0\). Then choose a finer depth \(k \geqslant m\) in the relevant centered-budget tail so that the budget at depth \(k\) is available and

\(\displaystyle 6 qa_k < a_m .\)

For expanding bounds use the expanding centered-budget clause; for a fixed-period bound use the fixed-\(p\) clause. After fixing \(k\), take the mesh index large enough that every fixed stencil fits inside a depth-\(k\) chamber. The finite torus placement lemma supplies a translate of the coarse tiling \(X\) of depth \(m\) such that all \(q\) marked sites lie in its margin of three fine-cell spans. The proof is a boundary count in each coordinate: one point forbids at most \(6 a_k\) of the coarse translation interval, so all \(q\) forbid at most \(6 qa_k < a_m\); hence an allowed translate exists. Let \(D_i\) be the coarse cell of \(X\) containing \(x_i\). These cells are distinct, since two sites in the same cell differ by less than \(a_m < \rho_0\) in every coordinate, contradicting the some-coordinate separation.

The centered budgets are at the fine depth \(k\), whereas the tuple will be placed in a coarse tiling \(X\) of depth \(m\). First choose the home site \(o\) of \(X\) on the fine center grid and in chamber zero of \(X\). Match the entire coarse translated-copy orbit of the observable at \(o\), one copy for each cell of \(X\), injectively to cells of the fine centered tiling at \(o\). The aligned scale divisibility supplies the matching; the required reflected and conjugated orientations are in the finite family for which the common budget was chosen. The fine planted inequality bounds the absolute expectation of the whole coarse copy product by \(A^{| \text{Cell} (X) |}\). By the definition of the root test, the root test of the observable at \(o\) relative to the coarse tiling \(X\) is at most \(A\). This is the full replicated-orbit argument; bounding just the original tuple's \(q\) entries would not prove a coarse root estimate.

For each marked point \(x_i\), let \(D_i\) be its assigned coarse cell. The tiling's pointed-site map chooses \(y_i\) in the home chamber (the chamber based at cell zero), and reflection of the margin shows that \(y_i\) has a margin of three fine-cell spans from the home-chamber boundary. The coarse cell orientation determines a reflected or conjugated token at \(y_i\); signed-coordinate covariance identifies its copy in \(D_i\) with the original observable at \(x_i\). The finite list of all such oriented tokens was included in the common budget. We next move the coarse home point from \(o\) to any such \(y_i\). In each of four coordinates, bracket \(y_i\) by two fine-center-grid points separated by at most one fine span \(d\). The sixteen corner points lie in the margin of two spans. The fixed-stencil fit gives chamber interiority at every intermediate point on these four coordinate segments. For a fixed axis, let \(u_j\) be the root test at successive integer translates of the full coarse copied product, and let \(M_X\) be the number of cells. Translate the entire copied product by one lattice step. In each chamber the translated copy becomes one of the two neighboring translates, with the sign determined by that chamber's orientation. Reflection positivity applied to this translated product bounds its moment by the planted product of the root tests of those neighboring translates. Pairing cells by the involution that reverses the orientation along this axis gives exactly \(M_X / 2\) copies of each neighbor. Thus

\(\displaystyle u_j^{M_X} \leqslant u_{j - 1}^{M_X / 2} u_{j + 1}^{M_X / 2},\)

and raising both sides to the power \(2 / M_X\) gives \(u_j^2 \leqslant u_{j - 1} u_{j + 1}\). If an interior value exceeds both endpoint bounds, choose a maximum \(u_j\) on the finite interval; it is positive. Both neighbors are at most \(u_j\), so the inequality forces both to equal \(u_j\). Repeating this at the adjacent interior maxima propagates that value to an endpoint, contradicting the endpoint bound. This proves the finite-interval maximum principle, including possible zeros elsewhere. Repeatedly applying this maximum principle along the four segments bounds the root at \(y_i\) by the maximum of the sixteen corner roots, all at most \(A\). This proves the coarse root estimate at each needed marked home point, with chamber interiority there; covariance transfers that bound to the coarse insertion associated with \(x_i\).

Now apply the planted inequality in the coarse tiling \(X\) to the injection \(i \mapsto D_i\). It bounds the tuple expectation by the product of the \(q\) coarse root tests, hence by \(A^q\). All choices were uniform over tuples in \(K\), so this is the expanding compact bound; for fixed \(p\) it gives the fixed-period compact NoHit bound. The former uses compactness away from full collisions and the period tail; the latter uses only NoHit at that fixed period and permits coordinate ties. Neither gives control at a torus-coincidence configuration, nor smoothness through a collision diagonal.\(\Box\)

Remark 4.6. (What remains upstream) The theorem assumes the centered budget. The exact upstream estimate needed is: for each grade \(d\), norm bound \(R > 0\), and exponent slack \(\eta > 0\), there is \(A \geqslant 0\) such that, for every depth \(m\) beyond the reference depth for grade \(d\), eventually in volume index \(N\), uniformly for every period index \(p\) and every label \(v\) of grade \(d\) with \(\|v\| \leqslant R\), the centered root test is at most \(Aa_m^{- d - \eta}\), with representation and chamber-interiority. The same volume tail works for all \(p\); restricting either to a fixed period or to the cofinal period schedule gives the two budget regimes used above. No proof of this Wilson estimate is contained here. In particular, the finite-volume cell-packing and transfer arguments above cannot supply its decay in \(a_m\), nor can they supply the required uniformity in labels of bounded norm. This is the next analytic input to establish from the renormalisation-group trajectory.

4.2The centered estimate on the selected Wilson history

Fix the family selected for a compact simple gauge group and positive penalty by the construction theorem. The compactness argument in Section 4.5 needs a uniform root bound for its centered local observables. We derive it from the paid single-source history selected with that family. The essential finite-volume input is a marked-copy estimate: placing one copy in each cell turns the RG bound for a finite census into a bound on the normalized root moment. The scale schedule and the factorial-column estimate then make its cost at most the desired power of the mesh.

Theorem 4.7. (Uniform centered power bound from the single-source receipts) Assume a compact simple gauge group and positive penalty, and a single selected history with the served height, branch, and occupancy fields and one common budget for all labels in each bounded grade ball. Assume the occupancy bounds satisfy \(1 \leqslant r_i\); the selected schedule, its width receipt and fixed finite local stencil at each grade; the factorial-columns condition; the treasury payment \(t_{k (m)} \leqslant q_{k (m)}\); and the subpower column input of Subsubsection 7.4.3. The schedule and quantitative inequalities used below are part of these hypotheses. For each grade \(d\), each \(R > 0\), and each \(\varepsilon > 0\), there is a finite \(A \geqslant 0\) such that for every \(m \geqslant m_{\text{ref}} (d)\), for all sufficiently large volumes \(N\), uniformly in every period \(p\), every label \(v\) of grade \(d\) with \(\|v\| \leqslant R\), and every site \(x\), the centered cell at depth \(m\) and site \(x\) contains the observable in its positive chamber and

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{R}_{m, N, p} (O_{N, p} (v, x)) \leqslant Aa_m^{- d - \varepsilon} .\)

The following quantitative contracts are assumed uniformly on that one history. For each fixed \(d, R\), let \(g_{d, R}\) be the coefficient-ball bound and write \(c_i \geqslant 1\) for the columns, \(\nu_i\) for the factorial order, and \(F_i = \sum_{j < i} \log c_{j + 1}\). Assume \(\nu_i ! \leqslant c_i\), \(F_i = o (\sigma_i)\), and \(\hat{\beta}_i = \bar{\beta} + c_G \sigma_i\) with \(c_G > 0\). The width is

\(\displaystyle W_i = \frac{\hat{\beta}_i^{- n_d / 2}}{g_{d, R} c_i \prod_{j < i} c_{j + 1}}, \quad P (d, R, i) = a_i^d W_i, \quad n_d = 2 (\deg F_d + 4),\)

and its calibrated coefficients obey \(q_i (v) \leqslant g_{d, R} \prod_{j < i} c_{j + 1}\) throughout the ball. The selected opening \(k (m) \geqslant m\) tends to infinity and, for each \(z > 0\), eventually obeys

\(\displaystyle w = \frac{a_m}{a_{k (m)}} \geqslant 1, \quad q_{k (m)} \geqslant 1, \quad w^4 q_{k (m)} \leqslant z \sigma_{k (m)}, \quad \sigma_{k (m)} - \sigma_m = \log w, \quad t_{k (m)} \leqslant q_{k (m)} .\)

For every finite centered census and sufficiently large terminal volume, uniformly in period, site and labels in the ball, assume the common-trajectory served representation, positive weights, vault, occupancy, migration and separation inputs of the finite-family estimate below. The local representative is lawful with nonnegative coupling and evaluation, the represented polynomial is integrable, and the selected threshold admits \(\rho = \hat{\beta}_k^{- 1 / 2} \leqslant 1\); at this radius the treasury bounds the extensive local bound. The width payment and the fixed stencil are uniform in the same variables.

The volume threshold may depend on \(m\), but not on \(p\), \(v\), or \(x\).

The argument below proves this conditional estimate through the actual single-source supplier used by the public construction. The upstream production of the uniform fields is tracked separately and is not folded into this theorem's hypotheses.

Proof.

4.2.1The finite-volume marked-copy estimate

Fix a grade \(d\), a label-norm radius \(R > 0\), a target depth \(m\), and an opened depth \(k \geqslant m\). The finite-dimensional grade-\(d\) label space has compact closed \(R\)-ball, and its coefficient-size function is continuous. Thus

\(\displaystyle g_{d, R} = \max \hspace{-0.17em} (1, \sup_{\|v\| \leqslant R} \text{coeffSize}_d (v)) < \infty .\)

Use this coefficient-size radius in the local payment and width estimates. Write \(a_i\) for the lattice spacing, \(\sigma_i = \log (a_i^{- 1})\), and

\(\displaystyle P (d, R, k) = a_k^d \hspace{0.17em} W (d, g_{d, R}, k), \qquad E (d, R ; m, k) = \left( \frac{a_m}{a_k} \right)^4 t_k + 6 - \log P (d, R, k),\)

where \(W (d, R, k)\) is the width allocated to the grade-\(d\) coefficient ball at depth \(k\), and \(t_k\) is the vacuum treasury per site at that depth. All these quantities are positive where they occur in logarithms. Let \(M_m\) be the number of cells in the centered tiling of depth \(m\). For an observable copy \(U_j\) of the same grade-\(d\) label, write \(c_k (U_j)\) for its column-weighted local production cost: the scale-\(k\) column count times the paid coefficient size of that label in the selected history. The paid-ticket inequality is \(P (d, R, k) c_k (U_j) \leqslant \text{gradeDecay}_k\). In particular this cost includes the column factor required by the local response estimate, even though the weaker unweighted size suffices for the separate propagation induction.

The selected coordinate and width bounds give this payment explicitly. Write \(\Gamma_i \geqslant 1\) for the column count, put \(D_k = \prod_{i < k} \Gamma_{i + 1}\), and let \(q_k (v)\) be the coefficient-size norm of the calibrated entry at level \(k\) for a label \(v\) in the ball. At the grade reference level the calibrated coordinate is \(v\); at each subsequent step its size grows by at most the next column count. Since all column counts are at least one, induction gives

\(\displaystyle q_k (v) \leqslant D_k g_{d, R} .\)

Let \(F_d\) be the grade-\(d\) frame, let \(\hat{\beta}_k > 0\) be the hatted coupling, and put \(h_k = \hat{\beta}_k^{- 1 / 2}\) and \(\mu_d = \deg F_d + 4\). The selected width is

\(\displaystyle W (d, g_{d, R}, k) = \frac{h_k^{2 \mu_d}}{g_{d, R} \Gamma_k D_k}, \qquad \text{gradeDecay}_k = \hat{\beta}_k^{- \mu_d} = h_k^{2 \mu_d} .\)

The grade dilation has norm \(a_k^{- d}\), so the column-weighted cost is \(c_k (U_j) = \Gamma_k a_k^{- d} q_k (v)\). All denominators are positive, and therefore

\(\displaystyle P (d, R, k) c_k (U_j) = W (d, g_{d, R}, k) \Gamma_k q_k (v) \leqslant h_k^{2 \mu_d} = \text{gradeDecay}_k .\)

This uses the calibrated adjacent coordinate bound and the displayed width choice on the same finite-volume history. It does not by itself establish the later subpower estimate for the width.

We now give the finite-family estimate used in the proof. Let \(V\) be the cells of the centered depth-\(m\) tiling, so \(|V| = M_m\). For each \(j \in V\), let \(U_j\) be the transported observable placed at that cell. Write \(\mathcal{C}_V\) for the finite squarefree marker algebra: its basis elements are the modes \(I\) in a downward-closed family \(\mathcal{C}\) of subsets of \(V\) containing the empty set and every singleton; multiplication is disjoint union when the union is an allowed mode, and zero otherwise, and

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{N}_r (F) = \sum_{I \in \mathcal{C}} |F_I | \prod_{j \in I} r_j, \qquad \omega_r (I) = \prod_{j \in I} r_j\)

for positive weights \(r_j\). The mode-zero coefficient is the quiet coefficient. The polynomial pass is the finite exponential series through degree \(|V|\); it satisfies \(\mathcal{N}_r (\text{pass} \hspace{0.17em} F) \leqslant \exp (\mathcal{N}_r (F))\), by submultiplicativity and the ordinary exponential-series bound.

The single-source receipt at each intermediate scale represents the propagated density in the form

\(\displaystyle X (U) = e^{E (U)} \text{pass} \hspace{-0.17em} \left( \text{Mach} (F (U)) + \sum_S H_S (U) \right) \text{field} \hspace{-0.17em} (\bar{K} (U) + \text{Mach} (G (U)) + K (U)) .\)

Here \(\text{Mach} (z) = \sum_{j \in V} \text{send}_j (z_j)\) places a scalar source in the marker for copy \(j\); each local unit \(H_S\) and forced-box source \(K_S\) has marker degree at least two whenever it is nonzero. The field is the sum over admissible families of pairwise disjoint boxes whose FLAG sets have union equal to the global flag set, with each term the product of its box factors. The vacuum terms are \(\bar{K}\) and the vacuum part of the box factors. This is the representation supplied by the source's served-slab receipt.

For positive \(r_j\), the support budgets give four contributions to the bound. The scalar observables contribute at most \(|V| x\) by the first part of the vault bound. The box-marked observables contribute at most \(|V| y\) by its second part. The local unit family and the forced-box family each contribute at most \(|V| f\) by their towed support budgets. Here is the origin-counting step in full. For an active unit \(H_S (U) \neq 0\), choose a nonzero marker coefficient. The degree condition gives a marker \(j\) in that coefficient; the support-placement condition for that unit says its site \(o_j\) lies in \(S\). Thus every active support contains a named origin. For a forced-box term the same argument gives \(o_j\) in its box. If \(a_S \geqslant 0\) is the envelope supplied by the towed bound, its weighted coefficient bound \(q_S (U)\) satisfies \(q_S (U) \leqslant a_S\) and \(\sum_{S : o_j \in S} a_S \exp (e \hspace{0.17em} \text{cover} (S)) \leqslant f\) at every origin. Only active supports need be charged; a zero polynomial has bound zero even if its envelope is positive. Because \(e \geqslant 0\), the exponential is at least one. Summing the envelope bounds over origins therefore gives \(\sum_{S \hspace{0.17em} \text{active}} q_S (U) \leqslant |V| f\). For the unmarked vacuum activities, each active box has a nonempty support and its nonnegative envelope has rooted sum at most \(\kappa\) at each site. Assign one site in the support to each active box and sum the rooted bounds over all \(s\) lattice sites. The total vacuum bound is therefore at most \(s \kappa\). Thus the total bound inside the finite pass is at most \(|V| (x + f)\), and the total bound of the box factors is at most \(s \kappa + |V| (y + f)\).

For the field, sum first over admissible box families. Dropping the disjointness restriction only adds nonnegative terms, so its bound is at most the product over boxes of one plus the bound of that box factor. The inequality \(1 + t \leqslant e^t\) bounds this by the exponential of the sum of box bounds. The same \(1 + t \leqslant e^t\) argument for the finite polynomial pass, together with submultiplicativity of bound, now yields the pointwise estimate

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{N}_r (X (U)) \leqslant \exp \hspace{-0.17em} (E (U) + |V| (x + y + 2 f) + s \kappa) .\) (4.9)

The lawfulness estimate bounds the scalar action by \(\text{scalar} + s \hspace{0.17em} \text{exCost}\), and the pointwise vacuum bound contributes \(s \kappa\). Assume the polynomial-valued function \(X\) is integrable against the prior; its mode coefficients are then integrable. Because the prior is a probability measure and \(|X_I (U) | \omega_r (I) \leqslant \mathcal{N}_r (X (U))\), integrating (4.9) gives

\(\displaystyle \left| \int X_I \hspace{0.17em} d \mu \right| \omega_r (I) \leqslant \exp \hspace{-0.17em} (\text{scalar} + s (\text{exCost} + \kappa) + |V| (x + y + 2 f)) .\)

Assume the action parameters satisfy \(0 < \rho \leqslant \rho_0 \leqslant 1\), where \(\rho_0\) is the flag-vanishing threshold, and \(\beta \geqslant 0\), the Wilson action is lawful, and its representation is pointwise nonnegative. The product prior is the normalized product Haar probability \(\mu\) on edge variables. The integral \(Z_A = \int \text{eval} (A) \hspace{0.17em} d \mu\) is positive and has the explicit lower bound

\(\displaystyle Z_A \geqslant \exp \hspace{-0.17em} \left( \text{scalar} - s \left[ \text{exCost} + \beta \hspace{0.17em} \text{baseBudget} (\rho) + 4 d_G \log (16 / \rho) \right] \right) .\)

Here \(d_G = \dim_{\mathbb{R}} G. \text{Mat}\) is the dimension of the ambient real matrix space used by the gauge representation. For completeness, the Haar small-ball estimate used here follows from a packing argument. Put \(D = d_G\), take \(0 < r \leqslant 1\), and use the Frobenius norm. Every group matrix is unitary and has the same positive norm \(\gamma\), the color scale. The chord is normalized by this scale: \(\text{chord} (g) = \|g - I\|_F / \gamma\). Choose a maximal finite family with pairwise distances strictly greater than \(r \gamma\). Its open balls of radius \(r \gamma / 2\) are disjoint and lie in the ambient ball of radius \(2 \gamma\). Comparing Euclidean volumes gives \(M (r / 4)^D \leqslant 1\). Maximality says the closed balls of radius \(r \gamma\) cover the group. Unitary left multiplication preserves the ambient matrix norm, so each such ball is a left translate of the identity ball; Haar invariance gives them equal measure. Thus \(1 \leqslant M \hspace{0.17em} \mu (B (e, r \gamma))\) and hence \(\mu (B (e, r \gamma)) \geqslant (r / 4)^D\). Taking \(r = \rho / 4\), each edge ball has probability at least \((\rho / 16)^{d_G}\). Restrict each of the \(4 s\) forward edge variables to this ball. A unitary edge and its inverse have the same Frobenius distance from the identity, so the same bound holds for reverse traversals. A four-edge plaquette path differs from the identity by at most the sum of its four edge deviations, hence its chord is at most \(\rho\) and the flag term vanishes. The loop action is bounded by the stated loop cost, and nonnegativity of \(\beta\) bounds the remaining local action by \(\beta\) times the base budget. Independence under the normalized product prior gives event probability at least \((\rho / 16)^{4 sd_G}\). On that event the exponential density is at least the scalar exponential times the exponential of minus the displayed action and vacuum costs. Integrating this nonnegative density over the event proves the bound. Dividing the numerator estimate by this denominator gives the integrated mode estimate

\(\displaystyle \frac{\left| \int X_I \hspace{0.17em} d \mu \right|}{Z_A} \omega_r (I) \leqslant \exp \hspace{-0.17em} \left( s \left[ 2 \hspace{0.17em} \text{exCost} + \kappa + \beta \hspace{0.17em} \text{baseBudget} + 4 d_G \log (16 / \rho) \right] + |V| (x + y + 2 f) \right),\) (4.10)

where \(Z_A > 0\) is the integral of the local representative, \(\rho\) is the small-ball radius below the action threshold, and \(d_G\) is the real dimension of the ambient gauge-matrix space. The base-budget term is evaluated at this same radius and the fixed lawful path parameter. For a selected history with \(k \leqslant N\), the transition-mass identity identifies \(Z_A\) with the terminal Wilson partition function \(Z_{N, p}\). This is a finite-volume estimate; no infinite-volume measure is involved.

Now propagate the terminal birth polynomial \(b_N\) backwards through the finite-volume transitions: \(b_i = T_i b_{i + 1}\) for \(i = N - 1, \ldots, k\). Start from the actual terminal birth, which is integrable because it is a finite polynomial in continuous observables on compact configuration space. At each step the transition density is jointly measurable, nonnegative and uniformly bounded in both configurations. Thus its product with a prior-integrable function is integrable against the product of the two priors, and Fubini's theorem makes the pushed function integrable. The kernel mass identity then preserves its integral against the corresponding normalized prior. The mode extraction commutes with this transition because it is a continuous linear coefficient map on the finite-dimensional polynomial space: applying it before or after integration gives the same scalar integral. Induction therefore preserves integrability, each coefficient identity, and the same partition normalization at every scale. The transition equation and terminal birth identity consequently give

\(\displaystyle \frac{\int (b_k)_I \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_k}{Z_{N, p}} =\mathbb{E}_{N, p} \prod_{j \in I} U_j .\)

The intermediate height, locality, and terminal Fourier statements are the exact hypotheses used to identify the propagated coefficient with the served-slab census; the conversion from these fields to the displayed squarefree bound is set out below. The weighted local-payment inequality is \(r_j c_k (U_j) \leqslant \text{gradeDecay}_k\). Separation controls the cover cost of each multi-marker support, while the occupancy bound controls the number of named origins per site. These are the geometric and payment hypotheses needed for the four support-estimates above.

At the opened depth, the weight march returns its initial value, so \(r_j = P (d, R, k)\) for all \(j\). The local scalar bounds are \(x = 3 \hspace{0.17em} \text{gradeDecay}_k\) and \(y = \text{gradeDecay}_k\); the action estimate has \(f = 1\) and \(\text{gradeDecay}_k \leqslant 1\). Thus \(x + y + 2 f = 4 \hspace{0.17em} \text{gradeDecay}_k + 2 \leqslant 6\). Now specialize the census to the full power set of \(V\) and choose \(\rho = \hat{h}_k = \hat{\beta}_k^{- 1 / 2}\). The selected delayed-coupling bound and vacuum threshold give \(0 < \hat{h}_k \leqslant \min \{1, \theta_k \}\), as in Subsection 4.7. At precisely this radius, the treasury receipt on the same selected history bounds the extensive bracket in (4.10) by \(M_m (a_m / a_k)^4 t_k\). Taking \(I = V\) therefore gives

\(\displaystyle \left| \mathbb{E}_{N, p} \prod_{j \in V} U_j \right| P (d, R, k)^{M_m} \leqslant \exp \hspace{-0.17em} \left( M_m \left[ \left( \frac{a_m}{a_k} \right)^4 t_k + 6 \right] \right) .\) (4.11)

This is the marked-copy inequality with all terms traced: the \(6 M_m\) is the sum of the \(3 \hspace{0.17em} \text{gradeDecay}_k + \text{gradeDecay}_k + 2\) scalar, forced, and action charges; the treasury is the cost of all \(M_m (a_m / a_k)^4\) scale-\(k\) cells.

By definition, the centered root test is \(X^{1 / M_m}\), where \(X = |\mathbb{E}_{N, p} \prod_j U_j |\). If \(X = 0\) the desired estimate is immediate. Otherwise divide (4.11) by \(P^{M_m} > 0\) to get

\(\displaystyle X \leqslant \left( \frac{\exp ((a_m / a_k)^4 t_k + 6)}{P (d, R, k)} \right)^{M_m} .\)

The map \(x \mapsto x^{1 / M_m}\) is increasing on \([0, \infty)\) and \(M_m \geqslant 1\), so taking its value on the two sides yields

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{R}_{m, N, p} (O_{N, p} (v, x)) \leqslant \frac{\exp ((a_m / a_k)^4 t_k + 6)}{P (d, R, k)} = \exp (E (d, R ; m, k)),\) (4.12)

for \(\|v\| \leqslant R\), once the centered tiling has interiority at the chosen site. Translation covariance transfers the estimate from the origin to any site; the finitely many signed-coordinate copies have the same coefficient and payment bounds. In the source proof, the marked-copy inequality is obtained by applying its finite-census RG estimate to the full census, then applying the root-test inequality. No continuum or limiting operation is used in this step.

4.2.2The width bound and the choice of opened depth

Set \(n_d = 2 \hspace{0.17em} \text{winter} (d)\) and, at scale \(i\), write \(c_i\) for the column count, \(\nu_i\) for the factorial order, \(\hat{\beta}_i\) for the hatted coupling, and

\(\displaystyle W_i = \frac{\hat{\beta}_i^{- n_d / 2}}{g_{d, R} \hspace{0.17em} c_i \prod_{j < i} c_{j + 1}}, \qquad L_i = \frac{W_i}{\nu_i !} .\)

These are the actual width, envelope, and paid last-column quantities in the factorial-column estimate. The accumulated column-log estimate \(F_i = o (\sigma_i)\) is proved by the scale-increment telescoping in Subsubsection 7.4.3. The hatted coupling has the exact schedule form \(\hat{\beta}_i = \bar{\beta} + c_G \sigma_i\) with \(c_G > 0\). Thus for all sufficiently large \(i\) there is a fixed \(C > 0\) such that \(1 \leqslant \hat{\beta}_i \leqslant C \sigma_i\), and

\(\displaystyle 0 \leqslant \frac{\log \hat{\beta}_i}{\sigma_i} \leqslant \frac{\log (C \sigma_i)}{\sigma_i} \longrightarrow 0.\)

This proves the logarithmic coupling subpower directly from the affine reference schedule; the actual finite-volume coefficient's running limit is not used in this width step. The paid factorial condition \(\nu_i ! \leqslant c_i\) and the width identity give, for every \(z > 0\), eventually

\(\displaystyle 5 - \log L_i \leqslant \frac{z}{2} \sigma_i .\)

The exact logarithmic expression is

\(\displaystyle 5 - \log L_i = 5 + \log g_{d, R} + \frac{n_d}{2} \log \hat{\beta}_i + \log c_i + \sum_{j < i} \log c_{j + 1} + \log (\nu_i !) .\)

For \(i \geqslant 1\), the sum \(F_i = \sum_{j < i} \log c_{j + 1}\) contains \(\log c_i\); since \(\nu_i ! \leqslant c_i\), it also dominates \(\log (\nu_i !)\). Thus the last three terms are bounded by \(3 F_i\). The fixed prefactor and coupling-log terms are \(o (\sigma_i)\) by the estimates just given. This is exactly the I04 inequality with slack \(z / 2\), and makes the finite-prefix handling explicit. Since \(\nu_i ! \geqslant 1\), one has \(L_i \leqslant W_i\), and therefore \(5 - \log W_i \leqslant z \sigma_i / 2\). For all sufficiently large \(i\), also \(1 \leqslant z \sigma_i / 2\). Hence

\(\displaystyle 6 - \log W_i \leqslant z \sigma_i .\)

The physical coefficient radius is \(P (d, R, i) = a_i^d W_i\), so \(- d \log a_i = d \sigma_i\) yields the exact bound

\(\displaystyle 6 - \log P (d, R, i) \leqslant (d + z) \sigma_i .\) (4.13)

This also explains the constants: the last-column estimate is on \(5 - \log L_i\); the extra \(1\) is absorbed into \(z \sigma_i / 2\), and the physical grade factor contributes exactly \(d \sigma_i\). No additional arbitrary width slack is hidden in (4.13).

Let \(k (m)\) be the depth opened by the selected schedule for target depth \(m\). The schedule gives \(k (m) \geqslant m\) and \(k (m) \to \infty\). Given \(\eta > 0\), choose

\(\displaystyle 0 < z \leqslant \min \left\{ \frac{\eta}{4}, \frac{\eta}{4 (d + \eta)} \right\} .\)

The schedule's pay bound and the vacuum-treasury estimate give, eventually in \(m\),

\(\displaystyle w^4 q_{k (m)} \leqslant z \sigma_{k (m)}, \qquad t_{k (m)} \leqslant q_{k (m)}, \qquad w = \frac{a_m}{a_{k (m)}} \geqslant 1,\)

where \(q_k \geqslant 1\) is the paid fiat at depth \(k\). Also \(\sigma_{k (m)} - \sigma_m = \log w \leqslant w^4 q_{k (m)}\). Combining these facts with (4.13) yields

\(\displaystyle E (d, R ; m, k (m)) \leqslant (d + 2 z) \sigma_{k (m)} \leqslant (d + \eta) (1 - z) \sigma_{k (m)} \leqslant (d + \eta) \sigma_m .\)

The middle inequality follows from the two restrictions on \(z\). Hence, for any \(0 < \eta < \varepsilon\), the opened-depth estimate is at most \(a_m^{- d - \eta}\) for every sufficiently large \(m\).

For the finitely many smaller depths, choose an opened \(k (m) \geqslant m\) and use the finite estimate (4.12). The maximum of \(1\) and the finitely many numbers

\(\displaystyle \exp (E (d, R ; m, k (m))) \hspace{0.17em} a_m^{d + \varepsilon}\)

is a single finite constant \(A\). It is independent of the period, volume, site, and label within the specified coefficient ball. For late depths, \(a_m^{- d - \eta} \leqslant a_m^{- d - \varepsilon}\) because \(a_m < 1\) and \(\eta < \varepsilon\). This proves the power bound at all depths at or beyond the grade reference.

The common interiority tail follows from the fixed finite stencil. Fix \(m\) and \(d\), and let \(S_d\) be that stencil of integer offsets. Since \(a_N \to 0\), for all sufficiently large \(N\),

\(\displaystyle 2 a_N (1 + \max (\{0\} \cup \{|z_{\alpha} | : z \in S_d, \alpha = 1, \ldots, 4\})) < a_m .\)

The schedule has odd block sizes, so the depth-\(m\) cell has an odd integer number of depth-\(N\) mesh steps along each axis, and its centered home site is at least the integer part of half that count from each boundary. The displayed strict inequality therefore places every link used by the stencil, including both endpoints, strictly inside the home chamber. The choice of \(N\) depends only on \(m, d\) and the fixed stencil, not on \(p\), the site, or the label. The selected observable is gauge invariant and continuous, hence measurable and bounded on each compact finite-volume configuration space; together with this link locality it is chamber-admissible. Intersecting this tail with the root-estimate tail gives the asserted common eventual volume threshold.

4.2.3The centered supplier on the public construction route

Write \(S_B = e \{2 (16 B + 9)\}^4\) for the packing factor of Subsection 7.8.

The public construction uses a single-source per-label supplier, followed by a downward induction across the scale transitions. We spell out the quantitative propagation and cost checks; the local transfer argument itself is supplied in Subsection 7.8. The public route does not call the separate multisource-transfer sequence construction. The finite-menu nonlinear response remains an explicit selected-history input to this conditional proof. At the adjacent step it must provide ordinary and flagged polynomial ledgers for finite nonquiet offers supported in their crests, under the history's regularity, arrival, response-rate, slot, and shipment-tariff conditions. The ordinary ledger is paid at exponent equal to the lower profile exponent plus \(1 / 2\) and site budget \(1\); the flagged ledger is paid at exponent plus \(1\) and site budget \(2 \exp (- \mu \Delta_{G, L} (\beta)^2 / 16)\). Its push identity fixes the quiet coefficient and the specified singleton coefficients for each box only; no arbitrary nonquiet coefficients are assumed. The offer threshold in this application is the upper support exponent minus \(4\), with allowance fixed by that step's tariff. The conditional response record also retains propagation, tuning, slot separation, and the finite-support bound data used below. Theorem 13.30 supplies this record uniformly on the public run under its common-purchase hypotheses. The adjacent-scale finite-support input is produced from the selected history: its nested realization, tariff, climbing-interface data, and support-exponent bounds are passed through the one-step response construction. At step \(i\) the two support exponents are at least \(100\), the bound shrinkage \(d_i = \delta_i\) lies in \((0, 1]\), and the one-step total bound \(t_i\) and local-response allowance \(y_i\) obey the exact inequalities displayed below. These are the finite-support response and one-step payment data required by the local-transfer step. The tariff and compatibility data are carried on the same chosen history; their producer is identified in the evidence file. At a step \(i\), the resulting one-step conditions are \(\nu_i, \nu_{i + 1} \geqslant 100\), \(0 < d_i \leqslant 1\), and, with \(T_i = r_i \hspace{0.17em} S_{B_{i + 1}} d_i + 4 d_i\), \(T_i + e^{T_i} T_i \leqslant t_i\) and \((2 B_i + 1)^4 (y_i + r_i \hspace{0.17em} S_{B_i} d_i) \leqslant \tfrac{1}{2}\), \(e^{y_i + r_i \hspace{0.17em} S_{B_i} d_i} - 1 \leqslant 1\). These are the one-step payment inequalities, with \(t_i\) the total response bound and \(y_i\) the local-response allowance. The climbing witness and tariff are used by the one-step response lemma to produce the finite insertion support; their nonlinear estimate is a separately tracked input, not inferred from scale geometry.

For occupancy and separation, write \(B_i\) for the integer separation scale, \(o_i (j)\) for the designated site, \(r_i\) for the occupancy bound, and \(W_i (j) = \phi_{i - 1} \cdots \phi_k w (j)\) for the marched weight. The scale fields give \(0 < \phi_i, \delta_i\), \(1 \leqslant \delta_i \phi_i e^{B_i / 128}\), \(B_{i + 1}^2 \leqslant \ell_i B_i^2\), and \(8 \leqslant B_i\), where \(\ell_i\) is the integer ratio count. The migration identity is \(o_{i + 1} = \text{embed}_i \circ o_i\), and \(r_i\) is nondecreasing. Occupancy means that each site has at most \(r_i\) names mapped to it. Because the embedding is injective, the fiber of \(o_{i + 1}\) over a site in its image is the corresponding fiber of \(o_i\), and the other fibers are empty. Thus the fine occupancy is at most the old occupancy bound \(r_i\), which is at most \(r_{i + 1}\). Iterating this comparison propagates occupancy from the opened scale through all finer scales. For a mode with at least two names, distinct origins have coarse separation at least \(B_i^2\). The scale comparison gives fine separation at least \(\ell_i\) times that coarse separation. The split inequality \(B_{i + 1}^2 \leqslant \ell_i B_i^2\) therefore propagates the required separation forward.

At a given scale, take any mode \(I\) of cardinality at least two and any support \(X\) containing its origin image. The following tour argument proves the required geometric estimate. Choose a minimizing cover of \(X\) by \(D = \text{cover} (B_i, X)\) balls of radius \(B_i\). In the source connected-halo cover, the centers form a connected graph: two centers are adjacent when their radius-\(B_i\) halos meet or contain sites at max-distance at most one. Thus adjacent centers are at max-distance at most \(2 B_i + 1\). Take a spanning tree. Join each tree edge by a unit max-distance path, advancing coordinates simultaneously so its length is at most \(2 B_i + 1\), and traverse each such path out and back. This gives a walk visiting every center with length at most \(2 (2 B_i + 1) (D - 1) \leqslant D (4 B_i + 2)\). Enumerate its unit steps as \(R (0), \ldots, R (N)\); successive sites, including intermediate sites that need not be cover centers, are at distance at most one. For each origin \(o_i (j)\), choose a covering center and a time \(t_j\) at which the walk visits it; that center is within distance \(B_i\) of the origin. If \(j \ne j'\) are in the mode, their origins are separated by at least \(B_i^2\). The triangle inequality and the unit-speed walk give, after ordering \(t_j \leqslant t_{j'}\), \(t_j + (B_i^2 - 2 B_i) \leqslant t_{j'}\). Therefore the buckets of width \(B_i^2 - 2 B_i\) containing these times are distinct. Since each time is at most \(N\),

\(\displaystyle (|I| - 1) (B_i^2 - 2 B_i) \leqslant N.\)

For \(B_i \geqslant 8\), \(B_i^2 \leqslant 4 (|I| - 1) (B_i^2 - 2 B_i) / |I|\) when \(|I| \geqslant 2\). Combining with the tour length and \(4 B_i + 2 \leqslant 16 B_i\) yields \(B_i |I| \leqslant 64 D\). The support \(X\) can be any support containing the origin image, so in particular

\(\displaystyle B_i |I| \leqslant 64 \hspace{0.17em} \text{cover} (B_i, X) .\)

Set \(a_j = W_i (j)\) and \(v_j = \delta_i W_{i + 1} (j)\). Since \(W_{i + 1} = \phi_i W_i\), the march and the inequality \(1 \leqslant \delta_i \phi_i e^{B_i / 128}\) give \(a_j \leqslant v_j e^{B_i / 128}\), with both weights positive. Multiplying over \(I\) and applying the preceding geometric estimate yields

\(\displaystyle \prod_{j \in I} a_j \leqslant \prod_{j \in I} v_j \hspace{0.17em} \exp \{ \text{cover} (B_i, X) / 2\} .\)

This is the support-bound comparison with cover exponent \(1 / 2\) and multiplier one. Its definition only asks about modes of cardinality at least two, so no singleton or empty-mode case is needed. Together with propagated occupancy, site migration, and positive marched weights, this gives the site/separation package data used downstream. For each label \(v\), let \(A_i\) be the scale-dependent grade dilation, \(\tau_i (v)\) its coefficient in the common-trajectory representation, \(\mathcal{C}_i (v)\) the associated coefficient family charge, and \(\Gamma_i\) the paid column count. Define the paid bond \(\Lambda_i (v) = \|A_i \| \hspace{0.17em} \text{coeffSize} (\tau_i (v))\). The representation supplies the coefficient cone estimate

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{C}_i (v) \leqslant \Gamma_i \text{coeffSize} (\text{core} (\tau_i (v))) \leqslant \Gamma_i \text{coeffSize} (\tau_i (v)) .\)

The dilation step gives \(\Lambda_{i + 1} (v) \leqslant \ell_i^d \Gamma_{i + 1} \Lambda_i (v)\). The paid scale data give, at every \(i\), \(0 < \phi_i \leqslant 1\), \(\phi_i \ell_i^d \Gamma_{i + 1} \zeta_i \leqslant \zeta_{i + 1}\), \(\zeta_i \Gamma_i \leqslant 1\), \(\psi_i \geqslant 0\), and \(2 \hspace{0.17em} \text{orbitCost} (\psi_i + 2 \Gamma_{i + 1}) \zeta_{i + 1} \leqslant 1\). The opened-scale payment is \(w_k \Lambda_k (v) \leqslant \zeta_k\). The marched weights satisfy \(w_{i + 1} = \phi_i w_i\). Multiplying \(\Lambda_{i + 1} (v) \leqslant \ell_i^d \Gamma_{i + 1} \Lambda_i (v)\) by \(w_{i + 1}\) and using the displayed bound inequality gives \(w_{i + 1} \Lambda_{i + 1} (v) \leqslant \zeta_{i + 1}\) from \(w_i \Lambda_i (v) \leqslant \zeta_i\); induction proves the bound at all scales. The one-step estimate charges the observable by \(b = w_{i + 1}\), and \(b \leqslant w_i\), so \(b \|A_i \| \mathcal{C}_i (v) \leqslant b \Lambda_i (v) \Gamma_i \leqslant w_i \Lambda_i (v) \Gamma_i \leqslant \zeta_i \Gamma_i \leqslant 1\). For the finite-support increment, put \(b = w_{i + 1}\) and \(r = w_i\). The march has \(b \leqslant r\); the adjacent paid bound and the column budget give exactly

\(\displaystyle 2 b \|A_{i + 1} \| \hspace{0.17em} \text{orbitCost} \hspace{0.17em} \text{coeffSize} (\tau_{i + 1} (v)) (\psi_i + 2 \Gamma_{i + 1}) \leqslant 1.\)

These are the two numeric inequalities passed to the scale-step construction. The common-trajectory compatibility bound is a finite set of tales, one for each coefficient in the frame; each tale relates the fine exponential entry and forced profile to the coarser forced profile, with the gauge-interface carrying the same local coordinate change. Together with the gauge-interface transport law, it yields a finite family of insertions and the two bounds \(2 w_{i + 1} s \leqslant 1\) and \(w_{i + 1} t \leqslant 1\). The charge estimate above is the first term in that tale budget; the displayed orbit-cost inequality pays the remaining coefficient increment and column contribution.

Here is the per-scale profile construction. Reorient the coefficient by the selected motion and gauge move, and denote the resulting audited coefficient by \(Q_i (v)\). Let \(F_i (v)\) be its exponential-entry field and let \(g_i (v)\) be the sequence of marked activities obtained by applying the grade dilation \(A_i\) to its forced activities. For clarity, the coefficient coin is

\(\displaystyle \chi_i (v ; U) = (R_i . \text{eval} (U)) F_i (v ; U) + e^{E_i (U)} \sum_j N_{i, j} (g_i (v) ; U),\)
