where \(E_i\) is the scalar action exponent. The audition identities identify this coin with the oriented density and identify the marked profile with the motion-transported forced profile. The remnant estimate gives a summable dominating function for \(Q_i (v) . \text{forced}\), hence for \(g_i (v)\). Reorientation preserves the normalized prior because it is a composition of a lattice motion and a gauge change; therefore the transformed ordinary field remains integrable. At the terminal level the same identity is the birth coefficient equation \(R_N . \text{eval} (U) b_N (v ; U) = \chi_N (v ; U)\). This proves the profile and coefficient identities used at every scale, rather than only a formal compatibility of names.

At terminal scale, the calibrated response construction uses the actual attainment, the place bound

\(\displaystyle \|S (\text{axis}_j, 0, U)\| \leqslant t,\)

cover number at most one, and bank inequality \(e^{\nu_N + 2} \mathfrak{c}_N (t) \zeta_N \leqslant u_N\) to produce the response with \(w_N a_N \leqslant u_N\) and the prescribed forced hook. Here \(\mathfrak{c}_N (t)\) is the full coefficient cost at section budget \(t\). The terminal response's hook agrees with the profile identity above, so the exact terminal birth coefficient is recovered.

Define the pitch \(\pi_i\) by \(\pi_i = \nu_i\) for \(i < N\) and \(\pi_N = \nu_N - 3\). Then \(\nu_{i + 1} - 4 \leqslant \pi_{i + 1}\) at every transition, including the terminal step. Now descend from scale \(N\). The terminal birth identity gives each singleton coefficient as its manifest and gives every allowed mode as the corresponding product of terminal insertions. To check terminal servedness quantitatively, put \(c_N = \text{forcedAllowance}_N\) and \(C_N = c_N + r_N S_{B_N} u_N\). The terminal receipt gives

\(\displaystyle ((2 B_N + 1)^4) C_N \leqslant 3, \qquad e^{C_N} - 1 \leqslant 1, \qquad \nu_N - 3 \geqslant 0.\)

The terminal response at label \(v\) has bound \(a_N (v) \leqslant \|A_N \| e^{\nu_N + 2} \mathfrak{c}_N (t_N) \text{coeffSize} (\text{core} (\tau_N (v)))\). Since core deletion does not increase coefficient size, the paid bond gives \(w_N \|A_N \| \text{coeffSize} (\text{core} (\tau_N (v))) \leqslant \zeta_N\). Hence \(w_N a_N (v) \leqslant e^{\nu_N + 2} \mathfrak{c}_N (t_N) \zeta_N \leqslant u_N\), by the bank budget. Its toll estimate converts this into the required weighted section bound; the forced response is dominated. More explicitly, the terminal bank bound requires \(t_N \geqslant 1\) and

\(\displaystyle e^{\nu_N + 2} \hspace{0.17em} \mathfrak{c}_N (t_N) \hspace{0.17em} \zeta_N \leqslant u_N,\)

and gives the response estimate stated above. Combining this toll control with the terminal birth expansion, Fourier hook, and the two displayed smallness conditions gives the served representation with exponent \(\nu_N - 3\), exactly the terminal pitch.

For the induction step, assume integrability, the singleton manifest identities, and the served representation at pitch \(\pi_{i + 1}\) at scale \(i + 1\). The bounded, measurable transition density preserves integrability as above; applying a mode coefficient before or after integration agrees because coefficient extraction is a continuous linear map on the finite-dimensional polynomial space. Thus each pushed coefficient at scale \(i\) is the transition applied to its scale-\(i + 1\) coefficient. The separate single-insertion transition identity identifies each singleton coefficient with its scale-\(i\) manifest; higher modes remain coefficients of the propagated census polynomial. The one-step geometry supplies positive old and new weights, occupancy at both the old and embedded site families with multiplicity at most \(r_i\), the parent-site identity, and the support-bound comparison. The transported representation is dominated, and its finite-support response satisfies \(2 w_{i + 1} s \leqslant 1\) and \(w_{i + 1} t \leqslant 1\). The one-step payment data are

\(\displaystyle 0 < d_i \leqslant 1, \quad \nu_i, \nu_{i + 1} \geqslant 100, \quad T_i + e^{T_i} T_i \leqslant t_i, \quad (2 B_i + 1)^4 (y_i + r_i \hspace{0.17em} S_{B_i} d_i) \leqslant \tfrac{1}{2}, \quad e^{y_i + r_i \hspace{0.17em} S_{B_i} d_i} - 1 \leqslant 1,\)

where \(T_i = r_i S_{B_{i + 1}} d_i + 4 d_i\) is the prepared insertion bound and \(t_i\) its response budget. The local-transfer theorem of Subsection 7.8 applies with these values; it yields servedness at scale \(i\). Finite downward induction proves the served-slab statement for every \(m \in [k, N]\). The remaining section-compatibility and response-construction receipts used to obtain these displayed one-step inputs are named explicitly in the evidence file; they are part of the conditional input, not consequences of the transfer lemma.

Proposition 4.16 and Corollary 4.17 attach these inputs uniformly on the retained selected history, under their stated common-purchase hypotheses. The inputs are: the levels, interfaces, weights and budgets of the selected run at every terminal volume; the adjacent-scale compatibility, place and bank bounds of the same history; and, at the terminal scale, the birth polynomial, the transition identities and the Fourier hook. Together they form the funded selected-history record; the same record is the hypothesis of Theorem 4.15. The common width and the uniform served-census assertion are proved in that corollary. The present argument uses those fields and the separate scale and opening hypotheses stated in the theorem.

The dependency chain is therefore

\(\displaystyle \begin{aligned} \text{funded selected run} & \longrightarrow \text{paid same-history receipts} \longrightarrow \textrm{(\hyperref[ex-centered-marked-copy]{4.11})}\\ & \longrightarrow \textrm{(\hyperref[ex-centered-opened-root]{4.12})} \longrightarrow \text{uniform centered power bound} \longrightarrow \text{continuum extraction} . \end{aligned}\)

The compact-bound and local-modulus arguments of Section 4.5 use that final centered estimate. The separate multisource transfer route, whose offered transfer sequences already package terminal births and raw adjacent-scale bounds, is not called along this public dependency chain. In particular, the normalized overlay-density bound calculation in the optional multisource draft is not an input here.\(\Box\)

4.3Producing the selected finite-volume transfer data

The centered estimate uses a finite-volume representation at every period and volume. On the public construction route these data are produced on the same selected history: the run chooses one terminal coefficient family, then reconstructs compatible coefficient families at all earlier scales. This section isolates that producer and explains the two algebraic steps that turn the reconstruction into transfer data. The scale-dependent estimate and one-step interpolation estimates remain the quantitative inputs of the selected history; the proof below does not replace them by compactness alone.

4.3.1One-step response and its finite-dimensional assembly

Fix a volume, period, and adjacent scales \(m, m + 1\). Let \(d\) be the chosen grade, let \(\ell_m \geqslant 1\) be the block side, let \(F\) be its finite-dimensional complex coefficient space, and let \(s \geqslant 1\) be the number of paid columns at scale \(m + 1\). Write \(\mu_F (u) = \sum_a |u_a |\) for its coefficient \(\ell^1\) norm, where \(| \cdot |\) is the ordinary complex modulus, and let \(\text{core} (u)\) erase the vacuum coordinate. Every frame coordinate has degree between \(0\) and \(d\). The scale dilation \(\textsf{D}^{\text{gr}}_{d, \ell_m}\) multiplies coordinate \(a\) by \(\ell_m^{d - \deg (a)}\), so

\(\displaystyle \mu_F (u) \leqslant \mu_F (\textsf{D}^{\text{gr}}_{d, \ell_m} u) \leqslant \ell_m^d \mu_F (u) .\)

A coefficient family at scale \(m + 1\) consists of a density family \(f_v\), a nonnegative charge, and an index isomorphism \(\iota : F \to F\). The selected coefficient family certificate requires \(\iota\) to commute with each motion on \(F\). The family is complex-linear in \(v\) and its motion covariance is semilinear according to the independent Boolean conjugation flag in \(\text{Motion} = \text{Signed} \times \text{Bool}\); it is not determined by coordinate orientation. For each \(v\), the selected coefficient family certificate also supplies the format and remainder bounds; the associated finite-volume integrability lemma gives \(f_v \in L^1\) of the normalized prior. We do not use continuity of the densities. The selected transition defines the Wilson push

\(\displaystyle (\textsf{P}_m f) (U) = \ell_m^d \int f (V) \hspace{0.17em} K_m (U, dV),\)

where \(K_m (U, dV)\) is the one-step Wilson transition density against normalized Haar measure and \(\ell_m\) is the block side. Thus \(\textsf{P}_m\) includes the grade-scaling factor \(\ell_m^d\); it is not a probability-preserving Markov operator. The selected transition density is jointly measurable, nonnegative and uniformly bounded in both configurations, and its integral in the coarse configuration equals one for every fine configuration. These properties give slice integrability: for every output \(U\), the transition-weighted section of a prior-integrable input is integrable, and the resulting pushed function is measurable. This is the conditional-kernel conclusion attached to the lawful transition, not a consequence of finite total mass alone. The transition commutes with the selected signed motions. These properties make the push well-defined and linear, and for each signed motion \(g\),

\(\displaystyle g \cdot (\textsf{P}_m f_v) = \textsf{P}_m f_{g \cdot v} .\)

Indeed, both identities follow first for finite linear combinations from linearity of the integral; the motion identity follows by the change of variables preserving the Wilson transition density. These identities are exact, not limiting statements.

The selected ticket's one-column estimates have three exact forms. For each nonzero frame axis \(e_a\), it supplies a coarse coefficient whose density is the pushed fine density, whose orbit-cost-weighted lead error is at most \(\eta \hspace{0.17em} \| \text{core} (e_a)\|_1\), whose activity residual bound is at most \(C \hspace{0.17em} \| \textsf{D}^{\text{gr}}_{d, \ell_m} (\text{core} (e_a))\|_1\), and whose finite gate-crossing certificate costs \(t\). For each regular supported term of mass \(u\), both the lead and remainder bounds are \(\eta u\) and the gate-crossing cost is \(2 u\). For each forced term of mass \(u\) on a marked insertion support site, both the lead and remainder bounds are \(\eta u / \kappa\) and the gate-crossing cost is \(2 u\), where \(0 < \kappa = \sqrt{\text{forcedAllowance}_{m + 1}} \leqslant 1\) uses the fine level. The output remainder is measured with the separate coarse-level forced allowance. Each such transfer record includes a format bound, the exact density or activity identity, an orbit-cost leading coefficient error bound, a finite response expansion, and its paid gate-crossing estimate through the selected interface. These are the exact hypotheses used below; we do not infer their numerical bounds from compactness.

On the public route these transfer records are produced together for the selected adjacent history. A held spring supplies an interval \([x, y]\), a parameter \(z\) in that interval, and a local realization \(K (z)\). The two selected fields are the fine representation \(K (z)\) and the coarse result of that same spring at \(z\). The gate is the composed local comparison between those fields; the same spring and parameter give the climb witness. No strict inequality between \(x\) and \(y\) is needed. For a grade \(d\) with \(d + 3\) no larger than the coarse clock, the ticket's insertion support contains the principal-field tags indexed by supports containing the origin and the marked keeper tags. It is finite because the finite-volume lattice has finitely many supports and keeper tags.

Write \(E_{m + 1}\) for the support exponent at the fine level, \(\vartheta_{m + 1}\) for its profile title, and \(b_m\) for the block side. Denote the coarse and fine clock values by \(J_m^{\text{c}} = J (m)\) and \(J_m^{\text{f}} = J (m + 1)\). The raw transfer record bounds are \(\eta_m = \text{rate} (b_m)\) for the lead and \(C_m = E (J_m^{\text{f}})\) for the residual, where \(E\) is the selected schedule. The exact total gate-crossing allowance is

\(\displaystyle t_m = 2 e^{E_{m + 1}} \vartheta_{m + 1} .\)

The selected clock interpolation chooses one coefficient map for the whole insertion support. Its three families of transfer records are exactly the axis, regular-activity, and forced-activity cases displayed above. In the raw ticket, the axis leading coefficient error bound is \(\eta_m \mu_F (\text{core} \hspace{0.17em} e_a)\), the residual bound is \(C_m \mu_F (\textsf{D}^{\text{gr}}_{d, \ell_m} (\text{core} \hspace{0.17em} e_a))\), and the crossing bound is \(t_m\). For a regular support of mass \(u\), the lead and remainder bounds are both \(\eta_m u\) and the crossing bound is \(2 u\). For a marked forced support of mass \(u\), the corresponding bounds are \(\eta_m u / \kappa\), \(\eta_m u / \kappa\) and \(2 u\). The unit at the origin has its own exact transfer record.

The transfer record-production step is conditional on its field estimates. Assume the selected spring supplies the two adjacent fields at the same parameter and a sound local section; assume the coefficient map forecast and cap satisfy the two-sided map bound, the principal-field estimate has the displayed axis bounds, each supported regular field has the displayed regular bounds, and each slot-fitted forced field at a marked keeper site has the displayed forced bounds. Then one coefficient map and one ticket for the whole finite insertion support are obtained by applying those three estimates to their respective terms and collecting the resulting certificates. The coefficient map forecast comes from the calibrated flight matrix. The selected schedule-estimate pays both the cap's triangular-estimate and the graded remainder bound. The axis, regular, and forced estimates are separate hypotheses; no one of them follows from another. This is the precise conditional finite transfer record package used here. It leaves the derivation of those analytic field estimates and the selected schedule-bound data to their upstream producers.

More precisely, we use the following finite interpolation statement: when the two fields at the selected spring parameter, slot table, motion/rate data, schedule-estimate, and keeper are those of the selected history, and \(d + 3 \leqslant J_m^{\text{c}}\), it returns one coefficient map and a ticket for every insertion support containing the principal-field tags just described. The ticket has axis residual bound \(C_m\), leading coefficient error bound \(\eta_m\), and crossing allowance \(t_m\), with the regular and forced bounds just listed. The coefficient map and all bounds are common to that whole insertion support. Substituting the finite insertion support formed by those tags together with the keeper's marked tags gives the ticket used by the public run. This statement and its three analytic transfer record estimates are inputs here; this conditional assembly does not prove their field-level derivation. Sections 10.9 and 10.10 supply this interface on the same funded interpolation. In their notation \(L = b_m = \ell_m\), \(b_f = E_{m + 1}\) and \(\tau_f = \vartheta_{m + 1}\), so the crossing allowance is exactly \(2 e^{b_f} \tau_f\). Their coefficient allowance is \(C = C_m\); the schedule pays both its triangular and graded remainder requests. The purchase inequalities below belong to the common scale purchase, rather than being conclusions of the local interpolation theorem. Proposition 13.34 attaches this ticket to the retained return and supplies the countable merger needed for its application to a whole coefficient family.

The ticket bounds \(C_m, \eta_m\) are then checked against the one-step reserves. Let \(T_m = B : F \to F\) be the selected coefficient map's coefficient isomorphism. Its dilated lead map is \(B \circ \textsf{D}^{\text{gr}}_{d, \ell_m}\); the two-sided bounds below concern \(B\), not this composition. The purchase inequalities are

\(\displaystyle \mu_F (T_m v) \leqslant C_m \mu_F (v), \qquad \mu_F (T_m^{- 1} v) \leqslant C_m \mu_F (v) .\)

The same selected clock purchase pays the ticket through

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rlll} 1 & \leqslant C_m, & 0 & \leqslant \eta_m,\\ \eta_m (1 + s) \text{orbitCost} (F) & \leqslant \text{stepSmall} (b_m), & & \\ (C_m + \eta_m s) \text{orbitCost} (F) & \leqslant W_{m + 1} . & & \end{array}\)

where \(s\) is the number of paid columns and \(W_{m + 1}\) is the available fine-level column allowance. The one-column theorem combines the raw transfer record with these purchases: after all \(s\) columns its residual and lead allowances are \(c_m = C_m + \eta_m s\) and \(\epsilon_m = \eta_m (1 + s)\), and its crossing bound is \(t_m + 2 s\). These inequalities are the actual interpolation and funding checks consumed by the one-column construction; the proof does not choose a new adjacent pair or reset the history.

The reason the ticket pays these three kinds of term is the local decomposition of the marked field. For a fine coefficient \(P\), first apply the local rack map: it preserves the coefficient coordinate and exponential entry, while its keeper supplies the forced-profile and remainder bounds. The density is then

\(\displaystyle (\text{exponential entry}) \hspace{0.17em} \cdot (\text{local regular part}) + \sum_j (\text{forced activity at } j) \hspace{0.17em} \cdot (\text{dressed field at } j) .\)

The regular summands use the regular transfer records. A forced summand with zero coefficient vanishes. For a nonzero forced coefficient, its support is marked and belongs to the selected finite insertion support; its absolute mass uses the forced transfer record after division by \(\kappa\). The origin term uses the principal axis transfer record. The keeper's remainder bound and the exponential-entry envelope, which are hypotheses of the local rack map above, dominate both countable activity series absolutely; Tonelli's theorem applied to the resulting absolute majorant proves integrability and permits summing the densities term by term. The transfer record-summation identity adds the costs and errors, so the three displayed bounds give the one-column response and preserve the exact pushed density.

The local selected-history ticket is a finite set of marked insertions with a linear cap. It is paid with nonnegative allowances \(C, \eta, t\) and yields an endpoint response on every coefficient of an incoming coefficient family. By the one-column theorem above, the response allowances after accounting for all \(s\) columns are

\(\displaystyle c = C + \eta s, \qquad \epsilon = \eta (1 + s), \qquad t + 2 s.\)

For each frame coordinate \(e_a\), the one-column estimate gives a marked polynomial \(Z_a\) for \(j_a = \iota^{- 1} (e_a)\). Applying a selected motion \(g\) gives \(Z_{g, a}\) with density \(g \cdot (\textsf{P}_m f_{j_a}) = \textsf{P}_m f_{\iota^{- 1} (g \cdot e_a)}\), lead error at most \(\epsilon \hspace{0.17em} \mu_F (\text{core} \hspace{0.17em} e_a)\), residual activity at most \(cD_a\), and charged cost at most \(t + 2 s\), where

\(\displaystyle D_a = \mu_F \hspace{-0.17em} \left( \textsf{D}^{\text{gr}}_{d, \ell_m} (\text{core} \hspace{0.17em} e_a) \right) .\)

Let \(\mathcal{G}= \text{Motion} = \text{Signed} \times \text{Bool}\) be the finite motion set. Write \(g = (\pi, \epsilon)\), where \(\pi\) is a signed coordinate permutation and \(\epsilon \in \{0, 1\}\) is the independent conjugation flag. Let \(\rho_g\) be its (possibly semilinear) action on \(F\) and let \(\sigma_g\) be its scalar action, identity for \(\epsilon = 0\) and complex conjugation for \(\epsilon = 1\). Then the actual averaging interpolation weights are

\(\displaystyle \chi (v ; g, a) = \frac{1}{|\mathcal{G}|} \hspace{0.17em} \sigma_g \hspace{-0.17em} ((\rho_g^{- 1} \iota v)_a), \qquad (g, a) \in \mathcal{G} \times \{1, \ldots, q\},\)

where \(q = \dim_{\mathbb{C}} F\). Applying the inverse motion, taking its \(a\)th coordinate, and then applying the matching scalar action makes \(v \mapsto \chi (v ; g, a)\) complex-linear, including when \(\epsilon = 1\). Averaging the reindexed coordinate expansions gives

\(\displaystyle \iota (v) = \sum_{g, a} \chi (v ; g, a) \hspace{0.17em} \rho_g e_a, \qquad v = \sum_{g, a} \chi (v ; g, a) \hspace{0.17em} \iota^{- 1} (\rho_g e_a) .\)

For any selected \(h\), reindexing \(g = hq'\) gives

\(\displaystyle \chi (\rho_h v ; hg, a) = \sigma_h (\chi (v ; g, a)),\)

so the assembled response has the required semilinear motion covariance. Thus conjugation comes from the independent flag, not from whether the signed coordinate permutation reverses orientation. The action preserves the grade and commutes with the core, but it need not permute the coordinate axes. Define the finite frame orbit bound by

\(\displaystyle \text{orbitCost} (F) := 1 + \sum_{g \in \mathcal{G}} \sum_{a = 1}^q \mu_F (\rho_g e_a) .\)

For any \(x = \sum_a x_a e_a\), axis expansion and the triangle inequality give

\(\displaystyle \mu_F (\rho_g x) \leqslant \sum_a |x_a | \mu_F (\rho_g e_a) \leqslant \text{orbitCost} (F) \mu_F (x) .\)

Apply this bound to \(\rho_g^{- 1}\) and to \(x = \text{core} (\iota v)\) or \(x = \textsf{D}^{\text{gr}}_{d, \ell_m} (\text{core} (\iota v))\). The scalar actions preserve modulus, and both vacuum deletion and grade dilation commute with the motion. Averaging over \(g\) in the displayed interpolation weight formula gives the two estimates

\(\displaystyle \sum_{(g, a)} | \chi (v ; g, a) | \hspace{0.17em} \mu_F (\text{core} \hspace{0.17em} e_a) \leqslant \text{orbitCost} (F) \mu_F (\text{core} (\iota v)),\)
\(\displaystyle \sum_{(g, a)} | \chi (v ; g, a) | D_a \leqslant \text{orbitCost} (F) \mu_F \hspace{-0.17em} (\textsf{D}^{\text{gr}}_{d, \ell_m} (\text{core} (\iota v))) .\)

They are the finite tally inequalities obtained by summing the absolute interpolation weights against the axis and dilated-axis costs. Therefore the weighted sums of lead errors and residual activities are at most \(\epsilon \text{orbitCost} (F) \mu_F (\text{core} (\iota v))\) and \(c \text{orbitCost} (F) \mu_F (\textsf{D}^{\text{gr}}_{d, \ell_m} (\text{core} (\iota v)))\), respectively; the corresponding finite tally of gate-crossing bounds is the purchased insertion support allowance. These are the exact finite sums paid by the selected column and orbit-cost budgets. The estimates hold after each signed motion because the marked ticket is closed under those motions and the Wilson transition is motion covariant. Here is the finite assembly step. Let \(\iota : F \to F\) be the coefficient family's coefficient-index isomorphism, and let \(\mathcal{J}\) be the finite tally of pairs \((g, a)\) consisting of a selected signed motion \(g\) and a coordinate index \(a\). Put \(e_a\) for the coordinate vector. Let \(j_a = \iota^{- 1} (e_a)\) and \(v_{g, a} = g \cdot j_a = \iota^{- 1} (g \cdot e_a)\); the equality uses equivariance of the index map. The frame interpolation coefficients \(\chi (v ; g, a)\) are the coefficients of the finite frame expansion of \(\iota (v)\), so

\(\displaystyle \iota (v) = \sum_{(g, a) \in \mathcal{J}} \chi (v ; g, a) (g \cdot e_a), \qquad v = \sum_{(g, a) \in \mathcal{J}} \chi (v ; g, a) v_{g, a} .\)

For each coordinate \(a\), first take the one-column response at \(j_a\), then apply the selected signed motion \(g\) to it. The resulting moved response \(Z_{g, a}\) has fine density \(g \cdot f_{j_a}\) and, by covariance of the transition, coarse density \(g \cdot (\textsf{P}_m f_{j_a}) = \textsf{P}_m f_{v_{g, a}}\). Its scalar lead is one term in the reconstructed coordinate, and its forced profile is the corresponding moved profile. Assemble them by the finite sum

\(\displaystyle Z (v) = \sum_{(g, a) \in \mathcal{J}} \chi (v ; g, a) Z_{g, a} .\)

The assembled scalar lead and forced profile are the same finite weighted sums of their one-column counterparts. By the exact one-column density identity, linearity of the push, and the second frame identity,

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} \text{density} (Z (v)) & = \sum_{(g, a)} \chi (v ; g, a) g \cdot (\textsf{P}_m f_{j_a})\\ & = \textsf{P}_m \left( \sum_{(g, a)} \chi (v ; g, a) f_{v_{g, a}} \right) = \textsf{P}_m f_v . \end{array}\)

All coefficient and residual bounds are summed with \(| \chi (v ; g, a) |\). The frame interpolation inequalities are proved by the finite frame identities: the lead error is bounded by \(\epsilon \hspace{0.17em} \text{orbitCost} (F) \| \text{core} (\iota v)\|_1\), and the residual by \(c \hspace{0.17em} \text{orbitCost} (F) \| \textsf{D}^{\text{gr}}_{d, \ell_m} (\text{core} (\iota v))\|_1\). The first is the triangle inequality followed by the frame orbit-cost bound; the second follows by summing the coordinate bounds and the dilation norm bound. The paid ticket bounds the total gate-crossing costs by the corresponding finite-support budget. Thus no infinite series or exchange of an infinite sum with the transition integral occurs. The one-step direction estimates give the cone bounds; here is the numerical argument. Put \(L = b_m\), let \(J_f\) and \(J_c\) be the fine and coarse clocks with \(J_f \leqslant J_c + 1\), and set \(C = W.K (J_f)\), \(D_f = W.K (J_f + 1)\), \(D_c = W.K (J_c + 1)\). Let \(s_f\) be the fine column count, \(s_c\) the coarse column count, and \(e = \text{stepSmall} (L)\). Write \(u\) and \(v\) for the coarse and fine frame coordinates, \(r\) and \(t\) for their charges, and \(y = \textsf{D}^{\text{gr}}_{d, L} (v)\). The direction certificate provides a coefficient isomorphism \(B\) such that

\(\displaystyle \|Bx\|_1, \|B^{- 1} x\|_1 \leqslant C \|x\|_1, \qquad B (\text{core} \hspace{0.17em} x) = \text{core} (Bx),\)
\(\displaystyle \|u - By\|_1 \leqslant e (\| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} v\|_1 + t), \qquad r \leqslant C\| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} y\|_1 + et, \qquad t \leqslant s_f \| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} v\|_1 .\)

The selected bound growth gives \(1 \leqslant C \leqslant D_f\) and, by the slow-step condition, \(C \leqslant D_c\). The bank certificate says

\(\displaystyle 100000 D_f^6 (1 + s_f)^2 \leqslant L^{1 / 8}, \qquad e = L^{- 3 / 8} .\)

In particular \(L^{1 / 8} \geqslant 1\) and \(2 C (1 + s_f) \leqslant L^{1 / 8}\), so \(2 Ce (1 + s_f) \leqslant L^{- 1 / 4} \leqslant 1\) and \(e \geqslant 0\). The fine room certificate gives \(64 D_f^2 \leqslant s_f\), and the coarse room certificate gives \(64 D_c^2 \leqslant s_c\). Hence \(2 C \leqslant s_f\) and \(4 C^2 \leqslant s_c\). Since \(\| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} y\|_1 = \| \textsf{D}^{\text{gr}}_{d, L} (\text{core} \hspace{0.17em} v)\|_1 \geqslant \| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} v\|_1\), the incoming charge bound implies

\(\displaystyle 2 Ce (\| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} v\|_1 + t) \leqslant 2 Ce (1 + s_f) \| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} v\|_1 \leqslant \| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} y\|_1 .\)

Consequently \(2 C \|u - By\|_1 \leqslant \| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} y\|_1\). Put \(\delta = \|u - By\|_1\). Then \(C \delta \leqslant \|y\|_1 / 2\), and the inverse norm bound gives

\(\displaystyle \|y\|_1 = \|B^{- 1} (By)\|_1 \leqslant C \|By\|_1 \leqslant C\|u \|_1 + C \delta \leqslant C\| u\|_1 + \tfrac{1}{2} \|y\|_1,\)

so \(\|y\|_1 \leqslant 2 C \|u\|_1\). In the other direction,

\(\displaystyle \|u\|_1 \leqslant \|By\|_1 + \delta \leqslant C\|y\|_1 + \frac{1}{2 C} \|y\|_1 \leqslant 2 C \|y\|_1,\)

since \(C \geqslant 1\). Apply the same absorption argument to the core vectors; the core projection contracts the \(\ell^1\) norm and commutes with \(B\). It yields

\(\displaystyle \| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} y\|_1 \leqslant 2 C \| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} u\|_1 .\)

It follows, using \(\|v\|_1 \leqslant \|y\|_1 \leqslant L^d \|v\|_1\), that

\(\displaystyle \|v\|_1 \leqslant s_f \|u\|_1, \qquad \|u\|_1 \leqslant s_f L^d \|v\|_1 .\)

Also \(et \leqslant C \| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} y\|_1\), by the displayed bank estimate, so

\(\displaystyle r \leqslant 2 C \| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} y\|_1 \leqslant 4 C^2 \| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} u\|_1 \leqslant s_c \| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} u\|_1 .\)

These are exactly the charge, fine-to-coarse, and coarse-to-fine bounds used below. In particular, the two norm inequalities are full coefficient norms; the core norm occurs only in the charge estimate.

The response defines a linear map \(Q_m : F \to F\) by its coarse reference coordinate. The first cone inequality gives \(\| \iota (v)\|_1 \leqslant s_f \|Q_m v\|_1\). If \(Q_m v = 0\), then \(\iota (v) = 0\); since \(\iota\) is an isomorphism, \(v = 0\). Thus \(Q_m\) is injective and, since \(F\) is finite dimensional, bijective. The one-step output stores this isomorphism as the new coefficient family index. The density family is not precomposed by its inverse. Across several scales, the full-norm cone inequality is iterated from the reference to the terminal frame; the exact recurrence and terminal isomorphism give the single global transfer normalization used below.

Lemma 4.8. (Finite backward reconstruction) Let \(t \leqslant N\). Suppose a selected finite-volume history has (i) a terminal coefficient family which agrees with the calibrated top coefficient frame and satisfies the terminal reflection condition; (ii) a motion-covariant Wilson push between each adjacent pair of scales whose transition density has the boundedness, measurability and mass properties stated above; (iii) at every \(m \in [t, N)\) a selected interface, a sound local section, and a finite paid ticket whose one-step response satisfies the column estimates and cap conditions described above; and (iv) the room and bank bounds needed to pay the cumulative responses. Then there is a sequence of coefficient families \(V_m\), \(t \leqslant m \leqslant N\), such that the terminal coefficient family is the selected one, every adjacent density satisfies

\(\displaystyle \text{density} (V_m (v)) = \textsf{P}_m \text{density} (V_{m + 1} (v)),\)

and each scale has the selected reality, motion, and coefficient-cone bounds. The pushed coefficient maps define a linear transfer system, normalized by the inverse of its reference coefficient map.

Proof. Use downward induction. At \(m = N\), take the supplied terminal coefficient family. Suppose \(V_{m + 1}\) has been constructed and has the required integrability, reality, and motion properties. Apply the one-step response assembly above to its paid ticket. The density identity follows from the exact finite linear assembly. The local cap gives the response and residual bounds at scale \(m\); the paid room and bank inequalities keep the accumulated expenditure within its assigned budget. The terminal reflection condition propagates because the Wilson push is invariant under reflection and the ticket is real on the selected real subspace. The coefficient-cone estimate propagates by the one-step paid inequality. The one-step construction packages its own invertible index map with its density family as a coefficient family; we do not alter the family by precomposing with the inverse. This completes one induction step. There are only \(N - t\) steps, so finite downward induction constructs every \(V_m\) and all the claimed identities. Finally, if the reference readout of a coefficient \(v\) is zero, the full-norm cone inequality propagates zero from scale \(t\) to scale \(N\). The terminal coefficient family index is an isomorphism, so \(v = 0\). The reference map is injective and hence bijective on \(F\); its inverse gives the one global normalization in the conclusion.\(\Box\)

4.3.2The transfer map and its normalization

For the unnormalized music just constructed, write \(x_m (v)\) for the coefficient index of its coefficient family at scale \(m\). The full-norm cone estimates at each adjacent pair have the form

\(\displaystyle \|x_{m + 1} (v)\|_1 \leqslant s_{m + 1} \|x_m (v)\|_1, \qquad \|x_m (v)\|_1 \leqslant s_{m + 1} \ell_m^d \|x_{m + 1} (v)\|_1,\)

where \(s_{m + 1}\) is the paid column count and \(\ell_m\) the block side. Let \(Q_t (v) = x_t (v)\) be the reference readout. If \(Q_t (v) = 0\), the first inequality propagates zero successively through every scale up to \(N\). At the terminal scale \(x_N\) is an isomorphism, so \(v = 0\). Thus \(Q_t\) is injective, and finite dimensionality makes it bijective. This is the sole global normalization. The transfer data record the unnormalized coefficient entries and set their terminal coordinate map to the reference readout \(Q_t\). When viewed on external labels \(w\), every scale uses the same reparametrization \(V_m (Q_t^{- 1} w)\); no scale gets an independently fitted inverse. The selected public supplier applies this construction for every grade and every pair \((N, p)\) beyond the grade reference. Its first finitary step is the selected endpoint: the terminal frame is obtained from the calibrated section and the keeper chosen by the nested history. The endpoint is on top because the selected Wilson trajectory represents the terminal field and the paid terminal receipt gives the exact coefficient identities. Its reflection condition follows from the real section and the terminal keeper. The selected climb provides the interface and the finite ticket at each adjacent pair. The ticket's allowance is the scale's scale-dependent estimate, and the paid room and bank bounds control the total cost. Applying Lemma 4.8 and then the reference normalization gives the transfer data required by the centered-volume calculation.

The resulting ordinary mathematical object is a sequence of compatible coefficient families with a selected terminal frame, the adjacent finite-support estimates, and a normalized transfer map. In the center argument, the separate terminal birth polynomial has coefficients given by finite products of the named observables. It is continuous on compact configuration space and hence integrable. The coefficient family and its transition identities supply the individual insertion profiles used in the served representation. The full birth polynomial is propagated separately; its higher-mode coefficients belong to the census construction, not to a single coefficient family. The backward integral identity then relates each propagated mode to the corresponding terminal moment. All later bounds concern normalized finite-volume expectations.

4.3.3Scope of the producer statement

The preceding induction proves the finite reconstruction from its exact selected-history ticket and terminal inputs. In the public run, the schedule is chosen from the purchased trajectory so that the grade reference lies before every requested terminal level. The selected climb and terminal receipt are retained on this same trajectory; they are not assembled by selecting a new history for each period. The consumer then uses the resulting transfer system to form the terminal birth and the root estimate.

The quantitative production of the ticket's nonlinear coefficient estimates is a distinct finite-volume step and is not proved in this chapter. It consists of the field-interpolation estimates together with the mode-slot and shipment estimates of the funded selected-history record; this chapter only states the numerical inequalities that the one-step theorem consumes. Likewise, uniformity over every coefficient in a bounded grade ball is part of the selected-history estimates; the attachment and uniform served-census construction are now Proposition 4.16 and Corollary 4.17. Their common-purchase hypotheses remain explicit. What is proved here is the finite backward assembly.

4.4Producing the occupied, migrating, separated sites

The mode-moment receipt needs a site assignment at its opened scale: it must have bounded occupancy, agree with its descendants under every selected coarse-graining map, and keep distinct names in every nontrivial mode far apart. On the public root-test route, one finite copy of the opened lattice supplies all three properties. This section proves that geometric production from the selected tab-opening schedule.

Let \(m \leqslant k \leqslant N\) be levels in one selected history and fix a period \(p\). Put \(V = \Lambda_m\) for the finite site set at level \(m\). Write \(a_j\) for the physical lattice spacing at level \(j\), \(L_j = a_j / a_{j + 1} \in \mathbb{N}\) for the exact adjacent ratio, and \(B_j\) for the schedule's covering radius. Write \(n_j\) for the integer side of the periodic lattice \(\Lambda_j\), so an aligned embedding with ratio \(q_j = a_m / a_j\) has \(n_j = q_j n_m\). The selected tab schedule gives

\(\displaystyle L_j \geqslant 3, \qquad B_{j + 1}^2 \leqslant 2 B_j^2, \qquad B_k^2 \leqslant a_m / a_k\)

when the root level \(m\) is opened at \(k\) with parameter one. At every level \(j \geqslant m\), define

\(\displaystyle o_j (x) = \text{embed}_{m \to j} (x), \qquad x \in V,\)

using the canonical aligned lattice embedding, and put \(o_j (x) = 0\) below \(m\). Let \(B_j\) be the schedule's separation radius at level \(j\); for the selected Wilson history this is also the radius of the local configuration there.

Lemma 4.9. (Opened-copy geometry) Assume the selected family identifies the represented field radius with \(B_j\), and the schedule bounds just stated hold. Then the opened-copy assignment has the following properties for \(k \leqslant j \leqslant N\):

  1. \(o_j : V \to \Lambda_j\) is injective, and hence has occupancy at most one.

  2. For each \(k \leqslant j < N\), \(o_{j + 1} (x)\) is the canonical embedding of \(o_j (x)\) into level \(j + 1\); in particular its parent is \(o_j (x)\).

  3. For distinct \(x, y \in V\), \(\text{sep} (o_j (x), o_j (y)) \geqslant B_j^2\).

  4. At the terminal level, the root copy map sends each \(x \in V\) to \(o_N (x)\).

Consequently, if an occupancy schedule \(r_j\) obeys \(1 \leqslant r_j\), the assignment is migrating, has occupancy at most \(r_k\) at scale \(k\), and satisfies the separated-origin condition for every census mode whose distinct names are drawn from \(V\).

Proof. The opening condition gives \(B_k^2 \leqslant a_m / a_k\). We propagate this to every later \(j\). If \(B_j^2 \leqslant a_m / a_j\), then

\(\displaystyle B_{j + 1}^2 \leqslant 2 B_j^2 \leqslant 2 \frac{a_m}{a_j} \leqslant L_j \frac{a_m}{a_j} = \frac{a_m}{a_{j + 1}},\)

because \(L_j \geqslant 3\). Thus \(a_m / a_j \geqslant B_j^2\) for all \(j \geqslant k\). The level-\(m\) to level-\(j\) ratio \(q_j = a_m / a_j\) is an integer, by multiplication of the adjacent integer ratios.

For sites on the periodic lattice \(\Lambda_m\), define separation as the maximum over coordinates of the shortest periodic distance, namely

\(\displaystyle \text{sep} (x, y) = \max_{\alpha} \min_{r \in \mathbb{Z}} | \tilde{x}_{\alpha} - \tilde{y}_{\alpha} + rn_m | .\)

Here \(\tilde{x}, \tilde{y}\) are any integer lifts of the periodic sites; the minimum does not depend on the chosen lifts. Choose in each coordinate a centered integer representative of the difference. Under the aligned ratio embedding of factor \(q_j\), each such representative is multiplied by \(q_j\) and remains centered modulo the new periodic side \(n_j = q_j n_m\), including the half-side tie case. Hence

\(\displaystyle \text{sep} (o_j (x), o_j (y)) = q_j \text{sep} (x, y) .\)

Distinct \(x, y\) in the level-\(m\) lattice have integer separation at least one. Therefore

\(\displaystyle \text{sep} (o_j (x), o_j (y)) = q_j \text{sep} (x, y) \geqslant q_j \geqslant B_j^2 .\)

In particular they are distinct; this proves injectivity and occupancy at most one. The exact ratio-composition identity for canonical embeddings gives \(o_{j + 1} = \text{embed}_{j \to j + 1} \circ o_j\). Applying the parent map, which is a left inverse of that embedding, gives the stated parent identity. The forward embedding identity is the migration condition used in the service theorem. By the definition of the opened root cell, its terminal copy map is precisely the canonical embedding \(o_N\). Finally, \(1 \leqslant r_k\) upgrades occupancy one to the requested bound, while the separation estimate is exactly the required bound for every pair of distinct names in any nontrivial mode.\(\Box\)

For the selected root test, take its finite root set as \(V\). Lemma 4.9 gives the migration, occupancy, and separated-origin inputs used by the finite-volume moment receipt. It gives these inputs for the same selected period, stop level, and schedule; it does not choose new sites independently at each scale. The estimate does not supply the weights or the response budget, which remain separate selected-history inputs.

4.5From terminal insertions to a centered moment bound

This section records the finite-volume terminal receipt used on the public selected-history route. It starts from the calibrated family, its same-history multiscale data, and the birth polynomial made from a finite list of observables. The result is a mode-by-mode normalized moment estimate. It is a conditional receipt: it uses the selected-history service and migration data stated below, rather than deriving those upstream construction inputs.

Let \(\mathcal{V}\) be a finite marker set and let \(\textsf{B}\) be a downward-closed family of allowed subsets containing the empty set and all singletons. Its elements are the census modes. For each \(a \in \mathcal{V}\), choose a grade label \(s_a\), a terminal site \(o_N (a)\), and a motion \((\pi_a, \epsilon_a)\) consisting of a signed coordinate transformation and an independent conjugation flag. Let \(O_a\) be the corresponding local cylinder observable, and define its terminal translate by

\(\displaystyle T_a (U) = \sigma_{\epsilon_a} \hspace{-0.17em} \left( O_a \left( \text{pull}_{\pi_a, o_N (a)} \hspace{0.17em} U \right) \right),\)

where \(\sigma_0\) is the identity and \(\sigma_1\) is complex conjugation. The pullback includes both the signed transformation and the translation to \(o_N (a)\). Assume the selected family makes each \(T_a\) continuous on the compact terminal configuration space. Write \(R_N\) for the selected terminal representative and set

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{B}_N (U) = R_N (U) \hspace{0.17em} \text{Pass} \hspace{-0.17em} \left( \sum_{a \in \mathcal{V}} e_{\{a\}} T_a (U) \right),\)

where the marker algebra has one basis element for each allowed mode and square-free multiplication: \(e_S e_{S'} = e_{S \cup S'}\) if the sets are disjoint and their union is allowed, and \(0\) otherwise. The polynomial pass is the finite exponential in this nilpotent algebra. For every allowed mode \(I \in \textsf{B}\), its coefficient satisfies

\(\displaystyle (\mathcal{B}_N (U))_I = R_N (U) \prod_{a \in I} T_a (U) .\)

To verify the identity, expand the \(r\)th power in the finite exponential. A term contributing to an allowed mode \(I\) must use precisely the \(|I|\) distinct singleton markers in \(I\): repeated markers vanish, and no term of another degree can produce \(I\). Every ordering of those singletons contributes the same product. Downward closure ensures that each intermediate union in such an ordering is allowed, so the \(|I|\)! orderings cancel the factorial in the exponential coefficient. The empty mode comes from the zeroth power and has coefficient \(R_N\). The centered-copy application uses the full census \(\textsf{B} = 2^{\mathcal{V}}\); the general mode estimate below does not require that choice. For this selected terminal representative, \(R_N\) is the bare Wilson density. Write

\(\displaystyle Z_{N, p} := \int R_N (U) \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_N (U) > 0, \qquad \omega_{N, p} (F) := Z_{N, p}^{- 1} \int R_N (U) F (U) \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_N (U) .\)

Theorem 4.10. (Selected-history birth moment bound) Fix a selected family, volume \(N\), period \(p\), grade \(d\), and reference scale \(k\) with \(C. \text{ref} (d) \leqslant k < N\). Assume all labels \(s_a\) have grade \(d\). Suppose the family satisfies its calibration conditions and the common-trajectory finite-volume receipt supplies the terminal birth, its backward Wilson transitions, the scale-\(k\) service resources, and the positive vacuum treasury. Assume also the geometric inputs at \(k\): positive weights \(w_a\), site assignments \(o_k (a)\), an occupancy bound, the migration identity for the site assignments, and the separated-origin condition for every nontrivial mode. Suppose further that

\(\displaystyle w_a \hspace{0.17em} \mathfrak{m}_a \leqslant \mathfrak{f}_k \leqslant 1 \quad (a \in \mathcal{V}),\)

where \(\mathfrak{m}_a = \Gamma_k \hspace{0.17em} \text{paid}_a\) is the column-weighted coefficient size of label \(s_a\) at scale \(k\), \(\Gamma_k \geqslant 1\) is the selected column count, and \(\mathfrak{f}_k\) is the scale's small bound. The factor \(\Gamma_k\) is needed by the local coefficient and activity response; the unweighted payment alone is insufficient for that response estimate. Require the service receipt to give measurable mode densities \(H_I\), a common scalar \(\sigma\), and real costs \(A_k, D_k\) such that its representative is nonnegative, its support exponent is nonnegative, and its reserve is \(f = 1\), with

\(\displaystyle w_I |H_I (U) | \leqslant \exp (\sigma + A_k), \quad Z_{N, p} \geqslant \exp (\sigma - D_k), \quad A_k + D_k \leqslant | \Lambda_k | \mathcal{T}_k + |\mathcal{V}| (x + y + 2 f),\)

where \(w_I = \prod_{a \in I} w_a\), \(x = 3\mathfrak{f}_k\), and \(y =\mathfrak{f}_k\). The common-trajectory receipt identifies \(H_I\) with the scale-\(k\) coefficient of the backward birth. Then for every census mode \(I\),

\(\displaystyle \left| \omega_{N, p} \hspace{-0.17em} \left( \prod_{a \in I} T_a \right) \right| \prod_{a \in I} w_a \leqslant \exp \hspace{-0.17em} \left( | \Lambda_k | \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{T}_k + 6|\mathcal{V}| \right), \) (4.14)

where \(\omega_{N, p}\) is the normalized finite-volume Wilson expectation, \(\Lambda_k\) is the scale-\(k\) lattice, and \(\mathcal{T}_k\) is the finite-volume vacuum treasury cost. The conclusion is conditional on the listed service, geometry, and paid-coefficient hypotheses.

Proof. First, \(\mathcal{B}_N\) is integrable. The selected terminal representative is continuous, each \(T_a\) is continuous by assumption, and the finite marker polynomial is a finite sum of products of these functions. Hence every coefficient of \(\mathcal{B}_N\) is continuous. The terminal configuration space is compact, so each coefficient is integrable against the normalized product-Haar prior.

For \(k \leqslant i \leqslant N\), let \(\mathcal{B}_i\) be the coefficient polynomial obtained by the selected backward transition. It satisfies

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{B}_i = \text{Push}_i (\mathcal{B}_{i + 1}), \qquad \mathcal{B}_N \text{ as above},\)

where the fine-to-coarse kernel is normalized in its output variable:

\(\displaystyle \int k_i (U, V) \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_i (V) = 1 \qquad \text{for each fine configuration } U.\)

The density push is therefore

\(\displaystyle (\text{Push}_i \hspace{0.17em} F) (V) = \int k_i (U, V) F (U) \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_{i + 1} (U),\)

where \(U\) is fine and \(V\) is coarse. For an integrable coefficient \(F\), slice integrability and Fubini give the precise conservation identity

\(\displaystyle \int (\text{Push}_i \hspace{0.17em} F) (V) \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_i (V) = \int F (U) \left( \int k_i (U, V) \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_i (V) \right) d \mu_{i + 1} (U) = \int F (U) \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_{i + 1} (U) .\)

For this terminal birth, \(R_N\) is the bare Wilson density, so \(Z_{N, p} = \int R_N \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_N\). The selected transition density is jointly measurable, nonnegative and uniformly bounded on the product of the finite-volume configuration spaces. Multiplying an integrable coefficient by that density gives an integrable function on the product of the priors; the same bound also gives integrability on every fixed coarse slice. Fubini therefore applies at each step. Since coefficient extraction is a continuous linear map on the finite-dimensional marker algebra, it commutes with this finite integral. Inductively, for each mode \(I\),

\(\displaystyle \int (\mathcal{B}_k (U))_I \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_k (U) = \int (\mathcal{B}_N (U))_I \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_N (U) = Z_{N, p} \hspace{0.17em} \omega_{N, p} \hspace{-0.17em} \left( \prod_{a \in I} T_a \right), \) (4.15)

where \(Z_{N, p} > 0\) is the finite-volume Wilson partition function. The second equality uses the terminal birth identity and the definition of normalized expectation. This establishes the exact finite-volume transfer of the moment; no continuum limit or interchange of an infinite sum is involved.

At scale \(k\), the common-trajectory receipt yields a served marked representation of \(\mathcal{B}_k\). Its finite response estimate gives at marker \(a\) a coefficient bound by three times the column-weighted coefficient size \(\mathfrak{m}_a\) and a total activity bound by \(\mathfrak{m}_a\). Multiplying by \(w_a\) and using \(w_a \mathfrak{m}_a \leqslant \mathfrak{f}_k\) gives the coefficient and activity bounds \(x = 3\mathfrak{f}_k\) and \(y =\mathfrak{f}_k\). The factor three is the local coefficient bound from the response estimate; the activity bound is its unmultiplied support charge. The separated-origin condition and occupancy estimate are what make this finite response estimate valid for a whole mode, and migration transports those facts through the backward scales. Since \(\mathfrak{f}_k \leqslant 1\), these satisfy \(x + y + 2 \leqslant 6\).

Here are the precise conditional bound assumptions used at the scale-\(k\) service step. The marked representation is lawful, its representative is pointwise nonnegative, its support exponent is nonnegative, and its served reserve is \(f = 1\). For each mode \(I\), the local density estimate is

\(\displaystyle w_I |H_I (U) | \leqslant \exp (\sigma + A_k), \qquad w_I = \prod_{a \in I} w_a,\)

with \(x = 3\mathfrak{f}_k\) and \(y =\mathfrak{f}_k\). The vacuum denominator bound is

\(\displaystyle Z_{N, p} \geqslant \exp (\sigma - D_k),\)

and the receipts give

\(\displaystyle A_k + D_k \leqslant | \Lambda_k | \mathcal{T}_k + |\mathcal{V}| (x + y + 2 f), \qquad f = 1.\)

The reserve contributes the term \(2 f\); the coefficient \(+ 2\) is not paid merely by the marker fee. Integrating the pointwise inequality yields

\(\displaystyle w_I \left| \int H_I \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_k \right| \leqslant \exp (\sigma + A_k) .\)

Divide by the positive denominator lower bound. The scalar \(\sigma\) cancels:

\(\displaystyle w_I \frac{| \int H_I \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_k |}{Z_{N, p}} \leqslant \exp (A_k + D_k) \leqslant \exp (| \Lambda_k |\mathcal{T}_k + 6|\mathcal{V}|),\)

using \(x + y + 2 f = 4\mathfrak{f}_k + 2 \leqslant 6\). Equation (4.15) identifies this normalized coefficient integral with the desired Wilson moment and proves the result.\(\Box\)

The terminal product, continuity, and exact backward integration have been proved above. For a general history the service certificate, its local coefficient envelope, the origin-counting response and the finite-volume vacuum lower bound are explicit inputs. For the retained public family, Proposition 4.16 and Corollary 4.17 attach them to this receipt under the common-purchase hypotheses.

4.5.1The local denominator on the retained history

Lemma 4.11. (Quiet coefficient and partition function) Fix the selected history at \((N, p)\). For each \(i \leqslant N\) let \(A_i\) be its unmarked representative, and put \(A_i^{\text{ev}} (U) = \text{eval} (A_i) (U)\). Assume the selected Wilson identities \(A_N^{\text{ev}} = R_N\) and \(A_i^{\text{ev}} = \text{Push}_i \hspace{0.17em} A_{i + 1}^{\text{ev}}\) for \(i < N\), where the push is the normalized transition in Subsection 4.5. Its kernel is that of step \(i\) at the coupling \(\beta_{N, p, i + 1}\), the same coupling used by the transfer and by the birth chain. Then

\(\displaystyle \int A_i^{\text{ev}} \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_i = Z_{N, p} \quad \text{for all } i \leqslant N, \qquad (\mathcal{B}_i)_{\varnothing} = A_i^{\text{ev}} \quad \text{for } k \leqslant i \leqslant N,\)

the range on which the chain \(\mathcal{B}_i\) is defined.

Proof. At \(N\), the constant coefficient of the finite exponential is one, so \((\mathcal{B}_N)_{\varnothing} = R_N = A_N^{\text{ev}}\). If the assertion holds at \(i + 1\), extracting the quiet coefficient commutes with the normalized push by the same finite-dimensional integration argument used in (4.15). Thus

\(\displaystyle (\mathcal{B}_i)_{\varnothing} = \text{Push}_i (\mathcal{B}_{i + 1})_{\varnothing} = \text{Push}_i \hspace{0.17em} A_{i + 1}^{\text{ev}} = A_i^{\text{ev}} .\)

For the integral identity, each \(A_{i + 1}^{\text{ev}}\) is integrable. At \(i + 1 = N\) it is continuous on a compact space. Below \(N\) it is the push of an integrable function by the bounded normalized kernel, by the argument used for (4.15). Integrating and using the normalized transition mass at every step gives the claimed equality with the positive terminal partition function.\(\Box\)

Common width and the vacuum cutoff.
Fix an admitted grade \(d\) and radius \(R > 0\). Let \(g_{d, R} \geqslant 1\) bound the coefficient norm on the closed grade ball, which is compact in the finite-dimensional grade space. Using the calibrated reference readout and the adjacent coefficient cone of Subsection 4.3, the coefficient at level \(k\) satisfies \(q_k (v) \leqslant g_{d, R} D_k\) throughout the ball, with \(D_k = \prod_{i < k} \Gamma_{i + 1}\). Indeed the reference coefficient is \(v\) and each forward bound multiplies by at most the next column count. Extending the product back to zero only increases it. Here \(\Gamma_i \geqslant 1\) are the columns of the fixed schedule. Writing \(a_k^{\text{mesh}}\) for the mesh spacing, take the single positive weight
\(\displaystyle \omega_k = \frac{(a_k^{\text{mesh}})^d \zeta_{d, k}}{\Gamma_k g_{d, R} D_k} .\)

Multiplication by the local column-weighted bound \(\Gamma_k (a_k^{\text{mesh}})^{- d} q_k (v)\) gives at most \(\zeta_{d, k}\) for every label in the ball. The weight is independent of the terminal volume, period, site and particular label. Moreover \(\zeta_{d, k} \leqslant 1\). This follows from the coin condition \(\zeta_{d, k} \Gamma_k \leqslant 1\) of the purchased schedule, item (R5) of Subsection 13.9, together with \(\Gamma_k \geqslant 1\). So the small bound \(\mathfrak{f}_k = \zeta_{d, k}\) satisfies the hypothesis \(\mathfrak{f}_k \leqslant 1\) of Theorem 4.10.

On a served census at \(k < N\), use the local bounds \(x = 3 \zeta_{d, k}\), \(y = \zeta_{d, k}\) and reserve one in (4.9). In the small-ball bound of Subsection 4.2 choose the radius \(s_k = \hat{\beta}_k^{- 1 / 2}\). The paid scale has \(\hat{\beta}_k > 1\) and the selected threshold is \(s_k\) times an exponential of a square, so \(0 < s_k \leqslant 1\) and \(s_k\) is at most the threshold. Write \(E\) for the local excess cost, \(\kappa\) for the unmarked activity allowance, and \(\beta\) for the local coupling. The numerator pays per site \(E + \kappa\) and the denominator pays \(E + \beta \hspace{0.17em} \text{baseBudget} (s_k) + 4 d_G \log (16 / s_k)\), with the same scalar in both. To compare their sum with the selected treasury, use the selected representation bounds: the local excess cost is at most the regular allowance plus the loop bound at its coupling, the activity allowance is bounded by the scheduled forced allowance, and \(0 \leqslant \beta \leqslant 2 \hat{\beta}_k\). The loop bound is an affine function of the coupling with nonnegative slope, and the base budget is nonnegative. Replacing the coupling in both costs by \(2 \hat{\beta}_k\) therefore gives at most the scheduled treasury at radius \(s_k\). The denominator in this calculation is \(\int \text{eval} (A) \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_k\), where \(A\) is the representative of the served representation at scale \(k\). The served census input is taken over the history's own representative, that is \(A = A_k\); this is part of the conditional served-census input below. Hence, by Lemma 4.11, the denominator is \(\int A_k^{\text{ev}} \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_k = Z_{N, p}\). Thus, whenever the shared retention and transfer give the served census and its local response on the actual geometry, the bound (4.14) holds with this same width throughout the grade ball. This argument uses the conditional served response only at the opened scale and makes no new choice of history.

4.6Large-coupling conditions for one RG step

This section proves the finite-volume scale estimates used by the selected multiscale service. It isolates the elementary large-parameter estimates and then explains how their separate eventual thresholds are made into one monotone delay sequence. All scale, profile, and gauge data below are the actual fixed data of the selected run.

Write

\(\displaystyle B (x) = \lceil (\log x)^2 \rceil, \qquad h (x) = e^{- \frac{1}{2} \log x}, \qquad f_d (x) = x^{- (\deg F_d + 4)}, \qquad s (x) = \tfrac{1}{4} h (x),\)

and, for \(x > 1\) and an integer radius \(B\),

\(\displaystyle \text{forcedReserve} (x) = \exp \hspace{-0.17em} \left[ - \exp \hspace{-0.17em} \left( \tfrac{1}{5} (\log \log x)^2 \right) \right], \qquad \text{siteFactor} (B) = e \{2 (16 B + 9)\}^4 .\)

Here \(F_d\) is the finite frame at grade \(d\). Fix a nonnegative integer occupancy schedule \(Q\) and the positive gauge slope constant \(c_G = s_G\) of (12.86). For a fixed stage \(j\), write \(L_j\) for its block side, \(b_j = c_G \log L_j\), \(y_j (x) = x + b_j\), and \(p_j = Q (j)\) for its occupancy allowance. At stage \(j\) fix the column count \(C_j\) and support exponent \(E_j\). For each grade fix its finite local-section constant \(A_d\). These quantities remain fixed while \(x \to \infty\). The stage index is not a finite-volume period index.

Lemma 4.12. (Uniform eventual scale estimates for one transition) Fix stages \(i, j\) satisfying \(\text{Step} (j, i)\), meaning \(i \leqslant j \leqslant i + 1\), and a finite grade range \(0 \leqslant d \leqslant i\). Let \(C_i, C_j \geqslant 1\) be the stage column counts and \(E_i, E_j \geqslant 0\) the support exponents. For each grade let \(A_d \geqslant 1\) be its uniform local-section constant, \(a_d \geqslant 1\) its motion orbit cost, and \(m_d = \deg F_d\) its frame degree. Let \(L_j \geqslant 1\) be the integer block side and assume the finite title cost \(T_j \geqslant 0\). Put

\(\displaystyle r_j = 2 e^{E_j} \hspace{0.17em} T_j .\)

Then there is \(x_0 > 1\) so that every \(x \geqslant x_0\) satisfies

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} & 8 \leqslant B (x), \qquad y_j (x)^3 \leqslant e^{B (x) / 128}, \hspace{3cm} \text{(4.16)} \\ & (2 B (x) + 1)^4 \left( \text{forcedReserve} (x) + p_j \hspace{0.17em} \text{siteFactor} (B (x)) s (x) \right) \leqslant 3,\\ & e^{\text{forcedReserve} (x) + p_j \text{siteFactor} (B (x)) s (x)} - 1 \leqslant 1, \hspace{3cm} \text{(4.17)} \end{array}\)

and, for every \(d \leqslant i\),

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} & f_d (x) C_i \leqslant 1, \hspace{3cm} \text{(4.18)} \\ & 2 a_d (r_j + 2 C_j) f_d (y_j (x)) \leqslant 1, \hspace{3cm} \text{(4.19)} \\ & e^{E_i + 2} A_d x^{m_d} f_d (x) \leqslant s (x), \hspace{3cm} \text{(4.20)} \\ & y_j (x)^{- 1} \leqslant 1, \qquad y_j (x)^{- 1} L_j^d C_j f_d (x) \leqslant f_d (y_j (x)), \hspace{3cm} \text{(4.21)} \end{array}\)

as well as \(\nu_d \leqslant B (x)\), where \(\nu_d\) is the minimum stencil side at grade \(d\).

Proof. We check each bound from its large-\(x\) mechanism. Since \(B (x) \to \infty\), eventually \(B (x) \geqslant 8\) and \(B (x) \geqslant \nu_d\) for all the finitely many grades. The first polynomial estimate follows from the same elementary comparison: for any fixed \(b\) and integer \(q\), eventually \(x + b \leqslant 2 x\), \(C 2^q \leqslant x\), and \((q + 1) \log x \leqslant (\log x)^2 / 128\). Thus

\(\displaystyle C (x + b)^q \leqslant C 2^q x^q \leqslant x^{q + 1} = e^{(q + 1) \log x} \leqslant e^{B (x) / 128} .\)

Apply this with \(C = 1, q = 3, b = b_j\) to get (4.16).

For (4.17), set \(H = h (x)\). We verify the reserves directly. The hat is \(H = x^{- 1 / 2}\), so \(H \leqslant 1 / 4\) eventually. Define

\(\displaystyle \text{smallK} (x) := \exp \hspace{-0.17em} \left( - \tfrac{1}{2} \exp \hspace{-0.17em} \left( \tfrac{1}{5} (\log \log x)^2 \right) \right) .\)

Writing \(y = \log \log x\), for \(y \geqslant 10\) we have \(y^2 / 5 - y \geqslant 10 > \log 6\), hence \(\exp (y^2 / 5) \geqslant 6 e^y = 6 \log x\). Comparing exponents yields \(\text{smallK} (x) \leqslant H^6\).

For the contact bound, put \(t = \frac{1}{2} \log x\), so \(H = e^{- t}\). For \(t \geqslant 1\), \(B (x) = \lceil 4 t^2 \rceil \leqslant 5 t^2\), and therefore

\(\displaystyle 2 B (x) + 1 \leqslant 11 t^2, \qquad \text{siteFactor} (B (x)) = e (2 (16 B (x) + 9))^4 \leqslant e (178 t^2)^4 .\)

Thus the contact expression is at most \(C_p t^{16} e^{- t}\) for a constant \(C_p\) depending only on the fixed occupancy allowance \(p_j\). For any fixed integer \(q \geqslant 0\), the exponential series gives \(e^t \geqslant t^{q + 1} / (q + 1)\)!, and so \(Ct^q e^{- t} \leqslant C (q + 1) ! / t \to 0\). Taking \(q = 16\) proves the contact reserve. Consequently, for large \(x\),

\(\displaystyle 0 < H \leqslant \tfrac{1}{4}, \qquad \text{smallK} (x) \leqslant H^6, \qquad (1 + p_j \text{siteFactor} (B (x))) (2 B (x) + 1)^4 H \leqslant 1.\)

The forced remainder allowance is \(\text{smallK} (x)^2\). Squaring the small-\(K\) bound gives \(\text{forcedReserve} (x) \leqslant H^{12} \leqslant H\) because \(0 < H \leqslant 1\). Also \(s (x) = H / 4 \leqslant H\). Therefore

\(\displaystyle \text{forcedReserve} (x) + p_j \text{siteFactor} (B (x)) s (x) \leqslant (1 + p_j \text{siteFactor} (B (x))) H,\)

which proves the product bound by the third reserve estimate. Since \((2 B + 1)^4 \geqslant 2\) once \(B \geqslant 8\), the exponent in that display is at most \(1 / 2\). It follows from \(1 / 2 < \log 2\) that its exponential minus one is at most \(e^{1 / 2} - 1 < 1\).

For the remaining five estimates, fix \(d \leqslant i\) and put \(m = \deg F_d + 4\), so \(m \geqslant 1\), and \(D = L_j^d C_j\). All constants are fixed, while \(x \to \infty\) and \(y = x + b_j \to \infty\). Eventually

\(\displaystyle x \geqslant \max \{1, C_i, 4 e^{E_i + 2} A_d \}, \quad y \geqslant \max \{1, 2 a_d (r_j + 2 C_j), D 2^m \}, \quad y \leqslant 2 x.\)

Now \(f_d (x) = x^{- m}\), so \(C_i x^{- m} \leqslant 1\) and

\(\displaystyle 2 a_d (r_j + 2 C_j) y^{- m} \leqslant 1.\)

For the local-section term, put \(m = m_d + 4 = \deg F_d + 4\); then

\(\displaystyle e^{E_i + 2} A_d x^{\deg F_d} f_d (x) = \frac{e^{E_i + 2} A_d}{x^4} \leqslant \frac{1}{4 \sqrt{x}} = s (x),\)

using \(4 e^{E_i + 2} A_d \leqslant x \leqslant x^{7 / 2}\) for \(x \geqslant 1\) (the weaker power inequality suffices). Also \(y^{- 1} \leqslant 1\).

Finally, \(D 2^m \leqslant y\) and \(y \leqslant 2 x\) imply

\(\displaystyle Dy^m \leqslant yx^m .\)

Indeed \(y^m \leqslant (2 x)^m\) and \(D 2^m \leqslant y\). Dividing by the positive quantity \(y^{m + 1} x^m\) yields

\(\displaystyle y^{- 1} Dx^{- m} \leqslant y^{- m},\)

which is the last inequality. There are finitely many \(d \leqslant i\), so the finitely many thresholds just chosen have a common maximum. This proves the lemma.\(\Box\)

Theorem 4.13. (One monotone delay for all selected transitions) Fix the selected schedule, profile, gauge, stage block sides, and an initial delay sequence \(z_j\). There is a coordinatewise larger delay sequence \(v_j\), nondecreasing in \(j\), such that for every later delay sequence \(u_j \geqslant v_j\), every \(j, i, x\) with \(\text{Step} (j, i)\) and \(u_j \leqslant x\) satisfies (4.16)–(4.21) for the actual profile, as well as the frame-fit inequality \(\nu_d \leqslant B (x)\) for every \(d \leqslant i\).

Proof. For a fixed pair \((j, i)\) with \(\text{Step} (j, i)\), apply Lemma 4.12 to its finite range \(d \leqslant i\). It provides a threshold \(t_{j, i}\) above which all its conclusions hold. A fixed \(j\) has only two possible stages, \(i = j\) and (when \(j > 0\)) \(i = j - 1\), because \(\text{Step} (j, i)\) means \(i \leqslant j \leqslant i + 1\). Set \(t_j\) to the maximum of the available \(t_{j, i}\), and enlarge it to include \(z_j\). Define

\(\displaystyle v_j = \max_{0 \leqslant \ell \leqslant j} t_{\ell} .\)

Then \(v_j\) is nondecreasing and \(z_j \leqslant v_j\). Whenever \(v_j \leqslant x\), we have \(t_j \leqslant x\), so the required estimates hold for either possible stage. If \(u \geqslant v\) coordinatewise and \(u_j \leqslant x\), then \(v_j \leqslant x\), giving the same conclusions. This is the finite-prefix maximum construction used to collect the eventual estimates; no uniformity over unbounded profile ranges is presumed at a fixed scale.\(\Box\)

Use in the service construction.
The theorem gives the exact quantitative service inequalities used by the selected finite producer: the cubic exponential bound, damping and support reserve, five interpolation bounds, and finite stencil fit. In Theorem 4.15 these are combined, as its hypothesis (ii), with the selected history, opening, menu, and finite-transfer inputs. This section proves the large-entrance estimates and their common monotone threshold; it does not prove the separate selected nonlinear menu response or the paid purchase that supplies the fixed profile data.

Proposition 4.14. (From scale estimates to service inequalities) Let \(x_m\) be the selected hatted scale, \(L_m\) its block side, and \(B_m = B (x_m)\). Let \(E_m\) be its support exponent and \(T_m\) its finite title cost. Suppose the selected schedule and delay \(u\) satisfy

\(\displaystyle x_{m + 1} = x_m + b_{J (m + 1)}, \qquad x_m \geqslant 1, \qquad B_{m + 1}^2 \leqslant 2 B_m^2, \qquad L_m \geqslant 2, \qquad u_{J (m + 1)} \leqslant x_m,\)

with \(u_j \geqslant v_j\) as in Theorem 4.13. Assume \(\text{threshold}_m > 0\) and \(\text{threshold}_m^{- 1} \leqslant x_m\). Let \(d \leqslant J (k)\), \(k \leqslant m\), and set

\(\displaystyle \zeta_m = f_d (x_m), \quad \phi_m = x_{m + 1}^{- 1}, \quad \delta_m = x_{m + 1}^{- 2}, \quad \psi_m = 2 e^{E_{m + 1}} T_{m + 1}, \quad \eta_m = s (x_m) .\)

Write \(\Gamma_m\) for the level-\(m\) column count, \(a_d\) for the finite motion orbit cost, and let \(A_d \geqslant 1\) be the same grade-specific local-section constant used in the scale estimates. Then for every \(m \geqslant k\) the scale estimates imply

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{c} \Gamma_m \zeta_m \leqslant 1, \qquad 2 a_d (\psi_m + 2 \Gamma_{m + 1}) \zeta_{m + 1} \leqslant 1, \qquad \psi_m \geqslant 0,\\ 0 < \phi_m \leqslant 1, \quad \delta_m > 0, \quad \phi_m L_m^d \Gamma_{m + 1} \zeta_m \leqslant \zeta_{m + 1},\\ e^{E_m + 2} A_d \hspace{0.17em} \text{threshold}_m^{- \deg F_d} \hspace{0.17em} \zeta_m \leqslant \eta_m,\\ 1 \leqslant \delta_m \phi_m e^{B_m / 128}, \quad B_m \geqslant 8, \quad B_{m + 1}^2 \leqslant L_m B_m^2 . \end{array}\)

If the local-section bounds are taken from the uniform estimate \(\| \text{places}_d (i, 0, U)\| \leqslant A_d\), its place bound is uniform in \(m\); its footprint cover is at most one once \(\nu_d \leqslant B_m\).

Proof. Apply Theorem 4.13 at \(j = J (m + 1)\), \(i = J (m)\), and \(x = x_m\). The selected clock identities give \(\text{Step} (j, i)\) and identify the next scale with \(y_j (x_m) = x_{m + 1}\). The delay condition places \(x_m\) beyond the collected threshold. Since \(d \leqslant J (k) \leqslant J (m)\), its scale inequalities apply to grade \(d\).

Write \(F_d\) for the grade frame and \(m_d = \deg F_d\). The first scale inequality is

\(\displaystyle x_m^{- (m_d + 4)} \hspace{0.17em} \text{columns}_m \leqslant 1,\)

the first coefficient-budget inequality. Its second is

\(\displaystyle 2 \hspace{0.17em} \text{orbitCost} (F_d) (\psi_m + 2 \hspace{0.17em} \text{columns}_{m + 1}) x_{m + 1}^{- (m_d + 4)} \leqslant 1,\)

the second coefficient-budget inequality. The title cost is nonnegative, so \(\psi_m \geqslant 0\).

The shifted reciprocal estimate \(x_{m + 1}^{- 1} \leqslant 1\) gives \(\phi_m \leqslant 1\). The final scale estimate reads

\(\displaystyle x_{m + 1}^{- 1} L_m^d \text{columns}_{m + 1} \hspace{0.17em} x_m^{- (m_d + 4)} \leqslant x_{m + 1}^{- (m_d + 4)} ;\)

this is the adjacent bound inequality with \(\phi_m = x_{m + 1}^{- 1}\) and the adjacent marker bounds \(\zeta_m, \zeta_{m + 1}\).

The local-section bound satisfies \(A_d \geqslant 1\). Its weighted norm cost is

\(\displaystyle e^{E_m + 2} \hspace{0.17em} A_d \hspace{0.17em} \text{threshold}_m^{- m_d} \hspace{0.17em} x_m^{- (m_d + 4)} .\)

The threshold comparison and \(\text{threshold}_m^{- 1} \leqslant x_m\) bound this by

\(\displaystyle e^{E_m + 2} \hspace{0.17em} A_d \hspace{0.17em} x_m^{m_d} x_m^{- (m_d + 4)} \leqslant s (x_m) = \eta_m,\)

using the third scale inequality. This verifies the weighted norm-cost bound. The separate local-section estimate gives the stated uniform place bound; the footprint estimate follows from the fitted stencil radius and the one-site cover lemma.

Positivity of \(\phi_m\) and \(\delta_m\) is immediate because \(x_{m + 1} > 0\). The exponential scale inequality follows from the second scale estimate:

\(\displaystyle \delta_m \phi_m e^{B_m / 128} = x_{m + 1}^{- 3} e^{B_m / 128} \geqslant 1.\)

The contact inequality supplies support \(B_m \geqslant 8\). Finally,

\(\displaystyle B_{m + 1}^2 \leqslant 2 B_m^2 \leqslant L_m B_m^2,\)

so the scale split holds. These inequalities supply the scale conditions used by the service certificate.\(\Box\)

4.7The centered moment estimate on the selected trajectory

The finite-volume moment estimate uses one service certificate at each period, reference scale, and terminal volume. The public route builds these certificates from a single funded run and its same-history multiscale record. This section follows that finite producer and spells out how its fields are used; it does not assume that a new history can be selected for each volume.

Theorem 4.15. (Same-history service certificates) Fix the selected run for a simple gauge group and a family of equivariant marked liftings on its trail, with its one family and gauge interfaces. For each grade \(d\), let \(k_d\) denote its selected reference scale. Assume the funded record on this trail supplies (i) a single transition history with retention clauses at every level and a terminal endowment; (ii) the scale room and bank budgets, the initial positivity, exponential scale, radius-splitting, and support-size inequalities of Proposition 4.14, and a nondecreasing occupancy schedule \(\rho_i\); (iii) for each grade \(d\), period \(p\), and indices \(k_d \leqslant k \leqslant N\), a height bound \(X = X (d, k, N, p) \geqslant 1\), the coefficient and activity-bound inequalities and adjacent weight-growth inequalities at levels at least \(k\), and the terminal bank, footprint-cover, and local-section estimates stated below; (iv) for each grade \(d\), period \(p\) and terminal volume \(N \geqslant k_d\), a calibrated finite coefficient sequence whose coefficient families are linear (the coordinate, regular and forced parts of each coefficient family depend complex-linearly on the coefficient, and the support assigned to each address does not depend on the coefficient; this is the assigned support address, not the set where its regular or forced part is nonzero), adjacent interface estimates, and its terminal forced-profile identity, realized by a response on the field footprint whose reserved mass is at most \(e^{E_N + 2} \text{fullCost}_d (P_N, X) \| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} v\|_1\) for terminal coefficient \(v\); and (v) gradewise thresholds, positive scale bounds with adjacent growth, and finite local tone tickets on those same interfaces. Then for every period \(p\), reference level \(k \geqslant 1\), and terminal volume \(N\), the certificate has retention and terminal endowment on the restricted history; room, bank, all four stated scale inequalities, and monotone occupancy at levels \(i \geqslant k\); and, for every grade with \(k_d \leqslant k \leqslant N\), the calibrated finite transfer and the height, coefficient, activity, adjacent-weight, terminal-bank, footprint-cover (at most one), and uniform local-section fields specified in the hypotheses. More explicitly, for each such grade and every \(i \geqslant k\), writing \(\Gamma_i\) for the columns, \(\ell_i\) for the block side, \(\zeta_i = \zeta_d (i)\) and \(a_d\) for the orbit cost, the stock witness satisfies

\(\displaystyle \zeta_i \Gamma_i \leqslant 1, \quad \psi_i \geqslant 0, \quad 2 a_d (\psi_i + 2 \Gamma_{i + 1}) \zeta_{i + 1} \leqslant 1, \quad \phi_i \leqslant 1, \quad \phi_i \ell_i^d \Gamma_{i + 1} \zeta_i \leqslant \zeta_{i + 1} .\)

At \(N\), let \(P_N\) be the terminal budget data, \(E_N\) its support exponent, \(\theta_N\) its positive threshold, and put \(\text{fullCost}_d (P_N, X) = X \theta_N^{- \deg F_d}\). The witness also satisfies

\(\displaystyle X \geqslant 1, \quad e^{E_N + 2} \text{fullCost}_d (P_N, X) \zeta_N \leqslant u_N, \quad C_{B_N} (\text{footprint}_d) \leqslant 1, \quad \| \text{places}_d (\text{axis}_j, 0, U)\| \leqslant X\)

for every frame index \(j\) and terminal configuration \(U\). The calibrated transfer uses the same interfaces and terminal profile identity as in the hypotheses. The lower restriction \(k \geqslant 1\) is retained from the public service constructor. All fields refer to the same selected history. The height witness can depend on all four displayed indices; its local-section bound is uniform over the finite frame indices and configurations at that volume.

Proof. The selected run fixes one trail \(T\), one family \(C\), transfer stories, and gauge interfaces at every adjacent scale; the purchase record supplies the multiscale ledgers. The public route applies the global Geyser service producer to exactly this tuple; its per-period Pond is a derived, weaker interface. Its output contains a retained history, the tab schedule, a terminal keeper, marked portfolios, purchases, and weak specifications. The history's cost field contains the numerical room and bank estimates, the scale inequalities, monotone occupancy, gradewise height data, and terminal music records. All are attached to the same selected run.

Fix \(p, k, N\). The service certificate is assembled from those records as follows.

First, restrict the retained history to levels \(i \geqslant k\). At each adjacent scale in this range, the selected spring and purchased tariff give a local comparison. The endpoint support exponents are at least \(100\), so the finite interpolation constructor returns both its local gate ticket and the duty estimate. These are exactly the clauses needed for the retained-history field; the terminal endowment is the terminal clause of the same history.

Next, use the room and bank estimates from the same clock. Restrict those initial positivity, exponential scale, radius-splitting, and support-size inequalities to indices \(i \geqslant k\); their bounds are unchanged. The occupancy schedule remains monotone under this restriction.

Finally, when \(k \leqslant N\), for every grade \(d\) with reference level at most \(k\), the height record at \((d, k, N, p)\) supplies a constant \(X\) and the data needed downstream: the coefficient and activity-estimates and adjacent weight-growth inequalities at every level at least \(k\), the terminal bank bound, the terminal footprint-cover estimate, and the uniform bound on the local-section insertions. The abstract certificate allows \(X\) to depend on all four indices. In the selected construction, the uniform insertion estimate gives a stronger witness \(X_d\) depending only on the grade: it bounds every frame-axis insertion at every finite volume, period and configuration, while the fitted stencil gives the required footprint cover. The terminal music record supplies the other part: its finite music begins with the calibrated factory entries, has this linearity at the grade reference, pays each adjacent transition through the same interfaces, and satisfies the terminal forced-profile identity. Joining these witnesses gives, for each such grade, the complete terminal service record. When \(k > N\), the stock clause has no obligation. The same construction applies at every allowed \((p, k, N)\) and introduces no new history; the other service fields retain their stated index dependence.

The common-trajectory per-label construction applies this service record to a finite list of observables. The affordability induction in Subsection 4.2 propagates each label's initial payment through the adjacent coefficient and scale-estimates. It uses the prescribed transported weights and one music on the selected history; it does not choose a new history at each scale.

For the moment estimate, take a finite set \(V\) of labels of one fixed grade \(d\), with positive marker weights \(w_i\). The per-label construction supplies compatible marked activities; finite backward propagation of their terminal product then gives a served marked representation at the reference scale. Let \(X (U)\) be its represented marked polynomial, \(A\) the local Wilson representative, \(\sigma = \text{scalar} (A)\) its common scalar, and \(\mathcal{T}_k\) the vacuum cost per site. Write \(\omega_w (I) = \prod_{i \in I} w_i\) and \(\mathcal{N}_w (Y) = \sum_I |Y_I | \omega_w (I)\) for its mode toll and polynomial bound. Suppose the terminal data satisfy the hypotheses of the selected root-test estimate: \(k < N\), the local section is sound, the representation is selected, weights are positive, the column-weighted bounds obey \(w_i \Gamma_k \hspace{0.17em} \text{paid}_i \leqslant \zeta_d (k)\), and the label sites have the stated migration, occupancy, and separation bounds. The strict inequality \(k < N\) identifies the propagated support exponent at level \(k\) with the current support exponent; the service certificate itself is also defined when \(k = N\). Here \(\Gamma_k \geqslant 1\) is the column count and each \(\text{paid}_i \geqslant 0\), so the same inequality also implies the unweighted initial payment \(w_i \hspace{0.17em} \text{paid}_i \leqslant \zeta_d (k)\) used in the preceding induction. If \(a = \zeta_d (k) =\mathfrak{f}_k \leqslant 1\) is the selected label-bound allowance, the column-weighted bound gives the vault budgets \(x = 3 a\) and \(y = a\) by the finite response estimate. The local representative is lawful, has nonnegative coupling and pointwise nonnegative evaluation, and its support exponent is nonnegative. At this selected level put \(s = \hat{h}_k = \hat{\beta}_k^{- 1 / 2}\). The selected delayed-coupling bound gives \(\hat{\beta}_k > 1\), hence \(0 < s \leqslant 1\); the selected vacuum threshold is \(s\) times the exponential of a square, so \(s \leqslant \theta_k\). This choice uses the same selected history and is the radius in its treasury receipt. Its served representation has reserve \(f = 1\) and integrable mode coefficients, as furnished by the finite transition receipt. The pointwise estimate is

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{N}_w (X (U)) \leqslant \exp \hspace{-0.17em} (\text{scalar} (A) + | \Lambda_k | (\text{exCost} (A) + \kappa) + |V| (x + y + 2)) .\)
