Indeed this is the pointwise estimate (4.9) of Subsection 4.2, with the site-count parameter in that formula equal to \(| \Lambda_k |\), \(x = 3 a\), \(y = a\) and reserve \(f = 1\), combined there with the lawfulness bound \(E (U) \leqslant \text{scalar} (A) + | \Lambda_k | \text{exCost} (A)\); the two extra units are charged by that reserve. The denominator lower bound is

\(\displaystyle \int \text{eval} (A) \hspace{0.17em} d \mu \geqslant \exp \hspace{-0.17em} \left( \text{scalar} (A) - | \Lambda_k | \left( \text{exCost} (A) + \beta \hspace{0.17em} \text{baseBudget} + 4 d_G \log (16 / s) \right) \right),\)

where \(\beta \geqslant 0\) and \(d_G\) is the real dimension of the ambient matrix representation space. The same vacuum treasury receipt bounds the per-site bound

\(\displaystyle 2 \text{exCost} (A) + \kappa + \beta \hspace{0.17em} \text{baseBudget} + 4 d_G \log (16 / s) \leqslant \mathcal{T}_k .\)

The selected Wilson transition identities preserve the integral of the unmarked density, so the denominator here equals the terminal Wilson partition function, denoted \(Z_{N, p}\). The common scalar cancels. Integrating a fixed mode coefficient, using its positive toll, and dividing by this denominator gives the finite local bound

\(\displaystyle \begin{aligned} \frac{\left| \int X_I \hspace{0.17em} d \mu \right|}{\int \text{eval} (A) \hspace{0.17em} d \mu} \omega_w (I) & \leqslant \exp \hspace{-0.17em} \left\{ | \Lambda_k | \left( 2 \text{exCost} (A) + \kappa + \beta \hspace{0.17em} \text{baseBudget} \right. \right.\\ & \left. \left. \hspace{47mm} + 4 d_G \log (16 / s) \right) + |V| (4 a + 2) \right\} . \end{aligned}\)

This follows directly from the displayed numerator and denominator: \(|X_I | \omega_w (I) \leqslant \mathcal{N}_w (X)\) pointwise, so \(\left| \int X_I \right| \omega_w (I) \leqslant \int \mathcal{N}_w (X)\); the prior is normalized, and \(x + y + 2 = 4 a + 2 \leqslant 6\). The finite marked-coefficient estimate is therefore at most \(\exp (| \Lambda_k |\mathcal{T}_k + 6| V|)\). In the public root test, let \(M\) be the number of cells at root scale \(m\), with one replicated label per cell and common weight \(w > 0\), and put \(q = (a_m / a_k)^4\), the four-dimensional volume ratio. The centered spacer estimate applies to this moment bound. The aligned side-count identity is \(| \Lambda_k | = Mq\). Thus the finite-volume exponent is \(M (q\mathcal{T}_k + 6)\). Taking the normalized \(M\)th root and dividing by \(w\) gives the centered root-test estimate

\(\displaystyle \exp (q\mathcal{T}_k + 6) / w.\)

This is the marked-copy spacer estimate (4.12) of Subsection 4.2. The treasury contribution survives with factor \(q\) in the root exponent; it is not canceled by the finite-volume denominator. Finite backward Wilson integration preserves each mode integral, as proved in Subsection 4.5.

The service record, the common-trajectory propagation and the finite local bound all come from the one funded record assumed in (i)–(v); the displayed computations show how its clauses enter the bound, and no unpriced per-volume assumption is added.\(\Box\)

4.7.1The funded record on the retained public family

The hypotheses of Theorem 4.15 are an interface for a general history. For the public family they are supplied by the following assembly. We use the direct family \(C_R\), reference map \(t_R\), transfers \(S_R\) and interfaces \(D_R\) of Subsubsection 13.2.1. In particular, no identification with the level-zero default interface of a separately chosen marked lifting is needed.

Proposition 4.16. (The selected family supplies the service record) Retain the public run of Subsection 13.10, with its common funded-history and observable purchases, including the local analytic estimates and common delays of Theorem 13.30. On this one family \(C_R\) and its fixed interfaces, all five hypotheses of Theorem 4.15 hold. Its reference level is \(k_d = t_R (d)\), with \(1 \leqslant t_R (d)\) and \(d + 3 \leqslant J (t_R (d))\). The transfer in its fourth hypothesis is the stored sequence of coefficient maps from Proposition 13.35, normalized once at \(t_R (d)\). Its terminal profile is the singleton hook of Proposition 13.31.

These conclusions hold simultaneously for all periods and terminal volumes in the theorem's domains. The numerical data and the height constant \(X_d\) are fixed independently of those two indices. The stock assertion is required only when \(t_R (d) \leqslant k \leqslant N\); when \(k = N\) it retains the terminal data and has no adjacent-step obligation.

Proof. The retained shooting parameter identifies every adjacent pair with one purchased local return. Lemma 13.27 identifies its fine input, paths, clipping and coupling with those used in the local estimates. These are equalities of the stored data, not a new choice of transition. Theorem 13.30 therefore supplies retention on each \(D_R (N, p, i)\), \(i < N\), and the separate numerical endowment at \(N\). It supplies the response to every admitted finite offer, including the quiet and singleton identities, the common coefficientwise majorants and the flagged bound. Restricting to \(k \leqslant i < N\) leaves the representatives and interface unchanged. This is hypothesis (i), including the shipment and mode-slot data needed in its use for finite censuses.

For (ii), put \(C_i = c_{J (i)} = \Gamma_i\) and \(L_i = \ell_{J (i + 1)}\). Proposition 13.3 gives

\(\displaystyle 64 W (J (i) + 1)^2 \leqslant \Gamma_i, \qquad 100000 W (J (i + 1) + 1)^6 (1 + \Gamma_{i + 1})^2 \leqslant L_i^{1 / 8} .\)

The level-\(i\) length is purchased at the entrance stage \(J (i + 1)\), so the fine column in the second formula has exactly the required index. The same formula holds at level zero. The common delays include those of Theorem 4.13; applying Proposition 4.14 gives positivity, \(B_i \geqslant 8\), the cubic exponential reserve and the radius split. The occupancy \(r_i = N_{J (i + 1)}\) is nondecreasing and at least one, because the prescribed stage occupancy and the clock have these properties. Restriction to \(i \geqslant k\) changes none of these bounds.

For (iii) use the dictionary

\(\displaystyle x_i = \hat{\beta}_i, \quad \zeta_{d, i} = x_i^{- (\deg F_d + 4)}, \quad \phi_i = x_{i + 1}^{- 1}, \quad \delta_i = x_{i + 1}^{- 2}, \quad u_i = \tfrac{1}{4} x_i^{- 1 / 2}, \quad \psi_i = 2 e^{E_{i + 1}} \vartheta_{i + 1} .\)

All entries are fixed by the same schedule; \(E_{i + 1}\) and \(\vartheta_{i + 1}\) are its fine support exponent and fine section bound. The coefficient, activity and adjacent-bound inequalities are precisely those of Proposition 4.14. It applies because \(d \leqslant J (t_R (d)) \leqslant J (k) \leqslant J (i)\). Choose \(X_d \geqslant 1\) to be the uniform grade-\(d\) section bound used in that proposition. At \(i = N\) its bank estimate is

\(\displaystyle e^{E_N + 2} X_d \theta_N^{- \deg F_d} \zeta_{d, N} \leqslant u_N .\)

The same fitted stencil gives footprint cover at most one. Thus this one \(X_d\) supplies every height witness \(X (d, k, N, p)\); no maximum over periods or volumes is being taken.

For (iv), Proposition 13.31 supplies the actual top coefficient family, its linear fields, unit, index isomorphism and singleton hook. Proposition 13.34 constructs the lower coefficient family and its two-cost comparison on each retained interface. The run retains the finite backward construction of Proposition 13.35 and its witness identifying the coefficient maps with its stored transfer, for every admissible \((d, N, p)\). This is the transfer-construction clause of Proposition 13.1: the history and interfaces are fixed first, then the transfers and their witnesses are stored together. We use that retained witness, rather than producing an arbitrary second transfer sequence. Let \(x_t\) be its readout at \(t = t_R (d)\). Precompose every play with the same linear inverse \(x_t^{- 1}\). Then the readout at \(t\) is the identity and all adjacent density and profile identities persist by substitution. This describes the normalization used when the retained transfer \(S_R\) was defined in Lemma 4.8; it is not a replacement of \(S_R\) by another valid transfer. It preserves linearity and the coefficient-independent support dictionary, the cone bounds and the physical unit. One does not normalize again at an intervening level or after choosing a label. The top hook is obtained from the top coefficient family under this same substitution. Its response also has the reserved-mass bound required for terminal birth. Indeed, if \(v\) is the terminal coordinate, the response is the singleton supplement \(f_v\) on the footprint \(Y\) of Subsection 13.7. It is local, gauge invariant, measurable and zero when \(Y\) is healthy, with

\(\displaystyle |f_v | \leqslant X_d \theta_N^{- \deg F_d} \| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} v\|_1 .\)

Reserved mass charges a support by \(\exp ((E_N + 2) C_{B_N} (Y))\) times this majorant (Subsection 7.9). Since \(C_{B_N} (Y) \leqslant 1\), its cost is at most \(e^{E_N + 2} \text{fullCost}_d (P_N, X_d) \| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} v\|_1\). The exact hook identity is the profile calculation for this same supplement. The adjacent profile-difference identity alone would supply neither the terminal hook nor this response witness.

Finally, (v) uses the positive bounds above and the finite local Transfer records of Proposition 13.36. Its regular and forced insertion hypothesis is supplied by Subsection 10.10 on the retained return, with the full ordered-derivative majorants of its remainder convention. Its soundness is part of the retention theorem and its merger is Lemma 13.33. The grade-zero floor supplies \((1 + 2 \Gamma_i) L_i^{- 2 / 5} \leqslant \text{stepSmall} (L_i)\); the insertion estimates themselves are bought at grade \(d\). All these purchases precede selection of the history and of the finite insertion support. This verifies (v), with no additional per-period or per-volume hypothesis. The stronger positive height and height-jump fields, when required, are the positive \(x_i\) powers and the common delay inequality (13.5) of the same purchase.\(\Box\)

Corollary 4.17. (One width for the selected centered censuses) Under the hypotheses of Proposition 4.16, fix a grade \(d\), a label-norm radius \(R > 0\), and a root level \(m\). Choose its opened level \(k \geqslant \max \{m, t_R (d)\}\) as in Subsection 4.4. For every \(N > k\) and every period, the aligned root copies have the served birth representation over the history's own unmarked representative at level \(k\). One positive width, independent of \(N\), period, site and label in the ball, pays all their labels. For every allowed census mode \(I\) its normalized moment satisfies Theorem 4.10 with this width, local bounds \(x = 3 \zeta_{d, k}\), \(y = \zeta_{d, k}\) and reserve one. After the common stencil-interiority tail, this includes the centered root test at every site.

Proof. Lemma 4.9 gives the aligned migrating sites with occupancy one and separation at least \(B_i^2\) at every \(i \geqslant k\). Since \(r_i \geqslant 1\), these satisfy the purchased occupancy bounds, uniformly in the period. The support-bound comparison in Subsection 4.2 applies to every nontrivial census mode. No restriction on the total number of root cells is introduced: the hypotheses are rooted occupancy and separation bounds.

Let \(g_{d, R} \geqslant 1\) bound the coordinate norm on the finite-dimensional grade ball, and put \(D_k = \prod_{i < k} \Gamma_{i + 1}\). The normalized transfer and its forward cone bound give \(q_k (v) \leqslant g_{d, R} D_k\). Thus the common width

\(\displaystyle w_k = \frac{a_k^d \zeta_{d, k}}{\Gamma_k g_{d, R} D_k}\)

satisfies \(w_k \Gamma_k a_k^{- d} q_k (v) \leqslant \zeta_{d, k} \leqslant 1\). The last inequality follows from \(\Gamma_k \zeta_{d, k} \leqslant 1\) and \(\Gamma_k \geqslant 1\). March the weight by \(w_{i + 1} = \phi_i w_i\). The adjacent cone and bound inequality then propagate the weaker payment \(w_i a_i^{- d} q_i (v) \leqslant \zeta_{d, i}\) at every later level, exactly as in Subsection 4.2.

We now instantiate the finite downward construction in that section. At \(N\) the marked polynomial is the finite exponential of the selected terminal insertions multiplied by the bare Wilson density. Its mode coefficients and its singleton profiles are those in Subsection 4.5. The top response from Proposition 4.16 can be moved to the label site and multiplied by its physical grade factor \(a_N^{- d}\). Its reserved cost is at most

\(\displaystyle a_N^{- d} e^{E_N + 2} \text{fullCost}_d (P_N, X_d) \| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} \tau_N (v)\|_1 .\)

Multiplying by \(w_N\), using core contraction and the propagated payment \(w_N a_N^{- d} q_N (v) \leqslant \zeta_{d, N}\), bounds this cost by \(e^{E_N + 2} \text{fullCost}_d (P_N, X_d) \zeta_{d, N} \leqslant u_N\). The response's hook is the moved physical forced profile, by linearity and motion covariance of the same singleton construction. The separate terminal endowment then starts servedness with pitch \(E_N - 3\). At each step the same record supplies the two-cost comparison for every label and the finite-menu response on the fixed interface. The marched payments give the two one-step smallness bounds, and opened-copy geometry supplies the support-bound comparison. Subsection 7.8 therefore advances servedness to the coarse representative of that very transition. The pitch is \(E_i\) for \(i < N\); the input inequality \(E_{i + 1} - 4 \leqslant \pi_{i + 1}\) holds also at the first step from the terminal pitch. Finite induction gives a served representation of the backward birth over \(A_k = R_{N, p, k}\), with its exact singleton identities. Bounded measurable transition kernels preserve integrability at every step.

The local response estimate in Subsection 4.2 now applies with the column-weighted payment at \(k\). It gives \(x = 3 \zeta_{d, k}\), \(y = \zeta_{d, k}\) and reserve one. Its numerator uses the scalar of \(A_k\). The denominator is the integral of the same \(A_k\), which equals \(Z_{N, p}\) by Lemma 4.11. The treasury comparison at the radius \(\hat{\beta}_k^{- 1 / 2}\) in Subsection 4.5 bounds the sum of numerator and denominator costs, so the scalar cancels and the asserted normalized mode bound follows.

The width and every numerical constant used above depend only on \(d, R\), the opened level \(k\) and the fixed schedule. The interiority argument in Subsection 4.2 uses only the fixed finite stencil and \(a_N \to 0\); intersecting its tail with \(N > k\) gives one volume threshold independent of the period, site and label.\(\Box\)

4.8Convergence of correlations along the full mesh sequence

We now pass from convergence of the local data at each fixed level to convergence of the finite-volume correlations themselves. The two limits are different: a terminal correlation contains interactions involving several marked insertions, whereas the fixed-level convergence theorem controls the single-insertion coefficients and their forced profiles. The missing estimate makes the effect of the former interactions small at a sufficiently fine intermediate level, uniformly in the terminal cutoff.

Fix a period \(p\), a finite set \(V\) of marked insertions, their grades \(d_i\), coefficient vectors \(z_i\), and one of the modes \(S\) of their finite marker algebra. Throughout the argument the history, the local fields, and the transfer maps belong to the one selected family. At cutoff \(N\), let \(R_{N, m}\) be its representation at level \(m \leqslant N\). Its product-Haar prior is denoted by \(\mu_{m, p}\), and the positive terminal Wilson partition function by \(Z_{N, p}\). The transferred polynomial density \(X_{N, m}\) satisfies the exact identities

\(\displaystyle X_{N, m} =\mathcal{T}_{N, m} X_{N, m + 1}, \qquad \int X_{N, m} \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_{m, p} = \int X_{N, m + 1} \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_{m + 1, p} .\)

Here \(\mathcal{T}_{N, m}\) is the actual gauge-covariant transfer at the coupling of \(R_{N, m + 1}\); it is applied coefficient by coefficient. The integral of the coefficient of mode \(S\), divided by \(Z_{N, p}\), is the correlation under consideration. In particular all normalizations in this section use the same partition function.

4.8.1The fixed-level data and their uniform bounds

At level \(m\) write \(\Phi_{N, m, i} (U)\) for the single-insertion term in the exponent and \(\omega_{N, m, i} (Q, U)\) for its forced profile, where \(Q\) records a support together with its exact set of flagged sites. Both include the field's scaling factor, its translated origin, and its prescribed motion. These are the actual transferred fields of Subsection 14.21.

For every fixed \(m\) above the entry levels of the finitely many grades, the fixed-level comparison of Subsection 10.13, which retains its adjacent-scale stability input, supplies uniform Cauchy convergence in \(U\) of each \(\Phi_{N, m, i}\) and each \(\omega_{N, m, i} (Q, \cdot)\), and eventual uniform boundedness. This assertion uses both parts of that comparison. The finite-dimensional principal coefficient vectors are Cauchy. The captured remainder vectors are Cauchy in the local norm which controls their evaluation uniformly in \(U\). Evaluation, translation, and the fixed motion preserve these bounds. The local masks are the same at this fixed level for every sufficiently large \(N\), so multiplying by a mask preserves uniform Cauchy convergence even if the mask is discontinuous. The selected paths agree with the stationary paths of the common horizon; their larger-width convergence supplies the path part of the vacuum comparison. The fixed-level activity comparison supplies its remaining part.

Here and below finite initial segments cause no difficulty. A Cauchy sequence of coefficient vectors is bounded, including its finite initial segment. Since the coefficient bound is a continuous seminorm times the fixed scaling factor, there is a number \(A_i\) such that the actual paid coefficient of insertion \(i\) at a fixed root \(r\) is at most \(A_i\) for every cutoff. Choose

\(\displaystyle w_i = \frac{\zeta_{d_i} (r)}{|A_i | + 1} > 0,\) (4.22)

where \(\zeta_d (r) > 0\) is the available single-insertion allowance. Then \(w_i\) times that paid coefficient is at most \(\zeta_{d_i} (r)\). The root \(r\) is chosen after the finite list of grades, so these allowances are positive there.

For later use, at any fixed level \(m\) and for any fixed positive weights \(v_i\), there exist \(x_m, y_m \geqslant 0\), independent of all sufficiently large \(N\), such that

\(\displaystyle v_i | \Phi_{N, m, i} (U) | \leqslant x_m, \qquad \sum_Q v_i | \omega_{N, m, i} (Q, U) | \leqslant y_m \quad (i \in V) .\) (4.23)

Indeed, take eventual bounds \(a_i\) and \(b_{i, Q}\) for these functions and put \(x_m = \sum_i v_i |a_i |\) and \(y_m = \sum_{i, Q} v_i |b_{i, Q} |\). There are only finitely many supports and flag inventories on the fixed torus. Taking the maximum of the finitely many entry thresholds proves both inequalities simultaneously. Measurability follows from the continuous local functions, measurable masks and absolutely summable forced series in their construction.

4.8.2Separating the higher marker terms

Write \(\textsf{M} (\Phi) = \sum_i \Phi_i e_{\{i\}}\) for the single-marker polynomial. With the level and cutoff suppressed, the transferred density has the exact form

\(\displaystyle \ \begin{aligned} \mathcal{R} (H, K) (U) = & e^{R. \text{exponent} (U)} \text{Pass} \hspace{-0.17em} \left( \textsf{M} (\Phi (U)) + \sum_P H_P (U) \right)\\ & \times \mathcal{F} \hspace{-0.17em} (\bar{R}_Q (U) + \textsf{M} (\omega (Q, U)) + K_Q (U) ; \text{flags} (U)) . \end{aligned}\) (4.24)

The finite nilpotent exponential \(\text{Pass}\) was defined in Subsection 4.2. In this formula \(\bar{R}_Q\) is the vacuum forced profile. For an inventory \(F\), \(\mathcal{F} (A ; F)\) is the sum of \(\prod_{Q \in \mathcal{Q}} A_Q\) over families \(\mathcal{Q}\) with disjoint supports whose flag inventories have union \(F\). This is a finite sum on the fixed torus. Every nonzero mode in \(H_P\) or \(K_Q\) contains at least two markers. Its marker origins lie in its support. The functions \(H_P\) vanish off the healthy set of \(P\); the functions \(K_Q\) vanish unless the flag inventory is exactly that specified by \(Q\). Both are local, gauge-invariant and measurable.

For positive marker weights \(v_i\), use the weighted coefficient bound of Subsection 4.2. The higher terms have nonnegative majorants \(a_P\) and \(b_Q\) bounding this bound uniformly in the field. Their rooted support budgets, with weight \(e^{e_m \text{cov}_{B_m} (P)}\), are at most one, for both \(H\) and \(K\). Here \(e_m\) is the level's support exponent, \(B_m\) its support scale and \(\text{cov}_{B_m}\) the cover number already used in those budgets.

We explain why these statements concern the actual density. The centered terminal construction gives (4.24) at level \(N\) with exponent \(e_N - 3\). Its bound uses

\(\displaystyle c_N = \kappa_N + r_N \text{siteFactor} (B_N) u_N, \qquad (2 B_N + 1)^4 c_N \leqslant 3, \qquad e^{c_N} - 1 \leqslant 1,\)

where \(r_N\) is the allowed number of origins per site, \(\kappa_N\) the vacuum forced allowance and \(u_N\) the terminal insertion cost. These are the terminal estimates in the centered birth construction. At a step \(m + 1 \to m\), the purchased finite-menu estimate accepts support exponent \(e_{m + 1} - 4\) and produces the full exponent \(e_m\). The incoming exponent is sufficient, including at \(m + 1 = N\), because \(e_N - 4 \leqslant e_N - 3\). The one-insertion transfer identities identify its single-marker coefficients with the actual \(\Phi_{N, m, i}\) and \(\omega_{N, m, i}\); they are not replaced by estimates. The local transfer ledger and the occupancy budget then give precisely the higher-term majorants above. Integrability propagates through the same transfer. Backward induction proves the assertion at every \(m < N\).

The hypotheses for this induction are the funded finite-menu service of Subsection 3.16 and the centered service of Subsection 4.7. Their single-insertion component is obtained as follows. At the root use (4.22), and propagate weights by \(v_i (m + 1) = \varphi_m v_i (m)\). The centered scale estimates give

\(\displaystyle 0 < \varphi_m, \quad 0 < \delta_m, \quad 1 \leqslant \delta_m \varphi_m e^{B_m / 128}, \quad B_{m + 1}^2 \leqslant L_m B_m^2, \quad B_m \geqslant 8, \quad e_m \geqslant 4.\)

The actual single-insertion transfer estimate and its coefficient bound recursion propagate the root inequality to every later level. The terminal local section has cover number at most one and its purchased coefficient bound. The coefficient generators in that estimate therefore give the terminal insertion, the exact single-insertion recursion, and its local finite transfer ledgers. Summing their forced coefficients by support and exact flag inventory returns the original \(\omega_{N, m, i}\): this is the defining profile identity, not a change of the fields. The remaining applicability conditions are the funded common service and the convergence comparisons just described; their simultaneous production is required in the common-family assembly.

4.8.3Removing the higher terms at a fine intermediate level

Suppose distinct origins at level \(j\) are separated by at least \(B_j^2\) in the lattice metric. A support of a nonzero higher term contains at least two such origins. The cover estimate of Subsection 4.2 gives

\(\displaystyle B_j \leqslant 64 \text{cov}_{B_j} (P) .\)

Put \(S_j = e^{B_j / 64}\). For \(|z| \leqslant S_j\), replace \(H, K\) by \(zH, zK\). The support and flag conditions are unchanged. The bound majorant of a nonzero term is multiplied by \(|z|\), and

\(\displaystyle |z| e^{(e_j - 2) \text{cov}_{B_j} (P)} \leqslant e^{\text{cov}_{B_j} (P)} e^{(e_j - 2) \text{cov}_{B_j} (P)} \leqslant e^{e_j \text{cov}_{B_j} (P)} .\) (4.25)

Zero terms retain the zero majorant. Thus the inflated density has the same cost one at exponent \(e_j - 2\). Its single-insertion terms are unchanged. Transport it from \(j\) down to a fixed root \(r < j\). The first transfer accepts it since \(e_j - 4 \leqslant e_j - 2\); subsequent steps again have the full exponent. The preceding induction therefore applies, with the same root single-insertion terms and weights.

For clarity, separation also supplies the weight reservation needed in this transport. If a mode contains \(q \geqslant 2\) distinct origins, the stronger cover estimate is \(B_m q \leqslant 64 \text{cov}_{B_m} (P)\). Changing each marker weight by at most \(e^{B_m / 128}\) multiplies its toll by at most \(e^{\text{cov}_{B_m} (P) / 2}\). This pays the half-unit reservation in the finite-menu transfer. The identities between parent origins preserve occupancy, and \(B_{m + 1}^2 \leqslant L_m B_m^2\) preserves separation under the exact lattice embeddings. These observations verify the geometric conditions at every step of the transport.

Let \(X_{N, j} =\mathcal{R} (H, K)\) be the actual density at \(j < N\), and set

\(\displaystyle F_N (z) = \frac{1}{Z_{N, p}} \int (\mathcal{R}(zH, zK) (U))_S \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_{j, p} (U) .\)

This is holomorphic in \(z\). Indeed, the marker algebra is nilpotent and the inventory sum finite, so the expression is a polynomial in \(z\) with measurable coefficients. The estimates give integrable coefficient majorants on every smaller disk. At the root, let \(x_r, y_r\) be the eventual bounds (4.23) with weights \(w_i\). The normalized density estimate proved in Subsection 4.2 gives

\(\displaystyle |F_N (z) | \leqslant C_r := \frac{\exp \{| \Lambda_{r, p} | \tau_r + |V| (x_r + y_r + 2)\}}{\omega_w (S)} \qquad (|z| < S_j) .\) (4.26)

To apply it, transfer the inflated density to \(r\). Its integral and its vacuum normalization remain exactly those above. The support budgets at \(r\) are one, and its single-insertion data have the root bounds \(x_r, y_r\). This proves (4.26) with a constant independent of \(j\) and of all sufficiently large \(N\). The bounds at \(j\) needed for integrability may depend on \(j\); they do not enter \(C_r\).

If \(S_j > 2\), Cauchy's estimate bounds \(|F_N' (z) |\) by \(2 C_r / S_j\) for \(|z| \leqslant 1\): the closed disk of radius \(S_j / 2\) about such a point lies in \(|z| < S_j\). Integrating the derivative on \([0, 1]\) therefore gives

\(\displaystyle \left| \frac{1}{Z_{N, p}} \int (X_{N, j})_S \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_{j, p} - \frac{1}{Z_{N, p}} \int (\mathcal{R}(0, 0))_S \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_{j, p} \right| \leqslant 2 C_r e^{- B_j / 64} .\) (4.27)

The first term is the original terminal correlation. Only the terms with at least two markers were erased in the second term.

4.8.4Cauchy convergence of the erased expression

Fix \(j\). Remove the scalar normalization from the representation and put

\(\displaystyle D_{N, j} (U) = e^{- R_{N, j} . \text{scalar}} \mathcal{R} (0, 0) (U), \qquad \ell_{N, j} = e^{- R_{N, j} . \text{scalar}} Z_{N, p} .\)

The second term in (4.27) is exactly \(\int (D_{N, j})_S \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_{j, p} / \ell_{N, j}\); the exponential scalar cancels. The fixed-level vacuum estimate gives a number \(a_{j, p} > 0\) with \(\ell_{N, j} \geqslant a_{j, p}\) eventually, and the fixed-level convergence theorem gives Cauchy convergence of \(\ell_{N, j}\).

The functions \((D_{N, j})_S\) are uniformly Cauchy and eventually uniformly bounded. To see this directly from (4.24), combine the fixed-level vacuum, activity, single-insertion and forced-profile comparisons. There are finitely many coordinates on the fixed torus. Finite products, sums and the nilpotent exponential are continuous functions of those coordinates, uniformly continuous on the compact closures of their bounded ranges. Fixed masks can be kept in every term, as noted earlier. These operations consequently preserve uniform Cauchy convergence. As the prior is a probability measure,

\(\displaystyle \left| \int (D_{N, j})_S \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_{j, p} - \int (D_{M, j})_S \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_{j, p} \right| \leqslant \sup_U | (D_{N, j} (U) - D_{M, j} (U))_S | \longrightarrow 0.\)

The numerator is Cauchy and the denominator has a positive limit, so their ratio is Cauchy as well.

Now \(B_j \to \infty\). Given \(\varepsilon > 0\), first choose a fixed \(j > r\) with \(S_j > 2\) and \(2 C_r e^{- B_j / 64} < \varepsilon / 4\). At this fixed \(j\) the erased expressions differ by less than \(\varepsilon / 4\) for sufficiently large cutoffs \(N, M > j\). Equation (4.27) and the triangle inequality bound the difference of the original correlations by \(3 \varepsilon / 4 < \varepsilon\). This proves Cauchy convergence along the full cutoff sequence, without choosing an additional subsequence.

4.8.5From nested-grid points to all distinct configurations

It remains to supply the separated origins used above and to pass to general points. Call a physical point ancestral if it lies on the mesh at some level. Integer block lengths imply that it lies on every finer mesh, and its addresses obey the exact embedding identity. Ancestral configurations are dense: round each coordinate to the nearest mesh point and use \(a_m \to 0\).

For a fixed ancestral configuration of distinct points on the period \(p\) torus, let \(\eta > 0\) be its minimum pairwise physical separation, measured in the periodic maximum-coordinate (\(\ell^{\infty}\)) distance, as in Theorem 4.28. For zero or one insertion take any \(\eta > 0\) instead. Its lattice separation at level \(m\), in the same periodic \(\ell^{\infty}\) distance between sites, is then at least \(\eta / a_m\). (With the Euclidean distance one would only get \(\eta / (2 a_m)\) in four dimensions, and the condition \(a_m B_m^2 \leqslant \eta\) below would become \(2 a_m B_m^2 \leqslant \eta\).) The chosen schedule satisfies \(a_m B_m^2 \to 0\). Here is the quantitative reason for this limit. Its support-scale estimate eventually gives \(B_{m + 1}^2 \leqslant 2 B_m^2\), whereas the block lengths satisfy \(L_m \geqslant 3\) and \(a_{m + 1} = a_m / L_m\). Thus \(a_{m + 1} B_{m + 1}^2 \leqslant (2 / 3) a_m B_m^2\) on a tail; iteration proves the limit. Choose a common root after all entry levels and after \(a_m B_m^2 \leqslant \eta\) holds on that tail. The origins are then separated by \(B_m^2\) at every later level. They are distinct at the root, so occupancy there is one, within the purchased occupancy schedule. This verifies all geometric conditions for the preceding Cauchy argument.

Finally apply the all-period finite-difference bounds of Subsection 4.11. They give local boundedness and an eventual common modulus on \(\text{NoHit}_p\), the open set of configurations distinct modulo the physical period. The rounded lattice readouts need not themselves be continuous; their mesh errors tend to zero. The explicit local bound is \(C (|x - y| + 2 da_N)\) for a fixed finite coordinate dimension \(d\), so one first chooses \(|x - y|\) small and then the mesh tail. Cauchy convergence on the dense ancestral set in this domain extends to locally uniform convergence there: cover a compact set by finitely many neighborhoods with a common equicontinuity modulus, choose an ancestral point in each, and use the triangle inequality between each point and its chosen center. The corresponding higher difference bounds give the smooth limiting correlations by the argument of Subsection 4.10. Restriction to the ordered Euclidean configuration domain gives the stated position-space ultraviolet limit.

In applying this result to the selected construction, the cutoff parameter is \(N\) itself and every branch is the history chosen by the same shooting construction. The alignment identities identify its paths with the common horizon; the selected transfer maps and their based local sections identify all of the \(\Phi\) and \(\omega\) above. The common centered service supplies the root allowances and their positive tails, the transfer ledgers, and the scale estimates. These identifications are part of the common-family assembly: a service on a different history would not establish the result here.

4.9Ordered limits and Euclidean properties

The lattice spacing is sent to zero at fixed volume before the volume is sent to infinity. A subsequence of pairs \((N_k, p_k)\) tending to infinity in both coordinates need not give either iterated limit. We record the compactness argument that produces one common pair of subsequences for all finite correlation signatures, and then explain which Euclidean properties pass to the resulting table. The construction is conditional on uniform finite-cutoff estimates stated below; their derivation from the Wilson trajectory is a separate input.

Fix one Wilson family \(\mathcal{C}\). Write \(a_N\) for its mesh, \(P_p\) for its physical linear period, and \(\ell_{N, p}\) for the number of sites in that period. For an \(n\)-tuple of field labels \(v\), let \(U_{N, p, v}\) denote the finite-lattice correlation, namely the finite expectation of the chosen insertions. We assume it is multilinear in each label. The factor \(a_N^{4 n}\) appears when these values are smeared by a test function. Let \(\text{Cyc}_p\) be the open set of configurations for which no two distinct insertions have coordinate differences equal to an integer multiple of the physical period \(P_p\) in any coordinate. Write \(\text{NoHit}_p\) for configurations in which each distinct pair has at least one coordinate difference that is not an integer multiple of \(P_p\). This excludes torus-coincident pairs but permits coordinatewise ties. Let \(\text{Gen}\) be the set for which every coordinate projection separates distinct insertions. Let \(\text{Conf}\) be the set of configurations with no coincident points. Thus \(\text{Gen} \subset \text{Conf}\); \(\text{Gen}\) also excludes noncoincident coordinate-projection ties, while \(\text{Conf}\) permits them. The table \(S\) below is the limiting Euclidean correlation table.

Here are the two uniform estimates used for extraction. In each statement “eventually” has its ordinary meaning along the indicated strictly increasing sequence.

Definition 4.18. (Uniform bounds and moduli) Fix an ordered pair \(f = (f_{\text{mesh}}, f_{\text{per}})\) of strictly increasing sequences.

  1. A local mesh modulus means that for every fixed period \(p\), arity \(n\), label tuple \(v\), compact \(K \subset \text{Cyc}_p\), and \(\varepsilon > 0\), there are \(\delta > 0\) and \(j_0\) such that, for \(j \geqslant j_0\) and lattice configurations \(x, y \in K\) at mesh \(a_{f_{\text{mesh}} (j)}\),

    \(\displaystyle |U_{f_{\text{mesh}} (j), p, v} (x) - U_{f_{\text{mesh}} (j), p, v} (y) | < \varepsilon \quad \text{whenever } |x - y| < \delta .\)

    The modulus may depend on \(p, n, v, K, \varepsilon\).

  2. An expanding-volume modulus means that for every \(n, v\), compact \(K \subset \text{Gen}\), and \(\varepsilon > 0\), there are \(\delta > 0\) and thresholds \(k_0\) and \(j_0 (k)\) such that for \(k \geqslant k_0\), \(j \geqslant j_0 (k)\), and lattice configurations \(x, y \in K\) at mesh \(a_{f_{\text{mesh}} (j)}\),

    \(\displaystyle |U_{f_{\text{mesh}} (j), f_{\text{per}} (k), v} (x) - U_{f_{\text{mesh}} (j), f_{\text{per}} (k), v} (y) | < \varepsilon \quad \text{whenever } |x - y| < \delta .\)

    The mesh threshold may depend on the fixed volume index \(k\).

  3. A compact norm bound means both: for every fixed \(p, n, v\) and compact \(K \subset \text{NoHit}_p\), the lattice values \(|U_{f_{\text{mesh}} (j), p, v} (x) |\) are bounded by one constant for all \(x \in K\) and all sufficiently large \(j\); and for every \(n, v\) and compact \(K \subset \text{Conf}\) they are bounded by one constant for all sufficiently large pairs in the ordered sense \(\forall k \geqslant k_0 \hspace{0.27em} \forall j \geqslant j_0 (k)\).

In the ordered-volume clause, the outer threshold is the period threshold and the inner mesh threshold may depend on that period. In particular these assumptions do not assert a single diagonal limit or a modulus uniform over all periods.

Proposition 4.19. (Common ordered extraction) Fix a countable algebraic basis for the local field-label spaces. Assume the local mesh modulus, expanding-volume modulus, and compact norm bound of Definition 4.18 for every finite tuple from this basis, for the same family \(\mathcal{C}\) and initial order \(f\). Then there are strictly increasing subsequences \(t, u\) of the mesh and volume indices. Put \(\rho_k = f_{\text{per}} (u (k))\). There are intermediate continuous functions \(J_{p, v}\) on each \(\text{Cyc}_p\) and a table \(S\) continuous on \(\text{Gen}\), with the following properties:

  1. for each fixed \(p, n, v\), the address-cell interpolants along the mesh subsequence \(f_{\text{mesh}} \circ t\) converge to \(J_{p, v}\) uniformly on every compact subset of \(\text{Cyc}_p\);

  2. for each \(n, v\), \(J_{\rho_k, v}\) converges to \(S\) uniformly on every compact subset of \(\text{Gen}\) as \(k \to \infty\);

  3. for each separated test function suitable at fixed \(p\), the finite-lattice smeared correlations converge first along the mesh subsequence to the integral against \(J_{p, v}\), and then along the volume subsequence to the integral against \(S\) (extended by zero off \(\text{Gen}\), which does not change this integral).

All signature limits use the same \(t, u\). The mesh subsequence is chosen independently of the requested test support. The volume limit is only asserted along \(f_{\text{per}} \circ u\), and the mesh limit remains an ordinary limit along the full mesh subsequence at each eligible fixed period.

Proof. We give the compactness argument, including the common-subsequence step. For each mesh index \(j\) and point \(x\), let \(Q_j (x)\) be the cell representative selected by the lattice address map, so that \(|Q_j (x) - x| \leqslant c_n a_{f_{\text{mesh}} (j)}\) for a constant \(c_n\) depending only on the arity. Define the position-space function \(\tilde{U}_j (x) = U_{f_{\text{mesh}} (j), p, v} (Q_j (x))\). This function is defined even when \(x\) is not a lattice point. Let \(K\) be a compact subset of an open patch and choose a compact neighborhood \(K'\) still inside that patch. For \(x, y \in K\) with \(|x - y| < \delta / 2\), the representatives \(Q_j (x), Q_j (y)\) belong to \(K'\) for all sufficiently large \(j\), and

\(\displaystyle |Q_j (x) - Q_j (y) | \leqslant |x - y| + 2 c_n a_{f_{\text{mesh}} (j)} < \delta .\)

The lattice modulus on \(K'\) therefore makes \(\tilde{U}_j\) asymptotically equicontinuous on \(K\); the rounded functions need not themselves be continuous. The compact norm bound on \(K'\) makes their values bounded there for all sufficiently large \(j\).

The basis-signature set and periods are countable. Choose a countable dense subset \(D\) of each patch. The values \(\tilde{U}_j (d)\), over all dense points and signatures for the fixed period, lie eventually in bounded closed disks in \(\mathbb{C}\). Countable diagonal extraction [Rud76, Theorem 7.23] makes all these scalar sequences converge along one subsequence. We check that this gives compact-uniform convergence, not just convergence on \(D\). Let \(K\) be compact in the patch and \(\varepsilon > 0\). Choose a compact neighborhood \(K'\) inside the patch. Use the common modulus on \(K'\) to choose a radius \(\delta\) for error \(\varepsilon / 3\). Finitely many balls of radius \(\delta / 3\) centered at points of \(D \cap K'\) cover \(K\). At these finitely many centers the selected subsequence is Cauchy. For any \(x \in K\), choose one such center \(d\); for all large indices \(i, i'\), the two modulus errors \(| \tilde{U}_i (x) - \tilde{U}_i (d) |\) and \(| \tilde{U}_{i'} (x) - \tilde{U}_{i'} (d) |\) are each less than \(\varepsilon / 3\), while the center values differ by less than \(\varepsilon / 3\). The triangle inequality proves the subsequence is uniformly Cauchy on \(K\). Completeness of \(\mathbb{C}\) gives a uniform limit on \(K\); limits agree on overlaps, and the same modulus estimate proves the resulting function is continuous. Exhausting the patch by compact sets gives one continuous limit there and uniform convergence on every compact subset. Diagonalize over the countable collection of signatures and a compact exhaustion of each patch to obtain one mesh subsequence \(t\) and all the intermediate functions \(J_{p, v}\).

Now consider the sequence of mesh-limit functions \(J_{f_{\text{per}} (k), v}\) on \(\text{Gen}\). For a compact \(K \subset \text{Gen}\) choose a compact neighborhood \(K' \subset \text{Gen}\) with \(K\) in its interior. For all sufficiently large \(k\), \(K'\) lies inside the fundamental box; since \(K' \subset \text{Gen}\), this gives \(K' \subset \text{Cyc}_{f_{\text{per}} (k)}\). If \(x, y \in K\) and \(|x - y| < \delta / 2\), their representatives at mesh \(a_{f_{\text{mesh}} (j)}\) lie in \(K'\) and have mutual distance less than \(\delta\) for all sufficiently large \(j\). Apply the expanding-volume modulus at the fixed outer index \(k\), then pass \(j \to \infty\) using the already established mesh convergence on \(K'\). This proves asymptotic equicontinuity of the functions \(J_{f_{\text{per}} (k), v}\) on \(K\). Similarly, the ordered compact norm bound and mesh convergence give a uniform bound for these functions on \(K\) for all sufficiently large \(k\).

Choose countable dense subsets in each generic configuration space and repeat the closed-disk diagonal argument, now with \(k\) as the sequence index. The finite-cover argument proves compact-uniform convergence on every compact subset of \(\text{Gen}\) and gives a continuous limit \(S\). Diagonalizing over the countable signature index makes the same volume subsequence \(u\) work for all signatures. Use arity together with finite tuples from a countable algebraic basis as the countable signature index. Every arbitrary finite tuple of labels is a finite linear combination of such basis tuples, and finite lattice moments are multilinear, so finite linearity extends these limits to arbitrary labels without any further subsequence. This is a diagonal argument over the countable basis index, not over the uncountably many arbitrary test functions.

It remains to pass from pointwise limits to smeared moments. Let \(\varphi\) be a separated test, meaning its support \(K\) is compact and contained in \(\text{Conf}\), and fix an eligible period \(p\). Assume \(p\) is eligible for this test, so \(K\) lies strictly inside the comparison cell. Choose a compact neighborhood \(K_1\) of \(K\) whose closure stays inside the comparison cell. For all fine meshes, every cell representative of a point in \(K\) lies in \(K_1\). The cell boundaries in this bounded region lie in finitely many affine hyperplanes and have Lebesgue measure zero. The complement of the cyclic patch is a countable union of affine hyperplanes and therefore has Lebesgue measure zero. At almost every point in \(K\), the cell representatives tend to that point and eventually belong to the cyclic patch. Extend \(J_{p, v}\) by zero off that patch. The mesh convergence gives almost-everywhere convergence of the cell density to \(\varphi J_{p, v}\). Since \(K\) is compactly contained in the fundamental cell and in \(\text{Conf}\), it lies in the fixed-period no-hit set. Choose a compact neighborhood \(K_2\) of \(K\) contained in that open set. For all sufficiently fine meshes the rounded samples of points in \(K\) lie in \(K_2\). The fixed-period compact norm bound on \(K_2\) bounds the finite-lattice moments for all sufficiently fine meshes. If \(\varphi (Q_N (x)) \ne 0\), the cell representative \(Q_N (x)\) belongs to \(K\) and hence to \(K_2\). Therefore the absolute value of the piecewise constant cell density is at most \(D \| \varphi \|_{\infty}\) on the whole fundamental box and is zero outside the union of cells meeting the support. The indicator of the bounded fundamental box times this constant is integrable. The exact cell identity writes the smeared sum as the integral of this density: each cell has volume \(a_N^{4 n}\). The strict cell margin keeps all samples inside the no-wrap region. At points outside the closed support \(K\), the cell density is eventually zero because \(Q_N (x) \to x\); outside the fixed box it is identically zero. Dominated convergence proves the fixed-period smeared limit.

For the volume limit, choose compact neighborhoods \(K_0 \subset K_1 \subset \text{Conf}\) of the support \(K\). For sufficiently fine meshes, every cell representative of a point in \(K_0\) lies in \(K_1\). The ordered compact norm bound on \(K_1\) gives one constant for these lattice values once the period index is sufficiently large and then the mesh threshold is met. For all sufficiently large period indices, \(K_1\) lies inside the fundamental cell. At generic points \(x \in K\), the no-wrap equivalence identifies the cyclic patch with \(\text{Gen}\), and the mesh evaluation lemma passes the lattice bound to \(J_{f_{\text{per}} (k), v} (x)\). The set \(K \setminus \text{Gen}\) is contained in finitely many coordinate hyperplanes and has Lebesgue measure zero. Therefore these intermediate functions are bounded by the same constant almost everywhere on \(K\). Extend \(S\) by zero off \(\text{Gen}\). The volume-point convergence holds at each \(x \in K \cap \text{Gen}\), hence almost everywhere on \(K\). Dominated convergence gives the second integral limit for every separated test, including supports that meet noncoincident coordinate-projection ties. Here \(\text{Gen}\) supplies a full-measure set for the integral passage, while the larger set \(\text{Conf}\) contains the entire test support.\(\Box\)

The two compactness stages use distinct moduli: one at each fixed period and one in the ordered volume limit. The argument preserves the order of limits: no estimate uniform in an unbounded volume threshold is substituted for the permitted threshold \(j_0 (k)\).

Proposition 4.20. (Passage of Euclidean identities and positivity) Let \(S\) be a table obtained with the ordered smeared limits of Proposition 4.19. Assume in addition that:

  1. finite-lattice correlations satisfy exact permutation and lattice translation identities, signed coordinate symmetries, conjugation, and normalization at admissible grid points; labels transform accordingly;

  2. the ordered point limits exist on the generic domain and \(S\) is continuous there; assume also that for each signature it extends to a smooth function on \(\text{Conf}\) agreeing with \(S\) on \(\text{Gen}\);

  3. for each finite list of positive-time smeared observables, the exact finite-lattice pairing identity expresses each reflected product as a finite linear combination of separated smeared correlations covered by the preceding extraction, with coefficients independent of the lattice indices; the same combination defines the limiting smeared pairing. The finite-lattice smeared reflection form is nonnegative for all sufficiently late indices in the ordered sense.

Then the corresponding permutation, translation, signed coordinate-permutation, and conjugation identities hold for \(S\) on the noncoincident domain, and the smeared reflection form of \(S\) is nonnegative for every finite list of admissible positive-time test observables. If, in addition, \(S\) satisfies the separate cap bound required by the Euclidean hierarchy interface, these conclusions together with normalization give its Euclidean positivity and symmetry clauses.

Proof. For permutation and conjugation, fix the signature and a generic configuration and approximate it by admissible lattice configurations. At each sufficiently fine mesh the finite-lattice change of variables gives the exact identity; the point limits along the common ordered filter and uniqueness of limits in \(\mathbb{C}\) give the identity for \(S\). A signed coordinate permutation preserves the lattice, so the same argument applies after transforming the grid points and the insertion labels. For translation by an arbitrary vector \(z \in E_4\), choose lattice translations \(z_j\) converging to \(z\) as the mesh tends to zero. The finite trajectory's anchoring identity gives equality between the two grid evaluations separated by \(z_j\). The grid points converge to the desired translated configurations, and continuity of the limiting correlation gives the translation identity. The generic set is dense in the noncoincident domain; if an identity has first been proved only there, continuity of both sides extends it throughout that domain.

For positivity, fix \(m\) positive-time smeared observables \(A_1, \ldots, A_m\) and coefficients \(c_1, \ldots, c_m \in \mathbb{C}\). The pairing assumption and the ordered convergence of each separated entry show that every entry in the finite reflection matrix converges to its limiting smeared pairing. At each sufficiently late admissible lattice pair, reflection positivity gives

\(\displaystyle Q_{N, p} := \sum_{i, j = 1}^m \overline{c_i} c_j \hspace{0.17em} \langle \theta A_i \hspace{0.17em} A_j \rangle_{N, p} \geqslant 0.\)

There are only \(m^2\) terms. Since every matrix entry has the same ordered limit, finite-sum continuity gives

\(\displaystyle Q_{N, p} \longrightarrow \sum_{i, j} \overline{c_i} c_j \langle \theta A_i A_j \rangle_S .\)

This convergence holds along the same ordered filter. The nonnegative real half-line is closed, so the limit is nonnegative. Since the list and coefficients were arbitrary, the limiting smeared reflection matrix is positive semidefinite. Pointwise reflection positivity for point insertions then follows from the separate bump-approximation argument in Lemma 5.1; that step uses continuity away from coincident points and is not a consequence of generic point convergence alone.

These limit arguments establish only the clauses just named. The extraction gives continuity on \(\text{Gen}\); passing identities to \(\text{Conf}\) requires the separate smoothness hypothesis there. Smoothness on \(\text{Conf}\) includes noncoincident coordinate-projection ties but excludes actual coincident points. Field quotients and Wightman reconstruction do not follow from a subsequence argument; they are separate hypotheses or results in the hierarchy and reconstruction units.\(\Box\)

Proposition 4.21. (A unit insertion in the original limiting table) Fix the same family \(\mathcal{C}\), its raw label spaces and reference depths \(r_d\) before taking limits. In grade \(d\), let \(e_{0, d}\) be the scalar coordinate, let \(D_{N, d}\) be the threshold scaling of coefficients, and write the insertion at site \(o\) and lattice configuration \(A\) as

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{O}_{N, p} (v ; o ; A) = a_N^{- d} \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{P}_d (D_{N, d} X_{N, p, d}^{- 1} v ; o ; A) .\) (4.28)

Here \(X_{N, p, d}\) is the invertible terminal coefficient map, and \(\mathcal{P}_d\) is the continuous local section, linear in its coefficient argument. For the positive threshold \(\tau_N\), the threshold scaling multiplies a coefficient of factor degree \(q\) by \(\tau_N^{- q}\); the scalar coordinate has factor degree zero. Assume the following grade-zero calibration laws on this same family. The local section satisfies \(\mathcal{P}_0 (e_{0, 0} ; o ; A) = 1\) for every site and configuration. For \(N \geqslant r_0\) and every period \(p\), the coefficient readouts \(L_m^{N, p}\) satisfy

\(\displaystyle L_{r_0}^{N, p} (v) = X_{N, p, 0} v, \qquad L_m^{N, p} (a_N^0 e_{0, 0}) = a_m^0 e_{0, 0} \quad (r_0 \leqslant m \leqslant N) .\)

Finite correlations are expectations of products of these insertions under the normalized finite-volume Wilson probability measure.

Let \(S\) be their original, unselected limiting table. Assume the same ordered point-limit hypotheses as in Proposition 4.20, for all signatures including arity zero, with one common order and table. More explicitly, the fixed-period mesh limits apply to the slotwise address samples in each cyclic patch, and the subsequent period limits give \(S\) on \(\text{Gen}\), as in Proposition 4.19. Assume the correlations of \(S\) extend continuously to \(\text{Conf}\); the smoothness hypothesis there is more than sufficient. Then the fixed raw grade-zero label \(\boldsymbol{1}= e_{0, 0}\) satisfies

\(\displaystyle S (\boldsymbol{1}, v_1, \ldots, v_n ; x_0, x_1, \ldots, x_n) = S (v_1, \ldots, v_n ; x_1, \ldots, x_n)\) (4.29)

for every \(n \geqslant 0\), every finite raw label tuple and every pairwise distinct configuration. Moreover \(S (\varnothing) = 1\).

Proof. All raw label spaces and their scalar coordinates were fixed with the family. In particular \(\boldsymbol{1}= e_{0, 0}\) has no dependence on \(N\) or \(p\). At a general grade the physical unit coordinate at level \(m\) is \(a_m^d e_{0, d}\); in grade zero it is exactly \(e_{0, 0}\) at every level. Taking \(m = r_0\) in the calibration law and using the terminal readout gives

\(\displaystyle X_{N, p, 0} e_{0, 0} = L_{r_0}^{N, p} (e_{0, 0}) = e_{0, 0}, \qquad X_{N, p, 0}^{- 1} e_{0, 0} = e_{0, 0} \quad (N \geqslant r_0) .\)

Threshold scaling also fixes this coordinate: its only nonzero component has factor degree zero, so its multiplier is \(\tau_N^0 = 1\). The physical prefactor in (4.28) is \(a_N^0 = 1\). The section's unit law therefore yields

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{O}_{N, p} (\boldsymbol{1}; o ; A) = 1 \quad \text{for every } N \geqslant r_0, \text{ every } p, o, A.\)

Thus no period-dependent or configuration-dependent threshold is needed for this identity; the terminal inverse cannot change the unit.

For any lattice sites \((o_0, \ldots, o_n)\), multiplication of the insertions and the identity just proved give the pointwise equality of integrands

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{O}_{N, p} (\boldsymbol{1}; o_0 ; A) \prod_{j = 1}^n \mathcal{O}_{N, p} (v_j ; o_j ; A) = \prod_{j = 1}^n \mathcal{O}_{N, p} (v_j ; o_j ; A) .\)

The local sections are continuous on the compact finite-lattice configuration space, so these finite products are integrable. Taking the same finite-volume expectation on both sides proves the exact unit-deletion identity for the finite correlations. For \(n = 0\) the product on the right is the constant one. Independently, the arity-zero finite correlation is the expectation of the empty product, hence is one by probability normalization at every cutoff and period. These are identities of point correlations, with no smearing factor \(a_N^{4 n}\).

We pass the deletion identity to the limits without changing the family or the order. Fix first \(x = (x_0, \ldots, x_n) \in \text{Gen}\). Its deleted tuple \(x' = (x_1, \ldots, x_n)\) is also generic: each coordinate projection remains injective after deleting a point. For every sufficiently large fixed period, both tuples lie in their cyclic patches and inside the comparison cell. Indeed their finitely many coordinate differences are nonzero, and the growing period eventually exceeds all their absolute values and the size of a box containing the tuples.

Use the slotwise address map to choose the lattice sites for \(x\), and for \(x'\) delete its zeroth site. Rounding each point coordinate separately, and then wrapping it into the periodic lattice, commutes exactly with this deletion. The lifted samples converge to \(x\) and \(x'\) as the mesh tends to zero. Openness of the two cyclic patches places both samples in compact neighborhoods inside those patches on a common mesh tail. The assumed local uniform mesh convergence there, together with continuity of the intermediate limits, identifies their limits with \(J_{p, (\boldsymbol{1}, v)} (x)\) and \(J_{p, v} (x')\). Intersect this tail with \(N \geqslant r_0\) (and the finitely many reference-depth tails of the requested labels, if needed). The exact finite identity then gives

\(\displaystyle J_{p, (\boldsymbol{1}, v)} (x) = J_{p, v} (x') .\)

Now send the period index to infinity along the same chosen sequence. Both signatures converge to their respective values in the same table \(S\), so uniqueness of limits proves (4.29) on \(\text{Gen}\). All mesh thresholds may depend on the fixed period; intersecting the two tails preserves the ordered quantifiers and requires neither a diagonal limit nor a mesh threshold uniform over periods. At arity zero the configuration space has one point; the two successive limits of its constant finite correlation are still one. This proves \(S (\varnothing) = 1\) and also treats the empty deleted tuple when \(n = 0\).

Finally, \(\text{Gen}\) is dense in \(\text{Conf}\): its complement there is the finite union of coordinate-equality hyperplanes. A distinct configuration has a neighborhood inside \(\text{Conf}\), and arbitrarily small perturbations within that neighborhood avoid all these hyperplanes. The two sides of (4.29) are continuous on \(\text{Conf}\); for the right side use continuity of deletion and the fact that deleting a point preserves distinctness. Equality on the dense subset therefore extends to every pairwise distinct configuration.\(\Box\)

This proposition derives a raw unit law from the retained finite calibration and the same-family limit hypotheses. It does not construct the calibrated family, prove its convergence or smoothness, or construct inclusions between field quotients. No selected-phase unit law is asserted here.

Remark 4.22. (Scope of the extraction) The local estimates are not a proof that the Wilson family has a convergent subsequence by themselves. One must first establish the exact norm and oscillation bounds for that family, uniformly over the compact sets and with the specified fixed-period or ordered-volume quantifiers. Also, the extracted first surface is only continuous on generic patches. The later proof of smoothness on the full noncoincident set (including noncoincident coordinate-projection ties), followed by the full Euclidean hierarchy, has its own proof obligations.

Remark 4.23. (Full mesh convergence versus extraction) Proposition 4.19 is a compactness statement: its first stage selects a mesh subsequence \(t\), and its second a volume subsequence \(u\). The construction used for the main theorem still extracts its hierarchy along a refined order whose mesh component is a subsequence (Subsection 19.6). Its public statement is nevertheless not limited to that mesh subsequence, because at each fixed period it also proves convergence along the whole mesh sequence, by a different argument: the finite-difference bounds of Subsection 4.11 hold at all sufficiently fine meshes, uniformly in the period, and Subsection 4.8 proves that the lattice correlations of one selected history are Cauchy at every ancestral configuration. Theorem 4.24 turns these two inputs into locally uniform convergence of all difference quotients along the full mesh sequence. Its limit is a smooth function on \(\text{NoHit}_p\), a larger set than \(\text{Cyc}_p\). Nothing in that argument passes to a subsequence. Lemma 19.4 then identifies, at every suitable period, the full-mesh limit with the row limit of the extracted hierarchy, so the volume limit of the extracted rows is also the volume limit of the full-mesh rows.

An alternative route is also available for the correlation functions themselves; it is not the route of Subsection 19.6. With these inputs one may take \(t\) to be the identity and \(J_{p, v}\) to be the full-mesh limit, for every eligible period \(p\), not only the periods on the volume sequence. The second stage uses only the functions \(J_{p, v}\), the expanding-volume modulus and the ordered compact norm bound. It applies unchanged and still selects a volume subsequence \(u\); no full volume limit is asserted. The resulting statement is the order of limits in the public theorem: a full mesh limit at every suitable period, followed by a volume limit along a subsequence of the given period sequence. This alternative concerns only the correlations treated in Proposition 4.19: the other data of the extracted hierarchy (fields, phases and the properties stated along its own order) are not thereby transferred to the full-mesh order, and for them the construction uses the identification above. The two stages must not be conflated. A diagonal subsequence \((N_k, p_k)\) yields neither iterated limit. A mesh subsequence chosen separately for each period would not give the full-mesh assertion.

4.10Smooth limits from lattice differences

The extraction argument initially gives continuity only on generic configuration patches. A stronger family of difference bounds removes coordinate ties and identifies every derivative of the limit. This argument also explains the additional input needed for convergence along the whole mesh sequence: pointwise Cauchy convergence on a dense set, without another subsequence selection.

We state the analytic result on an arbitrary open set \(U \subset \mathbb{R}^d\). In the fixed-period application, \(d = 4 n\), \(U\) is the set of configurations with no coincident points modulo the period, and the functions are periodic lifts of lattice correlations. Thus \(U\) includes configurations with coordinate ties and can cross a fundamental-domain seam. Write \(\Gamma_N = a_N \mathbb{Z}^d\), where \(a_N > 0\) and \(a_N \to 0\). For functions \(F_N : \Gamma_N \to \mathbb{C}\), define

\(\displaystyle \Delta_{N, j} F_N (x) = \frac{F_N (x + a_N e_j) - F_N (x)}{a_N}, \qquad F_{N, \alpha} = \Delta_{N, \alpha_1} \cdots \Delta_{N, \alpha_k} F_N\)

for a finite list \(\alpha\) of coordinates, including the empty list. The order convention is that prefixing a coordinate applies one further difference to the previous function. Choose cell representatives \(Q_N : \mathbb{R}^d \to \Gamma_N\) by rounding each coordinate, so \(|Q_N x - x|_1 \leqslant da_N\) and \(Q_N x = x\) on \(\Gamma_N\).

Theorem 4.24. (Full-sequence smooth convergence) Assume that for every compact \(K \subset U\) and every finite list \(\alpha\), there are \(C_{K, \alpha} < \infty\) and \(N_{K, \alpha}\) such that

\(\displaystyle |F_{N, \alpha} (x) | \leqslant C_{K, \alpha} \quad (N \geqslant N_{K, \alpha}, x \in \Gamma_N \cap K) .\) (4.30)

Suppose also that there is a dense subset \(D \subset U\) for which \(F_N (Q_N x)\) is Cauchy for every \(x \in D\). Then there is a smooth function \(f : U \to \mathbb{C}\) such that, for every \(\alpha\),

\(\displaystyle F_{N, \alpha} (Q_N x) \longrightarrow \partial^{\alpha} f (x) \quad \text{uniformly on every compact subset of } U.\) (4.31)

No further subsequence is taken. In particular, whenever \(N_k \to \infty\) and \(x_k \in \Gamma_{N_k}\) tends to \(x \in U\), \(F_{N_k, \alpha} (x_k) \to \partial^{\alpha} f (x)\).

Proof. We give the details in the \(\ell^1\) norm; equivalence of norms in fixed dimension gives the same local convergence in the Euclidean norm. All estimates below are made in a compact neighborhood strictly inside \(U\), and finitely many thresholds in (4.30) can be replaced by their maximum.

Grid Lipschitz and Taylor estimates. Fix \(\alpha\), and abbreviate \(G_N = F_{N, \alpha}\). On a slightly enlarged compact set, bound all its first differences by \(B_1\) and all its second differences by \(B_2\). Join sufficiently nearby grid points \(x, y\) by moving one coordinate at a time, always monotonically toward \(y\). The path has total length \(R = |y - x|_1\) and stays in the coordinate box between \(x, y\). Each edge changes \(G_N\) by at most \(a_N B_1\), including a negative edge by using the forward difference at its lower endpoint. Telescoping gives

\(\displaystyle |G_N (y) - G_N (x) | \leqslant B_1 |y - x|_1 .\) (4.32)

Apply the same argument to each \(\Delta_{N, j} G_N\). At every base point \(z\) of an edge of the path, \(| \Delta_{N, j} G_N (z) - \Delta_{N, j} G_N (x) | \leqslant B_2 R\). Subtract the linear function whose coordinate coefficients are \(\Delta_{N, j} G_N (x)\) from the edge increments, and telescope again:

\(\displaystyle \left| G_N (y) - G_N (x) - \sum_j (y_j - x_j) \Delta_{N, j} G_N (x) \right| \leqslant B_2 |y - x|_1^2 .\) (4.33)

The slightly enlarged compact set ensures that all shifted points used by these differences remain inside the region of the bounds. This is possible because only finitely many shifts occur and \(a_N \to 0\).

Extension from dense pointwise convergence. Equation (4.32) and the rounding bound imply on any compact patch, for sufficiently nearby \(x, y\),

\(\displaystyle |G_N (Q_N x) - G_N (Q_N y) | \leqslant B_1 (|x - y|_1 + 2 da_N) .\)

Thus the rounded functions are asymptotically equicontinuous; they need not themselves be continuous. For \(\alpha = \varnothing\), this and the Cauchy property on \(D\) imply uniform Cauchy convergence on each compact set. To see it explicitly, cover that compact set by finitely many sufficiently small balls with centers in \(D\), lying in a common compact neighborhood in \(U\). At the centers, choose one common Cauchy threshold. Choose the mesh still finer so that the two oscillation errors, from a point to its center for each of the two mesh functions, are small. The triangle inequality then bounds their difference uniformly on the whole compact set. Completeness of \(\mathbb{C}\) gives a uniform limit. The displayed modulus makes the limit continuous. Limits on overlapping compact neighborhoods agree by pointwise uniqueness, giving a single continuous \(f\) on \(U\).

This reasoning also proves a useful moving-point assertion: if the rounded \(G_N\) converge locally uniformly to a continuous \(g\), and grid points \(y_N \to y \in U\), then \(G_N (y_N) \to g (y)\). Indeed, eventually the points lie in one compact neighborhood; use \(Q_N y_N = y_N\), uniform convergence there, and continuity of \(g\). The same statement holds after any indices \(N_k \to \infty\).

Cauchy convergence of the next differences. Suppose now the rounded \(G_N = F_{N, \alpha}\) converge locally uniformly to \(g\). Fix \(x \in U\) and a coordinate \(j\). Take a small \(h > 0\) such that a compact neighborhood of the segment \([x, x + he_j]\) lies in \(U\). Put \(h_N = a_N \lceil h / a_N \rceil\), \(x_N = Q_N x\), and \(y_N = x_N + h_N e_j\). Then \(h \leqslant h_N < h + a_N\), so the moving-point assertion implies convergence of

\(\displaystyle V_N = \frac{G_N (y_N) - G_N (x_N)}{h_N} \longrightarrow \frac{g (x + he_j) - g (x)}{h} .\)

The Taylor estimate gives

\(\displaystyle | \Delta_{N, j} G_N (x_N) - V_N | \leqslant B_2 h_N .\)

Here \(B_2\) can be fixed on one compact neighborhood for every sufficiently small \(h\). Given \(\epsilon > 0\), first choose \(h\) so that \(4 B_2 h < \epsilon / 2\), then choose \(N\) large so that \(h_N \leqslant 2 h\) and \(V_N\) is Cauchy with error less than \(\epsilon / 2\). The triangle inequality proves that \(\Delta_{N, j} G_N (Q_N x)\) is Cauchy. Bounds for its first differences give asymptotic equicontinuity by the previous argument. It therefore converges locally uniformly to a continuous function \(g_j\).

Identification of derivatives and induction. Apply (4.33) with \(Q_N x, Q_N y\), for fixed nearby \(x, y\), and pass to the limit using the just-established convergence of all first differences. This gives

\(\displaystyle |g (y) - g (x) - \sum_j (y_j - x_j) g_j (x) | \leqslant B_2 |y - x|_1^2 .\)

The bound proves Fréchet differentiability, with \(Dg (x) [u] = \sum_j u_j g_j (x)\). Since the \(g_j\) are continuous, \(g\) is \(C^1\). Repeat this argument for every finite list \(\alpha\). Induction gives locally uniform limits for all iterated differences and identifies them with the corresponding derivatives of \(f\). Their continuity proves \(f \in C^k\) for every finite \(k\), hence smoothness. The case \(d = 0\) is simply convergence of one scalar sequence; there are no derivative steps. This completes the proof.\(\Box\)

Corollary 4.25. (Other fixed difference stencils) The same limits hold for any fixed finite composition of forward, backward or centered coordinate differences, provided every difference is divided by its full physical interval length.

Proof. For fixed integers \(b, c \geqslant 0\) with \(b + c > 0\), telescope:

\(\displaystyle \frac{G_N (x + ca_N e_j) - G_N (x - ba_N e_j)}{(b + c) a_N} = \frac{1}{b + c} \sum_{r = 0}^{b + c - 1} \Delta_{N, j} G_N (x + (r - b) a_N e_j) .\)

Each of the finitely many sample points approaches the same point, so the moving-point conclusion in Theorem 4.24 gives the same derivative for each summand. Translation averages commute with coordinate differences. A finite composition is therefore a finite average of shifted iterated unit forward differences, and has exactly the stated limiting derivative. Uniform convergence on compacts follows by using one slightly enlarged compact neighborhood for all the finitely many shifts. No assertion is made when the stencil sizes vary with \(N\).\(\Box\)

The theorem is applied separately for each fixed period, label tuple and finite derivative request. Its derivative bounds are supplied by the full-precision lattice estimates, and its dense Cauchy input by the renormalization-group comparison of different cutoffs. Neither input follows from a subsequential limit alone. Once they are supplied, the fixed-period smooth kernel and all its locally uniform difference limits follow without further mesh extraction. The fixed-period smeared limits then follow by the cell-integral dominated-convergence argument of Proposition 4.19, applied to each fixed stencil.

4.11Buying the full-mesh finite-difference bounds

We prove the finite-volume estimate needed before any continuum extraction. The important order is to pay for the bounded projected coefficient first, and only then use the exact Wilson difference identity. The result is uniform over every period at all sufficiently fine meshes. The proof has two parts: a finite dimensional absorption estimate for one coefficient row, and a packing argument which plants finitely many such rows into one large periodic box.

For a period \(p\), mesh \(N\), field list \(v = (v_1, \ldots, v_n)\), and finite list \(l\) of slot-and-coordinate directions, write \(K_{N, p ; v, l} (x)\) for the finite Wilson expectation of the product of the corresponding iterated periodic difference insertions at the sites \(x = (x_1, \ldots, x_n)\) of the period-\(p\) torus at mesh \(N\). Sites have integer coordinates; the physical position of a site \(z\) is \(a_N z\). The normalization is \(\Delta_{N, \alpha} F (z) = a_N^{- 1} (F (z + e_{\alpha}) - F (z))\) for a site \(z\) and a unit lattice step \(e_{\alpha}\). Thus the theorem below concerns the unscaled derivative kernel; the exact one-step identity used in its proof is \(F (z + e_{\alpha}) - F (z) = a_N \Delta_{N, \alpha} F (z)\).

Throughout this section the index set \(I\) is the set of mesh-period pairs \(i = (N, p)\) with the preorder \((N, p) \leqslant (N', p')\) if and only if \(N \leqslant N'\). A statement holds eventually in \(I\) if there is \(N_0\) such that it holds for all \(N \geqslant N_0\) and every period \(p\). A limit in \(I\) is understood in the same sense: \(y_i \to y\) means that for every \(\varepsilon > 0\) there is \(N_0\) with \(|y_{(N, p)} - y| < \varepsilon\) for all \(N \geqslant N_0\) and all \(p\). With the product order instead, “eventually” would exclude small periods and the uniformity asserted below would not follow.

Lemma 4.26. (Absorption of the projected coefficient) Let \(I\) be the mesh-period index set just described, and let \(X_i\) be a finite-dimensional normed coefficient space. Suppose that for each \(i\) there are a projection \(P_i : X_i \to X_i\), a linear map \(L_i : X_i \to Y_i\), and a constant \(Z > 0\) such that

\(\displaystyle \|P_i z\| \leqslant Z \|L_i (P_i z)\| . \) (4.34)

Let \(x_i \in X_i\), put \(X_i^0 = P_i x_i\), and set \(\lambda_i = (1 +\|X_i^0 \|)^{- 1}\). Assume the following estimates hold eventually in \(I\):

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} \|L_i x_i \| & \leqslant c, \hspace{3cm} \text{(4.35)} \\ \|L_i (\lambda_i (x_i - X_i^0))\| & \leqslant \frac{1}{2 Z} . \hspace{3cm} \text{(4.36)} \end{array}\)

Then eventually

\(\displaystyle \|X_i^0 \| \leqslant 2 Zc + 1. \) (4.37)

Proof. Write \(R_i = x_i - X_i^0\). By linearity and (4.35),

\(\displaystyle \|X_i^0 \| \leqslant Z \|L_i X_i^0 \| \leqslant Zc + Z \|L_i R_i \| .\)

Since \(\lambda_i = (1 +\|X_i^0 \|)^{- 1}\), (4.36) implies \(Z \|L_i R_i \| \leqslant (1 +\|X_i^0 \|) / 2\). Therefore

\(\displaystyle \|X_i^0 \| \leqslant Zc + \tfrac{1}{2} (1 +\|X_i^0 \|),\)

and rearranging gives (4.37). All estimates were eventual, so their two mesh thresholds can be replaced by their maximum.\(\Box\)

Proposition 4.27. (One coefficient row is bounded at every period) Let \(C \subseteq \prod_i X_i\) be one class of coefficient row families satisfying the following normalized-cost conditions. Suppose that the full family \(x = (x_i)\) belongs to \(C\), that \(C\) is closed under subtraction and pointwise multiplication by scalar families whose absolute values are at most one, and that every uniformly norm-bounded row family belongs to \(C\). Suppose also that the detector estimates

  1. \(\|P_i z\| \leqslant Z \|L_i (P_i z)\|\) for all \(z \in X_i\) with a scale-independent \(Z > 0\);

  2. for the primary paid trial there are assay values \(b_i \in Y_i\) with \(\|b_i \| \leqslant v\) and \(\|L_i x_i - b_i \| \to 0\);

  3. for every row family \(z \in C\) satisfying \(P_i z_i = 0\) for every \(i\), one has \(\|L_i z_i \| \to 0\).

Then, eventually in \(I\), that is, for all sufficiently fine meshes and uniformly over all periods,

\(\displaystyle \|P_i x_i \| \leqslant 2 Z (v + 1) + 1.\)

The uniformity means that the limits in items 2 and 3 are taken in \(I\): they hold beyond one mesh threshold for every period.

Proof. From item 2, eventually in \(I\), \(\|L_i x_i \| \leqslant v + 1 = : c\). Put \(X_i^0 = P_i x_i\) and

\(\displaystyle \lambda_i = (1 +\|X_i^0 \|)^{- 1}, \qquad z_i = \lambda_i (x_i - X_i^0) .\)

The scalar family \(\lambda = (\lambda_i)_i\) satisfies \(| \lambda_i | \leqslant 1\), so \(\lambda x \in C\). The family \(\lambda X^0\) is uniformly bounded by one, so it belongs to \(C\) as well. Therefore \(z = \lambda (x - X^0) \in C\) by subtraction, and \(P_i z_i = 0\) because \(P_i (x_i - P_i x_i) = 0\). By item 3, for all sufficiently fine meshes \(\|L_i z_i \| \leqslant (2 Z)^{- 1}\). Since \(L_i z_i = \lambda_i L_i (x_i - X_i^0)\), estimate (4.34) gives

\(\displaystyle \|X_i^0 \| \leqslant Z \|L_i X_i^0 \| \leqslant Zc + Z \|L_i (x_i - X_i^0)\| \leqslant Zc + \frac{1 + \|X_i^0 \|}{2} .\)

Rearranging proves the claim. The common mesh threshold is the maximum of the mesh thresholds in items 2 and 3.\(\Box\)

The cap alone bounds a projected row. The difference trial is paid by a separate closure argument which uses that bound; we give its steps. The primary paid trial, the exact equation with its vanishing owed cost, the bounded-coefficient receipt and the sweep for rows with zero projection are first kept explicit in Theorem 4.28 below. Proposition 4.31 verifies them for the retained public family. Let \(x\) be the coefficient row for the requested insertion and let \(F\) be its primary paid trial. The exact equation gives an owed row \(e = \text{upper} (x) - \text{hops} (F)\), with a root-test cost \(u\) tending to zero. Applying the cap to \((x, F, e)\) bounds \(Px\) uniformly. Thus \(y = Px\) is a uniformly bounded row family and its primary insertion is paid by the bounded-coefficient receipt. The row \(x - y\) lies in the normalized cost class by the class closure, and \(P (x - y) = 0\). The sweep theorem therefore makes \(\text{upper} (x - y)\) owed with a cost tending to zero. We have the identity

\(\displaystyle \text{hops} (F) = \text{upper} (y) + \text{upper} (x - y) - e.\)

It follows from \(\text{upper} (x) = \text{upper} (y) + \text{upper} (x - y)\) and the defining equation for \(e\). The paid budget is closed under finite addition and subtraction: once each vanishing cost is below the unit allowance at a sufficiently fine mesh, the primary paid budget together with the two unit owed budgets yields a finite paid budget for the requested difference trial. This is exactly the projection-and-remainder argument; it does not require the unsplit equation residual to be bounded by a quantity that depends on \(\|Px\|\).

Theorem 4.28. (Full-mesh bounds for all finite differences) Fix a family for which the preceding coefficient-row estimate and the paid-remainder decomposition just described apply to every finite list of fields and difference directions, including each resulting difference trial transformed by a signed-coordinate action, with or without coefficientwise conjugation. Assume that each bounded primary coefficient row family and each of its finitely many corresponding orientations has a paid root-test budget in the following sense: for each such oriented row family there is a depth \(k_0\) such that, for every centred scale \(k \geqslant k_0\), there are a budget \(b < \infty\) and a mesh threshold valid for every period beyond which the centred root test at scale \(k\) is at most \(b\). The bounds and thresholds may depend on the oriented family and \(k\). Assume also that the exact periodic one-step difference identity holds, and that translated insertions satisfy the product root-test inequality in distinct interior cells. Then for each finite field list \(v\), difference list \(l\), and \(r > 0\), there are \(B < \infty\) and a mesh threshold \(N_0\) such that for every \(N \geqslant N_0\), every period \(p\), and every periodic position tuple \(x\) such that any two distinct slot indices \(u \ne u'\) have sites \(x_u, x_{u'}\) with

\(\displaystyle a_N \hspace{0.17em} \text{sep}_{N, p} (x_u, x_{u'}) \geqslant r\)

(even if the corresponding insertions agree),

\(\displaystyle |K_{N, p ; v, l} (x) | \leqslant B. \) (4.38)

The threshold is independent of \(p\); the bound is independent of both \(N\) and \(p\). Here \(\text{sep}_{N, p} (y, y') = \max_{\alpha} |y_{\alpha} - y'_{\alpha} |_{\text{per}}\) is the periodic maximum-coordinate distance of two sites of the period-\(p\) torus at mesh \(N\), where \(|k|_{\text{per}}\) denotes the distance from the integer \(k\) to the nearest multiple of the side length \(\ell_{N, p}\). Thus the separation is measured in the periodic \(\ell^{\infty}\) metric, not the Euclidean one; the injectivity argument below uses exactly this metric.

Proof. First fund one row. For a field label \(w\), the constant coefficient row \(x_i = w\) has bounded normalized cost. The assumed primary receipt and the preceding cap-and-remainder argument give a paid budget for each finite difference list applied to that insertion, eventually at every mesh and every period. For a general finite list \(v = (v_1, \ldots, v_n)\) and positional derivative list, make one trial for each slot by applying to \(v_j\) the subsequence of directions assigned to that slot. The exact one-step identity is the periodic discrete difference formula

\(\displaystyle F_j (z + e_{\alpha}) - F_j (z) = a_N \hspace{0.17em} \Delta_{N, \alpha} F_j (z),\)

with the normalization of the difference operator. Iterating this identity and the exact product measure definition gives the desired multi-difference kernel, including wrapped coordinates. The list is finite, so the paid records have finitely many depth thresholds \(k_0\).

We now explain the two-scale tiling argument. First choose a tiling scale \(\ell\) with \(a_{\ell} < r\). Then use \(a_k \to 0\) to choose one recentering scale \(k \geqslant \ell\) which lies beyond all the finitely many depth thresholds \(k_0\) of the paid records and satisfies

\(\displaystyle 6 na_k < a_{\ell} .\)
