At this scale \(k\) all the root-test budgets hold beyond a common mesh threshold, uniformly over periods (take the maximum of the finitely many mesh thresholds). At a fine mesh \(N\) beyond both scales, let \(t\) be the integer span of scale \(k\) and let \(T\) be a periodic tiling whose side is the integer span of scale \(\ell\). The aligned-scale identities give \(a_N t = a_k\) and \(a_N T. \text{side} = a_{\ell}\). Thus \(n (2 (3 t)) < T. \text{side}\). The tiling placement lemma, applied with margin \(3 t\), gives one translated tiling and an assignment of the \(n\) insertion sites to cells, each site lying at least \(3 t\) from the cell boundary. The boundary count is the one in the proof of Theorem 4.5: in each coordinate one site forbids at most \(6 t\) of the \(T. \text{side}\) possible translations, so the \(n\) sites forbid fewer than \(T. \text{side}\) of them. This is uniform in the period: alignment gives the same physical side \(a_{\ell}\) for every period index, once \(N\) is sufficiently fine.

The cell assignment is injective for a geometric reason. If two sites were assigned to the same cell, then in each coordinate both lie in one window of \(T. \text{side}\) consecutive residues, so their periodic coordinate distance is at most \(T. \text{side}\); hence \(\text{sep}_{N, p} \leqslant T. \text{side}\). Multiplying by \(a_N\) would give \(a_N \text{sep}_{N, p} \leqslant a_N T. \text{side} = a_{\ell} < r\), contradicting the assumption. (With Euclidean separation one would instead need \(2 a_{\ell} < r\) in four dimensions; the statement above avoids this by using the \(\ell^{\infty}\) metric throughout.) The three-span margin is exactly what the centering lemma needs to transport each local insertion from the centered tile to its assigned cell.

For each occupied cell, recenter its insertion to the distinguished center cell. The budgets are root tests relative to the fine centred tiling at scale \(k\), whereas the planted inequality below is applied in the coarse tiling of side \(a_{\ell}\). Invariance of the Wilson law under translations and signed coordinate symmetries does not by itself compare root tests of different tilings. The comparison is the replicated-orbit and maximum-principle argument in the proof of Theorem 4.5 (the packing argument, with its coarse depth \(m\) and fine depth \(k\) taken to be \(\ell\) and \(k\) here): the fine planted inequality bounds the root test of each insertion relative to the coarse tiling at a home point on the fine centre grid by the fine budget, the one-dimensional maximum principle moves the home point to the assigned site, and signed-coordinate covariance identifies the oriented copy in each occupied cell with the original insertion. The hypothesis supplies the root-test budgets for the finitely many signed-coordinate and conjugated orientations of each trial. Include their paid records in the finite list above, as in Lemma 4.4, before taking the maximum of the depth thresholds and the maximum of the mesh thresholds at the chosen scale. This transports both the interior support condition and the same root-test budget to the occupied cell. No statistical independence is asserted: all factors are evaluated under the same Wilson law. Instead, the planted-cell inequality applies to the translated copies in distinct cells and gives

\(\displaystyle \left| \mathbb{E} \prod_{j = 1}^n F_j (x_j) \right| \leqslant \prod_{j = 1}^n b_j,\)

where \(b_j\) is the paid root-test bound for the \(j\)th trial. Their finite product is a constant independent of mesh and period. This proves (4.38).\(\Box\)

4.11.1The uniform supplier on the retained family

We now verify the hypotheses above on \(C_R\). In this subsection every occurrence of “eventually” means eventually in \(I\), including periods smaller than any prescribed comparison level. This is stronger than an ordered extraction statement.

Call a row \(x = (x_{N, p})\) of grade \(d\) uniformly subpower if

\(\displaystyle \forall \epsilon > 0 \exists N_{\epsilon} \forall N \geqslant N_{\epsilon} \forall p, \qquad |X_{N, p}^{- 1} x_{N, p} |_1 \leqslant e^{\epsilon s_N} .\)

Here \(X\) is the actual terminal calibration of Subsection 16.10. Denote this class by \(\mathscr{C}_d\). It is closed under addition, subtraction and scalar multiplication by families of modulus at most one: split the target exponent between the finitely many bounds and absorb their fixed sum using \(s_N \to \infty\). Every norm-bounded row belongs to it. Indeed the reverse-column estimate of Subsubsection 7.4.3 bounds its inverse coordinates by a constant times \(J_N\), and the displayed proof \(\log J_N = o (s_N)\) involves only the schedule index \(N\), not \(p\). The same observation applies to the bounds used below. The threshold powers, column allowances, profile costs and reserve inverses are sequences of \(N\) alone. Their subpower proofs use the clock and finite stage purchases; in particular the reserve inverse is bounded by \(4 \hat{\beta}_N\) and the derivative-shift bound by \(\hat{\beta}_N\). Their finite products therefore have one subpower majorant independent of \(p\).

Lemma 4.29. (Uniform equation sweep and local encoding) Let \(\Pi_{N, p}\) be the uniformly bounded finite projection with kernel \(K_{N, p}\) constructed in Subsection 16.10, using Subsection 16.12. For \(z \in \mathscr{C}_d\) with \(\Pi_{N, p} z_{N, p} = 0\), the physical insertion \(O (z)\) has centered root bound tending to zero in \(I\), at every sufficiently fine fixed cell depth, in all its finitely many signed and conjugated orientations. The same holds in translated cells with a fixed relative interior margin.

Every continuous protected trial whose two bounds are uniformly subpower has, on the same family, a comparison row \(x \in \mathscr{C}_e\) and a discrepancy \(O (x) - F\) with such a vanishing root bound. In particular this applies to every finite difference word of a bounded primary row, with \(e\) increased by the word length.

Proof. The columns in (16.78) are constructed beyond one \(N\)-threshold for every \(p\), with the same bound \(E_N\). All factors in that bound are the \(N\)-only factors just described. Consequently its subpower bound holds in \(I\). For a row in the kernel, put \(v = P_{N, p}^{- 1} X_{N, p}^{- 1} z\) in the notation of that section; \(|P_{N, p}^{- 1} | \leqslant 2\). Thus \(v\) has a uniform subpower bound and the finite sum of oriented column errors has bound

\(\displaystyle c_{d, m, k} E_N |v_{N, p} |_1 e^{- s_N / 8} .\)

For each fixed opened pair this is at most \(e^{- s_N / 16}\) eventually in \(I\). The common-tiling argument there transfers it to any prescribed coarser cell and relative margin, using a finer fixed opened depth. Its finite sum and fixed geometric factors can, if needed, be absorbed by reducing the rate to \(e^{- s_N / 32}\). The alignment identities and interiority thresholds depend on \(a_N\) and the fixed stencils, not on \(p\).

It remains essential to dispose of the comparison insertion itself, without using an ordered spectator estimate. Since \(z \in K_{N, p}\), the comparison is the marked lifting insertion of an element of the exact Maxwell equation ideal: the factors \(S_{\theta_N^{- 1}} S_{\theta_N}\) cancel exactly. Choose a finite basis of that ideal and one radius containing all its microscopic equation stencils. In a centered root test at a fixed physical cell size, the other reflected copies depend only on links outside this radius once \(N\) is large. The smallest admitted physical period is positive, so the finite nonwrapping floors are also reached by one \(N\)-threshold for every \(p\). Apply the exact microscopic integration identity to one copy and the product of all other copies. This product is bounded and measurable at each finite cutoff. Its integral is exactly zero, even if the coefficients of the equation vector are large. The centered root of the comparison is therefore zero. Linearity covers every vector in the ideal, including cutoff-dependent vectors; the stencil threshold is independent of those coefficients. The root triangle inequality now gives the claimed sweep. No conversion to an ordered spectator limit occurs.

For the second assertion use the terminal construction in Subsection 16.11. Its bound \(E_{N, p}\) is a finite sum of products of the two protected bounds and the same schedule factors. It has a uniform subpower majorant. The actual descent gives \(|X_{N, p}^{- 1} x_{N, p} |_1 \leqslant 2 E_{N, p}\) and root discrepancy \(c_{e, m, k} E_{N, p} e^{- s_N / 8}\). The preceding common-tiling argument is therefore uniform in \(p\) here as well. For a word of length \(q\), repeat (16.83) \(q\) times. At engineering weight \(d + q\) the powers \(a_N^{- q}\) cancel; the stencil is a finite union of at most \(2^q\) fixed translates. The derivative bound is at most \((J_N + 1)^q\) times the initial bound and the global bound at most \(2^q\) times it. These are uniformly subpower. This proves the assertion for all finite words and their orientations without assuming a bound for the differentiated correlations.\(\Box\)

Lemma 4.30. (A uniformly bounded primary and a finite detector) Fix a grade \(e\) and a finite direction word \(l\). There are finite linear detectors \(L_i\) on its reference coefficient space and \(Z > 0\), chosen before \(N, p\), such that eventually in \(I\)

\(\displaystyle | \Pi_i v| \leqslant Z |L_i \Pi_i v| \qquad (v \in V_e) .\)

The detectors are averages of correlations with finitely many bounded primary spectator rows in small neighborhoods of the purchased generic detector configurations. They have the following two properties:

  1. If \(F\) has a uniform paid primary root budget, the assay of \(D_l F\) is bounded eventually in \(I\).

  2. A continuous trial with a vanishing uniform root bound has assay tending to zero in \(I\).

Every bounded primary row has the required uniform root budget, at every sufficiently fine fixed centered depth, as do its orientations.

Proof. The last assertion follows directly from Corollary 4.17. At a fixed grade and radius its width \(w_k\) is independent of \(N, p\) and the label in that ball; its displayed normalized moment bound gives a finite root budget. For a requested cell depth choose a finer opened depth and transfer that budget by the replicated-orbit and maximum-principle argument of Theorem 4.5. All thresholds are stencil interiority thresholds or \(N > k\), uniformly in \(p\). Finitely many orientations remain in a bounded ball because their coefficient maps are fixed linear or conjugate-linear maps.

Write \(r = \dim (V_e /\mathcal{I}_e)\) for the free quotient dimension, with the marked lifting identification understood. The exact calibrated isomorphism taking \(\mathcal{I}_e\) to \(K_i\) gives \(\text{rank} \hspace{0.17em} \Pi_i = r\) at each finite cutoff; this does not use a limiting null-space assertion. If \(r = 0\) the projection vanishes and there is nothing to prove. Otherwise choose the \(r\) independent free columns and finitely many separating rows used in Subsection 16.8. Their target matrix \(G\) is injective. In supremum norms choose \(K\) with \(|c| \leqslant K |Gc|\). If every entry of \(Q - G\) is less than \(\delta = (2 Kr)^{- 1}\), then \(|c| \leqslant K|Qc| + \tfrac{1}{2} |c|\), hence \(|c| \leqslant 2 K |Qc|\).

The purchased comparison in that section holds before extraction: its corrected root error is \(\epsilon_k\), and undoing the finitely many connected subtractions costs the free-subset moment polynomial \(P_k\) of (16.57). At each fixed comparison level its rescaled lattice configurations are fixed before \(N, p\); let \(G_k\) be their free measurement matrix. These configurations converge to the prescribed generic configurations as \(k\) increases. Continuity of the finitely many free moments on a common separated neighborhood gives \(G_k \to G\) and bounds \(P_k\). Choose one sufficiently deep comparison level \(k\) so that, entrywise, \(|G_k - G| < \delta / 8\) and \(P_k \epsilon_k < \delta / 8\). Before either cutoff limit is taken, the finite-cell inequality applied to the paid corrected coefficient therefore puts the actual point measurement matrix within \(\delta / 4\) of \(G\), beyond one mesh threshold for every period. This is the finite-cutoff estimate used here; its later passage to the ordered limits is not needed. Its lifts \(u_{j, i}\) have one norm bound \(B_k\) for all \(N, p\), by (16.56). Their detector positions have a fixed interior margin in the comparison cell. Choose small closed neighborhoods of those positions inside the smallest admitted torus and the generic set. All coordinate gaps there are at least \(4 \rho > 0\) modulo every period: for the smallest period this follows by a compact minimum; larger periods cannot create a wrap inside this common interior box. Shrink the neighborhoods if necessary.

The primary budgets just established give, on these neighborhoods, the uniform lattice modulus proved in the directional part of Theorem 4.5:

\(\displaystyle |H_i (x) - H_i (y) | \leqslant 4 nA^n \rho^{- 1} |x - y| .\)

Here \(n\) is the arity of the detector row, all its labels lie in the fixed ball containing the lifts, and the centered depth is chosen with \(a_m < \rho\). The proof uses the bounded primary bricks and the Schatten–Cauchy transfer estimate; it does not use a derivative-field bound. All gaps and primary budgets here are uniform over every period, so the displayed proof gives one mesh threshold for this neighborhood. The exact aligned detector sites represent the same physical centers once \(N\) exceeds their fixed depth.

We specify the averaging operation, also to control derivatives. Choose \(b > 0\) small enough and put \(h_N = \lceil b / a_N \rceil\). Average a detector correlation over shifts \(0, \ldots, h_N - 1\) in the distinguished slot, once for each direction in \(l\). The total physical displacement is at most \(2 |l| b\) eventually. Choose \(b\) so that this lies inside the neighborhoods and changes each primary detector entry by less than \(\delta / 4\). For the empty word the averaging is the identity. These averages define \(L_i\). The sweep lemma applies to the bounded rows \((I - \Pi_i) u_{j, i}\): both the rows and their projected parts are bounded uniformly, and thus belong to \(\mathscr{C}_e\). Planting with the other bounded detector rows bounds each averaged swept correlation by a constant times its vanishing root bound, uniformly on these neighborhoods. Consequently the matrix \(Q_i = (L_i \Pi_i u_{j, i})_j\) is within \(\delta\) of \(G\) eventually in \(I\).

It follows that the \(r\) vectors \(\Pi_i u_{j, i}\) are independent and therefore span \(\text{ran} \hspace{0.17em} \Pi_i\). Write \(\Pi_i v = \sum_j c_j \Pi_i u_{j, i}\). If \(A\) bounds \(\| \Pi_i \|\), then

\(\displaystyle | \Pi_i v| \leqslant ArB_k |c| \leqslant 2 ArB_k K |L_i \Pi_i v| .\)

Take \(Z \geqslant 1\) at least this last constant. This proves the detector bound on every vector, not merely on the purchased columns.

For its assay properties, use the same separated neighborhoods and plant the distinguished trial with the bounded primary spectators in distinct cells. If its root bound is \(u_i\), the result is bounded by \(Cu_i\), with \(C\) independent of \(N, p\) and the point in the neighborhood. Averages have total mass one, so this also proves assertion (2). For (1), move differences through the expectation by linearity. One box average telescopes a difference exactly:

\(\displaystyle \frac{1}{h_N} \sum_{j = 0}^{h_N - 1} \frac{H (x + (j + 1) e_{\alpha}) - H (x + je_{\alpha})}{a_N} = \frac{H (x + h_N e_{\alpha}) - H (x)}{h_N a_N} .\)

The difference and averaging operators commute. Applying this identity once for each entry of \(l\) bounds the averaged derivative by \((2 / b)^{|l|}\) times the undifferentiated correlation bound, since \(h_N a_N \geqslant b\). That bound follows from the paid root budget of \(F\) and the bounded spectator budgets. All translated endpoints remain in the chosen neighborhoods. This proves (1) without estimating \(D_l F\) pointwise in advance.\(\Box\)

Proposition 4.31. (All-period payment for the actual difference trials) The retained public family supplies every coefficient, remainder, orientation and root-budget hypothesis of Theorem 4.28. Consequently that theorem's conclusion holds for its actual normalized insertions, with one mesh threshold valid for every period.

Proof. Let \(F\) be a bounded primary row, initially a fixed public label, and \(l\) a finite direction word. At grade \(e = d + |l|\), the encoding lemma gives \(x \in \mathscr{C}_e\) and \(E = O (x) - D_l F\) with uniform vanishing root bound. Apply the detector lemma at this grade and word. Its assay of \(D_l F\) is bounded and that of \(E\) tends to zero. Thus \(|L_i x_i | \leqslant c\) eventually for some \(c\) independent of \(p\).

Put \(y_i = \Pi_i x_i\) and \(z_i = (1 + |y_i |)^{- 1} (x_i - y_i)\). The first term in this subtraction belongs to \(\mathscr{C}_e\) by its scalar closure, and the second is norm-bounded by one. Thus \(z \in \mathscr{C}_e\), and \(\Pi_i z_i = 0\). The sweep and assay lemmas give \(|L_i z_i | \to 0\) in \(I\). Lemma 4.26 now gives \(|y_i | \leqslant 2 Zc + 1\) eventually. Changing \(y_i\) to zero at earlier mesh cutoffs makes it a globally bounded row without affecting any eventual assertion.

This bounded row has a uniform paid primary budget by the detector lemma. The difference \(x - y\) belongs to \(\mathscr{C}_e\) and has exact projected kernel zero on the tail, so its insertion has a vanishing uniform root bound. The exact identity

\(\displaystyle D_l F = O (y) + O (x - y) - E\)

therefore gives a finite root budget for \(D_l F\), at every sufficiently fine fixed cell depth. Each of its two vanishing bounds is eventually at most one. The root triangle inequality gives the sum of the primary budget and two, with one common mesh threshold valid for all \(p\). Apply the same argument to the finitely many orientations, or retain them in the finite lists throughout; take maxima only after fixing the cell depth. No limit of a moving projection is used.

Finally the periodic difference formula is the definition of these trials and is exact also at wrapped sites. The planted-cell product inequality is the Wilson reflection-positive inequality of Subsection 4.1; its chamber interiority follows from the fixed finite stencils. These verify the remaining hypotheses of the full-mesh theorem. Its two-scale packing argument now applies to each finite list of actual insertions and difference directions. The use of full-mesh estimates has been confined throughout to uniformly subpower rows and uniform primary budgets; no assertion for arbitrary ordered subpower rows has been strengthened by changing its quantifiers.\(\Box\)

Remark 4.32. (What this estimate supplies) The statement is the full-mesh separated-kernel estimate: each finite difference order and each positive separation have one eventual mesh threshold valid at all periods. The bounds at fixed period also control compact families of separated positions and their coordinate ties. The estimate does not itself give convergence of smeared correlations; that is the separate same-history convergence estimate. With both inputs, the positional theorem follows by the interpolation, compactness, and dominated-convergence argument in Subsection 4.9.

4.12From one-field purchases to the common comparison grid

This section isolates the quantitative steps on the selected finite-volume route to the comparison grid. It proves the finite-frame estimate, the radial decay once the trajectory bounds hold, and the realification and four-term assembly steps. The local approximation and separated-pair receipts are substantive upstream inputs to this finite assembly. Their construction from the paid selected history is given in Subsection 13.11.

Write \(a_j\) for the physical spacing at depth \(j\), \(c_j\) for the number of columns there, and \(\sigma_j\) for its running scale. For a coefficient \(L\) of grade \(d\), let \(\|L\|_1\) be its coefficient norm in the finite frame. The bound appearing in the construction is

\(\displaystyle K_j (L) = 2 \exp (\ell_j) \|L\|_1,\)

where \(\ell_j\) is the accumulated column logarithm. The rooted bound of a block of side at depth \(m\), compared with a fine observable at depth \(j\), is

\(\displaystyle S_{m, j} = \exp \hspace{-0.17em} \left( \left( \frac{a_m}{a_j} \right)^4 \mathcal{T}_j + 5 \right),\)

with \(\mathcal{T}_j\) the vacuum treasury. This is the four-dimensional marked-copy estimate (4.10). For depths \(m \leqslant j\) the gain is the calibrated factor

\(\displaystyle \text{gain} (j, m) = e^{(\sigma_j - \sigma_m) / 8}\)

of Subsection 13.9.

Lemma 4.33. (Finite-frame recovery from approximate coordinate atoms) Let \(V\) be a finite-dimensional complex coefficient space with coordinate norm \(\| \cdot \|_1\) and basis \((e_s)_{s \in I}\). Suppose a family \((y_s)_{s \in I}\) satisfies \(\|e_s - y_s \|_1 \leqslant 1 / 4\) for every \(s\). Define \(Av = \sum_{s \in I} v_s y_s\). Then \(A\) is invertible and \(\|A^{- 1} z\|_1 \leqslant 2 \|z\|_1\) for all \(z \in V\).

Proof. For \(v = \sum_s v_s e_s\), the triangle inequality gives

\(\displaystyle \|v - Av\|_1 = \left| \sum_s v_s (e_s - y_s) \right|_1 \leqslant \sum_s |v_s | \|e_s - y_s \|_1 \leqslant \tfrac{1}{4} \|v\|_1 .\)

Consequently \(\|Av\|_1 \geqslant 3 \|v\|_1 / 4\). Thus \(A\) is injective and, by finite dimensionality, surjective. The displayed inequality gives \(\|A^{- 1} z\|_1 \leqslant 4 \|z\|_1 / 3 \leqslant 2 \|z\|_1\).\(\Box\)

Lemma 4.34. (Linear assembly preserves the local bound) Fix a finite coefficient frame and a compact local region \(W\). Suppose the complex basis approximants \(Z_s\) satisfy

\(\displaystyle \sup_x |Z_s (x) | \leqslant A_0, \qquad Z_s \text{ is supported in } W, \qquad \|Z_s - f_s \|_{\text{root}, m} \leqslant \epsilon_0 \quad (s \in I),\)

where \(f_s\) are the finite-volume insertions corresponding to the frame columns. Let \(\mathcal{C}\) denote the centered chamber-interior test class; assume \(Z_s, f_s, Z_s - f_s \in \mathcal{C}\) and, for finite combinations,

\(\displaystyle \rho \hspace{-0.17em} \left( \sum_s t_s H_s \right) \leqslant \sum_s |t_s | \rho (H_s) \qquad (H_s \in \mathcal{C}),\)

where \(\rho\) is the rooted test. If \(L = \sum_s t_s f_s\) with \(\sum_s |t_s | \leqslant K\), then

\(\displaystyle Z = \sum_s t_s Z_s\)

is supported in \(W\), belongs to \(\mathcal{C}\), satisfies \(\sup_x |Z (x) | \leqslant A_0 K\), and

\(\displaystyle \|Z - L\|_{\text{root}, m} \leqslant K \epsilon_0 .\)

Proof. Support is preserved under finite linear combinations. For every \(x\), \(|Z (x) | \leqslant \sum_s |t_s | |Z_s (x) | \leqslant A_0 K\). Also \(Z - L = \sum_s t_s (Z_s - f_s)\), so subadditivity and homogeneity give the root bound. The same closure assumptions give \(Z - L \in \mathcal{C}\). No independence assumption is used: all rooted tests are evaluated in the same finite-volume Wilson law.\(\Box\)

Proposition 4.35. (A finite-depth real approximation receipt) Fix grade \(d\), a star-real coefficient \(L\), a station depth \(J \leqslant N\), an opened intermediate depth \(m\), and a fine depth \(k < N\) with \(J \leqslant m\) and \(C.ref (d) \leqslant m \leqslant k\). The centered block below is the block of the centered tiling at the station depth \(J\) (of physical side \(a_J\)); it carries the support and the rooted test. The depth \(m\) enters only through the opening condition in item 4, the scale inequality and the error \(S_{J, m}\). Assume the following selected-history data at these depths. The rooted quantity below is the centered root test of Definition 4.1, for the same finite-volume law and centered chamber. It is absolutely homogeneous: multiplying the observable by \(t \in \mathbb{C}\) multiplies each of the \(M\) copies, original or conjugated, by \(t\) or \(\bar{t}\), hence the product by a number of modulus \(|t|^M\), and the \(M\)th root by \(|t|\). We assume, in addition, its triangle inequality and its invariance under complex conjugation on the chamber-interior test class; these two properties are inputs here and are not proved in this section.

  1. each coordinate coefficient has a complex local approximation in the window of radius \(R_d + 4\), with supremum bound \(A_0\) and rooted error at most \(S_{J, m} / w\);

  2. the finite-volume insertion columns span the requested insertion \(L\), with coefficient sum at most \(K_k (L)\), and the resulting complex sum \(Z\) lies in the centered chamber interior; that interior is preserved by the real-part projection used below;

  3. transporting each local approximation from the fine depth \(k\) to the cutoff lattice preserves its support inside the centered station block, its supremum bound \(A_0\), and its rooted error bound;

  4. the scales fit: the local window embeds in the block, the station block of depth \(J\) is open at depth \(m\), and

    \(\displaystyle w \hspace{0.17em} \| \text{invDil}_{m, d} \| \frac{4 (1 + c_k)}{\text{gain} (k, m)} \leqslant \frac{\zeta_{d, m}}{2}, \qquad w > 0, \quad \zeta_{d, m} > 0.\)

Then there is a real field \(B_N\) supported in the centered station block, uniformly bounded by \(A_0 K_k (L)\), whose rooted distance from the insertion \(L\) is at most

\(\displaystyle K_k (L) \hspace{0.17em} \frac{S_{J, m}}{w} .\)

The same bound holds at every period once the station and scale inequalities hold there.

Proof. Use the representation and coefficient bound in item 2. Transport the complex coordinate approximants from depth \(k\) to the cutoff lattice, inside the common station block; by item 3 they keep the supremum bound \(A_0\) and their rooted errors. Form their complex linear combination \(Z\). The finite-sum lemma gives \(|Z| \leqslant A_0 K_k (L)\) and rooted error at most \(K_k (L) S_{J, m} / w\). The exact selected-family reality identity says that the target insertion \(U\) is real when \(L\) is star-real. Put \(B = \text{Re} \hspace{0.17em} Z\). Then \(|B| \leqslant |Z|\), and \(\text{paint} (B)\) has the same local support. The selected realification lemma, namely chamber-interior compatibility for averaging a field with its conjugate, gives its chamber interior. Since \(U = \bar{U}\),

\(\displaystyle \text{paint} (B) - U = \tfrac{1}{2} ((Z - U) + \overline{(Z - U)}) .\)

Conjugation preserves the rooted test, and its triangle inequality and homogeneity therefore give the same rooted error bound for \(B\). This is the realification argument of the selected route, not an assumption that the complex coordinate approximants are themselves real. The support condition and scale inequality ensure that all summands are legal tests in one block. Uniformity in period follows from the common station and aligned physical block in the hypotheses.\(\Box\)

Lemma 4.36. (Replacing a finite local word) Let \(\mathcal{I}\) be a finite index set, \(\mathcal{T}\) a finite tiling, and \(\tau_a\) its copy maps, indexed by the cells \(a \in \text{Cell} (\mathcal{T})\). Assume each copy map is additive. Let \(\mu\) be a probability law and \(\rho\) the same nonnegative rooted test on all functions considered. Let \(a : \mathcal{I} \to \text{Cell} (\mathcal{T})\) be injective. Suppose \(X_i, Y_i\) lie in a linear local test class that is closed under cell copies, and suppose all finite products below are integrable.

\(\displaystyle \rho (X_i) \leqslant M, \qquad \rho (Y_i) \leqslant M, \qquad \rho (Y_i - X_i) \leqslant \delta \quad (i \in \mathcal{I}),\)

where \(M, \delta \geqslant 0\). Assume the planted-product estimate

\(\displaystyle \left| \int \prod_{i \in \mathcal{I}} \tau_{a (i)} (Z_i) \hspace{0.17em} d \mu \right| \leqslant \prod_{i \in \mathcal{I}} \rho (Z_i)\)

for every family in this local test class. If \(|\mathcal{I}| = n \geqslant 1\), then

\(\displaystyle \left| \int \prod_{i \in \mathcal{I}} \tau_{a (i)} (Y_i) \hspace{0.17em} d \mu - \int \prod_{i \in \mathcal{I}} \tau_{a (i)} (X_i) \hspace{0.17em} d \mu \right| \leqslant n \delta M^{n - 1} .\)

For \(n = 0\) the two empty products agree and the difference is zero.

Proof. Order the finite index set as \(i_1, \ldots, i_n\), and define \(Z^{(r)}\) to use \(Y_{i_s}\) for \(s \leqslant r\) and \(X_{i_s}\) for \(s > r\). Then the difference of the two products is the sum, for \(r = 1, \ldots, n\), of the product with \(\tau_{a (i_r)} (Y_{i_r} - X_{i_r})\) in position \(r\) and the hybrid factors in all other positions. The copy operation is additive, including its reflected cases, so this is an exact telescoping identity. Each hybrid family remains in the local test class. The planted-product estimate bounds the absolute value of the \(r\)th integral by \(\delta M^{n - 1}\): the changed factor costs at most \(\delta\) and each of the other \(n - 1\) factors costs at most \(M\). Summing these \(n\) bounds proves the claim.\(\Box\)

Remark 4.37. (The selected pair receipt) This is the quantitative replacement step used to turn centered single-word receipts into whole-word product receipts. Its local inputs are: for each copy and each mesh/period in the fused late set, the exact insertion \(X_i\) and real prepared insertion \(Y_i\) lie in the centered chamber-interior test class, both rooted costs are at most a common \(M\), their difference costs at most \(\text{costHint} (k)\), and the prepared field has a finite supremum bound. The finite-dimensional spin frame is absorbed by summing its centered and purchase budgets. For an injective list of slots, the measure's planted inequality is applied to each telescoping term above. Thus the word-product discrepancy is bounded by \(n \hspace{0.17em} \text{costHint} (k) M^{n - 1}\), uniformly over the finite grid positions; concatenating two finite lists gives the four pair receipts used in the reflection-difference assembly. The selected proof still needs the origin receipts that feed the centered single-word estimates, but the many-factor replacement estimate itself is proved here.

Lemma 4.38. (The radial choice makes the coefficient bound affordable) Fix \(d, m, J\) and \(\varepsilon > 0\). Suppose the scale schedule satisfies

\(\displaystyle \sigma_j \longrightarrow + \infty, \qquad \ell_j = o (\sigma_j), \qquad \log (1 + c_j) = o (\sigma_j), \qquad \text{gain} (j, m)^{- 1} \leqslant C_m e^{- \gamma (\sigma_j - \sigma_m)}\)

for some \(\gamma > 0\). Assume \(\| \text{invDil}_{m, d} \| > 0\), \(\zeta_{d, m} > 0\), and \(\text{gain} (j, m) > 0\). Let \(w_j\) be the positive allowance

\(\displaystyle w_j = \frac{\zeta_{d, m} \text{gain} (j, m)}{8 \| \text{invDil}_{m, d} \| (1 + c_j)} .\)

If \(\|L\|_1\) and \(\zeta_{d, m}^{- 1}\) are fixed, then for all sufficiently large \(j\),

\(\displaystyle K_j (L) \hspace{0.17em} \frac{S_{J, m}}{w_j} \leqslant \varepsilon .\)

Proof. For fixed \(d, m, J, L\), all factors except \(\exp (\ell_j)\), \(1 + c_j\), and \(\text{gain} (j, m)^{- 1}\) are constant in \(j\); the remaining treasury ratio in \(S_{J, m}\) is fixed at the chosen \(m, J\). By the first two hypotheses, for any \(\eta > 0\) both \(\exp (\ell_j)\) and \(1 + c_j\) are eventually at most \(\exp (\eta \sigma_j)\). Choose \(\eta < \gamma / 3\). The gain factor is bounded by a constant times \(e^{- \gamma \sigma_j}\), after absorbing \(e^{\gamma \sigma_m}\) into that constant. The product is thus bounded by a constant times \(e^{- (\gamma - 2 \eta) \sigma_j}\), which tends to zero. This gives the claimed threshold.\(\Box\)

Remark 4.39. (The schedule hypotheses) For the calibrated gain the last hypothesis of Lemma 4.38 holds with equality, \(C_m = 1\) and \(\gamma = 1 / 8\). The first two are consequences of a logarithmic-cost bound on the schedule, of the kind used in Subsubsection 7.4.3. Suppose that \(\ell_j = \sum_{i < j} \log c_{i + 1}\), that every column count satisfies \(c_i \geqslant 1\), that \(\sigma_{i + 1} - \sigma_i = \log L_i > 0\) with \(L_i\) the block side, and that there are numbers \(h_i \to \infty\) with \(\log c_{i + 1} \leqslant h_i^{- 2} \log L_i\). Given \(\epsilon > 0\), choose \(k\) with \(h_i^{- 2} \leqslant \epsilon / 2\) for \(i \geqslant k\); telescoping gives \(\ell_j \leqslant \ell_k + \tfrac{\epsilon}{2} (\sigma_j - \sigma_k)\) for \(j \geqslant k\), and \(\ell_k - \tfrac{\epsilon}{2} \sigma_k \leqslant \tfrac{\epsilon}{2} \sigma_j\) eventually because \(\sigma_j \to \infty\). Thus \(0 \leqslant \ell_j \leqslant \epsilon \sigma_j\) eventually, that is, \(\ell_j = o (\sigma_j)\). Since \(c_j \geqslant 1\), \(\log (1 + c_j) \leqslant \log 2 + \log c_j \leqslant \log 2 + \ell_j\), so also \(\log (1 + c_j) = o (\sigma_j)\). That the selected schedule satisfies the logarithmic-cost bound with its column counts included, and diverges, is an input of this section.

Theorem 4.40. (Conditional assembly of the common comparison grid) Fix a finite list of star-real tokens \(v_i\), that is, tokens whose label is fixed by complex conjugation, \(v_i^{\ast} = v_i\), and a generic grid of pairwise separated positions \(x_i\). Write \(H_i\) and \(R_i\) for the exact unreflected and reflected whole-word insertions at common volume, period, and translation; reflection acts on both labels and positions. For each approximation index \(k\), suppose there are real measurable approximants \(F_{k, N, p, t, i}\) and \(G_{k, N, p, t, i}\) to the unreflected and reflected insertions. Assume:

  1. \(A (k) \geqslant 0\), \(\delta (k) \geqslant 0\), and \(\delta (k) \to 0\). For each fixed \(k, t, i\), eventually in the fused mesh/period filter \(f. \text{fuse}\), \(F_{k, N, p, t, i}\) and \(G_{k, N, p, t, i}\) are measurable and bounded in absolute value by the common reserve \(A (k)\).

  2. For each \(k\) and each \(i < j\), there is a translation threshold such that for every \(t\) beyond it, eventually in \(f. \text{late}\) in \((N, p)\), the four approximating products and their target products are integrable and their integrals differ by at most \(\delta (k)\):

    \(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rlll} \int F_i F_j & \leftrightarrow \int \text{Re} (H_i H_j), & \int F_i G_j & \leftrightarrow \int \text{Re} (H_i R_j),\\ \int G_i F_j & \leftrightarrow \int \text{Re} (R_i H_j), & \int G_i G_j & \leftrightarrow \int \text{Re} (R_i R_j) . \end{array}\)

    The arrows mean that the absolute difference of the two real numbers is at most \(\delta (k)\).

Then the real plots \(P_{k, N, p, t, i} = F_{k, N, p, t, i} - G_{k, N, p, t, i}\), with reserve \(2 A (k)\) and error schedule \(4 \delta (k)\), define a covering satisfying the exact Works quantifiers: controls hold eventually in \(f. \text{fuse}\) for each fixed \(k, t, i\); and for every \(k\) and \(i < j\), its pair product differs from the bound

\(\displaystyle \int \text{Re} [(H_i - R_i) (H_j - R_j)]\)

by at most \(4 \delta (k)\) eventually in \(f. \text{late}\) for every sufficiently large translation \(t\). Thus \(k\) is the approximation index whose error tends to zero; it is not the extraction index. This is a conditional finite assembly statement, not a proof of the four local pair receipts.

Proof. Put \(P_i = F_i - G_i\). The bound \(|P_i | \leqslant |F_i | + |G_i | \leqslant 2 A (k)\) proves the reserve assertion. The identity

\(\displaystyle P_i P_j = F_i F_j - F_i G_j - G_i F_j + G_i G_j\)

is an exact identity of real functions. The target bound has the same four-term expansion after taking real parts of the exact complex products \(H_i H_j, H_i R_j, R_i H_j, R_i R_j\). By the four assumed receipts and the triangle inequality, the difference of the two integrals is at most \(4 \delta (k)\). The common late set exists because there are finitely many controls and four receipts per pair. The translation threshold is chosen first, followed by the mesh/period late set. This is precisely the order of quantifiers in Works.\(\Box\)

Remark 4.41. (Selected-route boundary) The public endpoint reaches this construction through the selected run's history records, then uses the finite-dimensional insertion recovery, a finite real sum, a radial choice of the intermediate depth, and the four-term reflection expansion. The arguments above prove the coefficient recovery, finite-sum estimates, radial decay under the displayed scale bounds, and the final four-term assembly. The selected-history receipts supplied by Subsection 13.11 are: the real local coordinate approximations, their rooted one-field errors, the back-transport support and estimate, and the separated pair integral estimates. These are the substantive content of the history lemmas producing the local approximants and their served pair bounds; this section does not treat their names alone as proofs. The public theorem also separately requires same-history full-mesh convergence for positioned correlations. This chapter supplies the real grid estimate on its own hypotheses and does not identify it with that convergence theorem.

4.13The selected one-scale linear interpolation

We next expand the finite construction that turns the one-column transfer records into one coherent coarser coefficient family. It is essential that the transfer records be combined before the coefficient is chosen: choosing separately for each label would not give a single linear coefficient family, and the next scale's cone estimate would not apply. Let \(\mathcal{T}_m\) denote the actual fine-to-coarse density map, \(\mathcal{T}_m f = L^d \text{push}_m \hspace{0.17em} f\) on grade \(d\), where \(L\) is the block scale and \(\text{push}_m\) integrates against the selected transition kernel. It is integration against the selected transition kernel, so its linearity on the displayed integrable columns follows from the probability-kernel identity.

Let \(F\) be the finite coefficient frame, \(V\) a coefficient family at the fine scale, and write \(I\) for its invertible coefficient readout. Assume the admissible identities hold:

    (H1)

    the played coefficient has \(x\)-coordinate \(I (v)\);

    (H2)

    its density is a complex-linear function of \(v\), and the density obeys the stated motion covariance;

    (H3)

    its charge is bounded by \(s \| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} I (v)\|_1\), where \(s\) is the fine column count, and is subadditive in \(v\);

    (H4)

    the readout intertwines the motions: \(I (gv) = gI (v)\) for every motion \(g \in \mathcal{M}\) defined below, including the conjugation flag;

    (H5)

    the coefficient, regular and forced fields of \(V (v)\) are complex-linear in \(v\), and the supports of the regular atoms of \(V (v)\) do not depend on \(v\);

    (H6)

    the readout maps the physical unit at the cutoff to the unit at the coarse scale: \(I (u_N) = u_{m + 1}\), where \(u_m = a_m^d e_0\).

Hypotheses (H4)–(H6) are used in the proof of Proposition 4.43 below: (H4) to identify the recombined coefficient with \(V (v)\) and for covariance of the new readout, (H5) to recover the exponential entry and forced profile of \(V (v)\) from those of the basis columns, and (H6) for the unit statement. For the profile conclusions below assume the coefficient law is sound under local reorientation, the forced profiles transform covariantly, and the local evaluation is nonzero on every configuration. Density covariance alone does not imply covariance of the exponential entry. Let \(L\) be the block scale and \(T\) the selected heading. Set

\(\displaystyle D (z) = \| \textsf{D}^{\text{gr}}_{d, L} (\text{core} \hspace{0.17em} z)\|_1, \quad c = C + \eta s, \quad \epsilon = \eta (1 + s), \quad R_F = \text{orbitCost} (F) .\)

The frame has basis \((e_j)_{j \in J}\) and finite motion set \(\mathcal{M}= \text{Signed} \times \{0, 1\}\); the second coordinate is the independent conjugation flag. Write \(\sigma_g (c) = c\) or \(\bar{c}\) according to that flag, and \(gz\) for the corresponding coefficient action. The exact repainting coefficient is

\(\displaystyle \chi_{g, j} (z) = |\mathcal{M}|^{- 1} \sigma_g ((g^{- 1} z)_j), \qquad R_F = 1 + \sum_{g \in \mathcal{M}} \sum_{j \in J} \|g (e_j)\|_1 .\)

It is complex-linear: applying \(g^{- 1}\) to \(cz\) contributes \(\sigma_{g^{- 1}} (c)\), and the outer \(\sigma_g\) changes it back to \(c\). For fixed \(g\), coordinate expansion gives

\(\displaystyle \sum_j \chi_{g, j} (z) g (e_j) = |\mathcal{M}|^{- 1} z ;\)

summing over \(g\) proves exact reconstruction. The weighted bounds used below are stronger than the unweighted reconstruction bound. For \(h (z) = \| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} z\|_1\) or \(h (z) = \| \textsf{D}^{\text{gr}}_{d, L} (\text{core} \hspace{0.17em} z)\|_1\), coordinate expansion gives

\(\displaystyle \sum_{g, j} | \chi_{g, j} (z) | h (e_j) = |\mathcal{M}|^{- 1} \sum_g h (g^{- 1} z) .\)

For each fixed motion, the weighted coordinate sum is exactly \(h (g^{- 1} z)\), by expanding in the frame basis; the core and grade weights commute with motion. The finite patch-cost estimate gives \(h (g^{- 1} z) \leqslant R_F h (z)\), and therefore yields

\(\displaystyle \sum_{g, j} | \chi_{g, j} (z) | h (e_j) \leqslant R_F h (z) . \) (4.39)

This weighted identity also explains why one must include the motion cost: a motion need not preserve the frame's \(\ell^1\) norm. The same coefficient formula with \(h (z) = \|z\|_1\) gives the unweighted budget used for finite-support additions:

\(\displaystyle \sum_{g, j} | \chi_{g, j} (z) | \leqslant R_F \|z\|_1,\)

by the same finite patch-cost estimate. Assume each grade weight in \(\textsf{D}^{\text{gr}}_{d, L}\) lies in \([1, L^d]\); this is the grading convention used below. The selected purchase inequalities are

\(\displaystyle C \geqslant 1, \quad \eta \geqslant 0, \quad \eta (1 + s) R_F \leqslant \text{stepSmall} (L), \quad (C + \eta s) R_F \leqslant K_{J (m + 1)} . \) (4.40)

For each basis vector \(e_j\), set \(v_j = I^{- 1} e_j\). The fine played column \(V (v_j)\) is formatted and integrable, and its remainder is bounded by its charge. The selected one-column purchase record has three clauses: the principal insertion when \(j \ne 0\), every regular remainder atom with its support and supremum bounds, and every forced atom with its support-mass bound. The forced clause applies to every forced slot of the funded insertion support at which the insertion function is nonzero; the keeper condition places every such slot in the funded insertion support. The unit coordinate uses the exact vacuum transfer record. The density has an absolutely convergent principal-plus-regular-plus-forced expansion: the regular and forced majorants are dominated by the coefficient envelope and remainder bound. For each component, the corresponding purchase clause gives a transfer record. The transfer record merger sums their densities and profiles termwise; Tonelli applies to the nonnegative majorants, and each scalar cost sum is bounded by the given purchase budget. We use the merger in the following form, which this chapter does not prove: for a countable family of formatted coefficients \(P_i\) with the same vacuum, each with remainder bound \(t_i\) at a common positive allowance, with \(\sum_i t_i < \infty\) and \(\sum_i \|P_i .x\|_1 < \infty\), the merged coefficient (whose lead is the absolutely convergent sum \(\sum_i P_i .x\), whose regular atoms are those of the \(P_i\), kept separately, and whose forced coefficient at each address is \(\sum_i P_i . \text{forced}\)) is formatted, has remainder bound \(\sum_i t_i\), and its forced profile is the sum of the forced profiles of the \(P_i\). The summable remainder budgets control the regular and forced series; they do not by themselves control the leads. For the density identity the same lawful representation and the required dominated, measurable density expansion are also retained. The merger, including the coefficient remainder bound and the response decomposition witnesses, is proved in Lemmas 13.32 and 13.33, under the summability and dominated density hypotheses just stated.

In the column application, let \(u_i\) be the predicted lead and let \(x_i\) be the actual lead chosen by the component transfer record, in each of the finitely many required motion orientations. The separate lead-bound receipt must give \(\sum_i \|u_i \|_1 < \infty\) and summable nonnegative errors \(c_i\) with \(\|x_i - u_i \|_1 \leqslant c_i\). Then the triangle inequality gives \(\sum_i \|x_i \|_1 < \infty\). The predicted family has one principal lead \(T (L) e_j\) and zero leads for the regular and forced components, so its lead sum is finite; the summable error and remainder bounds are separate requirements on the purchased transfer records. Subject to these receipts and the merger of Lemma 13.33, this gives the basis transfer record with predicted lead \(T (L) e_j\) and the lead-error budget displayed below. Before simplification its three budgets are

\(\displaystyle \eta (\| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} e_j \|_1 + V. \text{charge} (v_j)), \quad CD (e_j) + \eta V. \text{charge} (v_j), \quad t + 2 V. \text{charge} (v_j) .\)

Each paid column receipt controls its lead after every motion: the transported coefficient \(X_{g, j}\) satisfies \(\|X_{g, j} - T (L) (ge_j)\|_1 \leqslant \epsilon \| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} e_j \|_1\) for all \(g, j\). This is the all-motion clause in the one-column payment; no norm-isometry of a motion is used. The admissible charge estimate gives \(V. \text{charge} (v_j) \leqslant s \| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} e_j \|_1\); \(\| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} e_j \|_1 \leqslant 1\) and \(D (e_j) \geqslant \| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} e_j \|_1\). Hence the budgets are at most \(\epsilon \| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} e_j \|_1\), \(cD (e_j)\), and \(t + 2 s\). The marked coefficient/profile payment is proved in Subsection 9.2. The forced component is paid there after the forced allowance is at most one. The selected primary and regular-card comparisons are supplied by Sections 10.8 and 10.7, assembled on the retained transition in Subsection 10.10. Proposition 13.34 attaches these component estimates to the fixed interfaces under the common selected-history purchase hypotheses. Every component here is evaluated under one background law and one transition kernel.

Lemma 4.42. (The paid one-step cone used in the interpolation) Let \(K \geqslant 1\), \(s \geqslant 1\), \(L \geqslant 1\), and put \(q = L^{1 / 8}\), \(e = q^{- 3}\). Suppose

\(\displaystyle 100000 D^6 (1 + s)^2 \leqslant q, \quad K \leqslant D, \quad 64 D^2 \leqslant c_-, \quad 64 D^2 \leqslant c_+ .\)

Let \(B\) be an invertible linear map with both \(B\) and \(B^{- 1}\) of \(\ell^1\) norm at most \(K\), commuting with deletion of the unit coordinate. For vectors \(u, v\) set \(y = \textsf{D}^{\text{gr}}_{d, L} (v)\) and assume

\(\displaystyle \|u - By\|_1 \leqslant e (\| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} v\|_1 + t), \qquad r \leqslant K\| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} y\|_1 + et, \qquad 0 \leqslant t \leqslant s \| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} v\|_1 .\)

Then

\(\displaystyle r \leqslant c_- \| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} u\|_1, \qquad \|v\|_1 \leqslant c_+ \|u\|_1, \qquad \|u\|_1 \leqslant c_+ L^d \|v\|_1 .\)

Proof. The bank inequality, \(K \leqslant D\), and \(K, D \geqslant 1\) imply

\(\displaystyle 2 Ke (1 + s) = \frac{2 K (1 + s)}{q^3} \leqslant 1.\)

Indeed the assumed lower bound on \(q\) is at least \(2 K (1 + s)\), and \(q \geqslant 1\). The error bound and \(t \leqslant s \| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} v\|_1\) therefore give

\(\displaystyle 2 K \|u - By\|_1 \leqslant \| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} v\|_1 \leqslant \| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} y\|_1,\)

where the second inequality is the coordinatewise fact that all grade-weights in \(\textsf{D}^{\text{gr}}_{d, L}\) are at least one. The elementary perturbation estimate for \(B\) now gives

\(\displaystyle \|y\|_1 \leqslant 2 K \|u\|_1, \qquad \|u\|_1 \leqslant 2 K \|y\|_1 .\)

Apply the same estimate after deleting the unit coordinate. The map \(B\) commutes with that deletion and deletion is contractive, so the same error bound holds for \(\text{core} \hspace{0.17em} u - B (\text{core} \hspace{0.17em} y)\). Thus

\(\displaystyle \| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} y\|_1 \leqslant 2 K \| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} u\|_1 .\)

Also

\(\displaystyle et \leqslant e (1 + s) \| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} v\|_1 \leqslant \| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} y\|_1,\)

since \(2 Ke (1 + s) \leqslant 1\) and \(2 K \geqslant 1\). Hence

\(\displaystyle r \leqslant 2 K \| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} y\|_1 \leqslant 4 K^2 \| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} u\|_1 \leqslant c_- \| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} u\|_1 .\)

Finally, the grade weights also satisfy \(\|y\|_1 \leqslant L^d \|v\|_1\). Consequently

\(\displaystyle \|v\|_1 \leqslant \|y\|_1 \leqslant 2 K \|u\|_1 \leqslant c_+ \|u\|_1, \quad \|u\|_1 \leqslant 2 K \|y\|_1 \leqslant 2 KL^d \|v\|_1 \leqslant c_+ L^d \|v\|_1 .\)

The two room assumptions imply the needed \(4 K^2 \leqslant c_-\) and \(2 K \leqslant c_+\).\(\Box\)

Proposition 4.43. (Common linear interpolation of the purchased columns) Assume \(s \geqslant 1\), \(L \geqslant 1\), and \(t \geqslant 0\). Write the heading as \(T (L) = B \circ \textsf{D}^{\text{gr}}_{d, L}\), where \(B\) commutes with core deletion and \(\|B\|_1, \|B^{- 1} \|_1 \leqslant D_0 := K_{J (m + 1)}\). Assume the bank and room bounds

\(\displaystyle 100000 D_0^6 (1 + s)^2 \leqslant L^{1 / 8}, \qquad 64 D_0^2 \leqslant c_-, \quad 64 D_0^2 \leqslant c_+,\)

with \(\text{stepSmall} (L) = L^{- 3 / 8}\) and the purchase inequalities (4.40). These are exactly the scalar hypotheses of Lemma 4.42, with \(K = D = D_0\). Assume that the fine coefficient family \(V\) satisfies the admissible identities (H1)–(H6) and the profile hypotheses stated before (4.40). The basis transfer records have their stated exact density/profile identities, summable remainder and lead-error bounds as above, and a common gauge interface. Assume the merger of Lemma 13.33 applies to these component transfer records. Their insertion supports are the motion images of the common set of insertion types. There is a coarser coefficient family \(Q\) with:

  1. every \(Q (v)\) is formatted and has remainder bound \(a (v) = cR_F D (Iv)\) at the coarse forced allowance;

  2. its readout \(I_Q (v) = Q (v) .x\) is a linear isomorphism;

  3. at the centered anchor, the exact transition identity \(Q (v) . \text{density} =\mathcal{T}_m (V (v) . \text{density})\) holds;

  4. its lead discrepancy satisfies

    \(\displaystyle \|Q (v) .x - T (L) I (v)\|_1 \leqslant \epsilon R_F \| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} I (v)\|_1 ;\)

  5. the finite-support witness for the exponential entry and forced profile of \(V (v)\) has budgets

    \(\displaystyle R_F \|I (v)\|_1 (t + 2 s) \quad \text{and} \quad a (v),\)

    respectively.

The coefficient, regular, and forced fields are complex-linear in \(v\). The density and readout obey motion covariance, while the forced profile and exponential entry obey the corresponding covariance after reindexing their address dictionaries; the conjugation flag is independent of orientation.

Proof. For each basis coordinate put \(v_j = I^{- 1} e_j\). Its played coefficient has \(x\)-coordinate \(e_j\) by the admissible identity. Let \(Z_j\) be the coefficient from its elementary transfer record. For each motion \(g\), the transition slice has a move taking the coarse origin to the required fine anchor. Reorient \(Z_j\) by that move and, on the conjugating branch, apply complex conjugation to all coefficient fields. The centered transport identities give a coefficient \(Z_{g, j}\) with

\(\displaystyle Z_{g, j} .x = g (Z_j .x), \qquad Z_{g, j} . \text{density} = g \hspace{-0.17em} (\mathcal{T}_m (V (v_j) . \text{density})),\)

with the same remainder and two response decomposition budgets. The remainder assertion follows from the turned tab estimate and the transfer record's future bound; the finite insertion support is carried to its image under the move. Soundness of the transition interface gives the common transport, and the side condition \(2 \leqslant n_m\) makes every move admissible on the selected schedule. This explains the coordinate, format/remainder, density, and insertion support transport used below.

Index these transported coefficients by the finite set

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{I}= \{(g, j) : g \text{ a signed motion}, j \text{ a frame coordinate} \} .\)

For a vector \(v\), assign weight

\(\displaystyle w_v (g, j) = \chi_{g, j} (Iv) .\)

The finite orbit coefficients are the averaging/rebasing coefficients; their identity is the finite reconstruction formula

\(\displaystyle Iv = \sum_{(g, j) \in \mathcal{I}} w_v (g, j) \hspace{0.17em} g (e_j) . \) (4.41)

Define \(Q (v)\) by summing the transported coefficients with these weights, using the disjoint-address merge that retains their support and label data. Equation (4.41) makes the operation complex-linear in \(v\). Since the index set is finite, there is no limiting argument in this assembly.

The \(x\)-coordinate is the weighted sum of the transported column coordinates. Its leading part is reconstructed by the finite motion average, since \(T (L)\) intertwines the motion action. For the error use the paid all-motion estimate and (4.39) with \(h (z) = \| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} z\|_1\):

\(\displaystyle \begin{aligned} \|Q (v) .x - T (L) Iv\|_1 & \leqslant \sum_{g, j} | \chi_{g, j} (Iv) | \hspace{0.17em} \|X_{g, j} - T (L) (ge_j)\|_1\\ & \leqslant \epsilon \sum_{g, j} | \chi_{g, j} (Iv) | \| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} e_j \|_1\\ & \leqslant \epsilon R_F \| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} Iv\|_1 . \end{aligned}\)

Each transported remainder has bound \(cD (e_j)\). Summing its absolute coefficient-weighted bound and using (4.39) with \(h (z) = \| \textsf{D}^{\text{gr}}_{d, L} (\text{core} \hspace{0.17em} z)\|_1\) gives

\(\displaystyle \sum_{g, j} | \chi_{g, j} (Iv) | cD (e_j) \leqslant cR_F D (Iv) .\)

The finite coefficient merge preserves formatting and adds these remainder budgets, proving item 1.

For the density, linearity of the transition integral is justified by the integrability of each played column. Motion covariance of the admissible density identifies the transported transfer record density with the corresponding motion of the transitioned basis density, so that \(Z_{g, j} . \text{density} =\mathcal{T}_m (V (gv_j) . \text{density})\). By (H4), \(gv_j = gI^{- 1} e_j = I^{- 1} (ge_j)\). Sum with the weights in (4.39) and use the linearity of the density (H2) and of \(I^{- 1}\): the result is \(\mathcal{T}_m\) applied to the density of \(V \left( I^{- 1} \sum_{g, j} \chi_{g, j} (Iv) \hspace{0.17em} ge_j \right) = V (v)\), by (4.41). The same computation with the regular and forced fields, which are complex-linear in \(v\) by (H5), recovers the forced profile of \(V (v)\) from those of the basis columns; the exponential entry then follows from the dressing identity below. Without (H4) the weighted sum would be \(V\) evaluated at \(\sum_{g, j} \chi_{g, j} (Iv) \hspace{0.17em} gI^{- 1} e_j\), which need not equal \(v\). The regular and forced address arrays are finite coefficient sums, hence complex-linear in \(v\); their address dictionaries are reindexed by motion and need not themselves be invariant. The admissible density is motion-covariant by hypothesis. The forced profile covariance is an explicit assumption. For the exponential entry, use the coefficient identity \(\text{density} (U) = \text{eval} (U) \hspace{0.17em} \text{exponentialEntry} (U) + \text{forcedDress} (U)\). The profile covariance and lawful dressing identity give covariance of the last term; subtract it from the density covariance identity and divide by the nonzero local evaluation. This proves the needed exponential-entry covariance. Covariance of the new readout uses (H4) once more: the weights satisfy \(\chi_{g, j} (I (hv)) = \chi_{g, j} (hIv)\) for every motion \(h\). Thus the readout and the two profile outputs obey the finite motion reindexing identities, without asserting invariance of raw address arrays. Every summand is supported on the same common stencil, so the output support is independent of \(v\). The unit identity follows from (H6): \(I (u_N) = u_{m + 1}\), the core of the unit coordinate vanishes, so the lead error is zero, and the heading preserves the unit coordinate; the unit at each scale is \(u_m = a_m^d e_0\).

For each \(v\), put \(u = Q (v) .x\) and \(y = \textsf{D}^{\text{gr}}_{d, L} (Iv)\). The heading map gives the two-sided map bound with \(K = K_{J (m + 1)}\); (4.40) bounds the leading error by \(\text{stepSmall} (L) \| \text{core} (Iv)\|_1\) and the new charge \(a (v) = cR_F D (Iv)\) by \(K \| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} y\|_1\). Heard gives \(V. \text{charge} (v) \leqslant s \| \text{core} (Iv)\|_1\). Apply Lemma 4.42 with the room and bank bounds. It yields the new charge cone and the forward/backward coordinate estimates. In particular, if \(Q (v) .x = 0\), its estimate \(\|Iv\|_1 \leqslant c_+ \|Q (v) .x\|_1\) gives \(Iv = 0\), and therefore \(v = 0\). Thus the linear coordinate map of the new coefficient family is injective and, in finite dimension, is an isomorphism.

Finally, transport each finite-support witness to its motion address, multiply it by \(w_v (g, j)\), and take their finite union. The finite repainting identity, motion covariance, (H4) and the linearity (H5) show, as for the density above, that the exponential entries and forced profiles sum to those of \(V (v)\). The first budget is bounded by

\(\displaystyle \sum_{(g, j)} |w_v (g, j) | (t + 2 s) \leqslant R_F \|Iv\|_1 (t + 2 s),\)

while the second is the already proved remainder-bound sum \(a (v)\). The finite-support addition rule therefore gives the required response decomposition. This proves item 5 and completes the common-pencil construction.\(\Box\)

The proposition is the finite assembly used by the selected one-scale transition. Its three purchase clauses do not have the same status in this monograph. The forced clause is supplied by the large-projection estimate after the forced allowance is at most one and the keeper contains every nonzero slot. The other two are supplied on the retained interpolation by Sections 10.7 and 10.8, with their common-history attachment in Proposition 13.34: (regular clause) the regular-card estimate for the small-support, vanishing-jet principal term; and (main clause) for each nonunit basis coordinate, the selected primary-card and budget-card comparison. Neither follows from the finite pencil. Once the exact component receipts are supplied, their absolutely convergent density decomposition is merged using the merger of Lemma 13.33, the summable lead and remainder receipts, and the dominated-series argument above, and the finite-support merger sums the two budgets using the same motion coefficients. No compactness or diagonal extraction is involved.

4.14Same-run Cauchy input for the full mesh

We record the actual Cauchy route used to upgrade the positioned limit to the whole mesh. This is a same-run statement. Fix a selected run and its marked lifting, Winter witness \(H\), and Pillars witness \(W\), together with the chosen growth function \(f_i\) and the selected history tables \(C_i\). For a fixed period \(p\), word length \(n\), list of local labels \(v\), and physical tuple \(x\) which is both non-hit and an ancestor point, write

\(\displaystyle o_j (x) = \text{wrap}_{a_j} (\text{address}_{a_j} (x)), \qquad U_i (x) = C_i . \text{numeric} (f_i, p, v, o_{f_i} (x)) .\)

We show that \((U_i (x))_i\) is Cauchy, conditional on the growth, bound, transfer and geometric receipts of one fixed run listed at the end of this section; the same conclusion is proved in Subsection 4.8. For the full-mesh conclusion at the end of this section we use the specialisation \(f_i = i\) for every \(i\) and \(C_i = C\), one fixed selected history table for all cutoffs; the grade-dependent reference depth that enters the receipts is a separate function \(\psi\) of the grade, not the cutoff map. With this specialisation \(U_i (x)\) is the finite-volume correlation of the fixed family at the cutoff \(i\) itself. For a general growth function or \(i\)-dependent tables, Cauchyness of \((U_i (x))_i\) would not by itself give convergence of the cutoff sequence of one family. This section proves the Cauchy implication and the analytic interpolation step. The selected-history limits and fixed-level comparisons supplying the inputs below are constructed in Subsections 14.21 and 10.13.

The Cauchy step uses one quantitative insertion comparison. We state its exact logical form so the upstream estimate is visible. Fix a finite label set \(A\), a reference rung \(k\), a later rung \(j\), and a terminal mesh index \(i\). Let \(U_i\) be the full-volume pilot and \(V_{j, i}\) its fixed-rung interpolant. Suppose \(F_0 < \infty\) is independent of \(j\), \(G_j \to \infty\), and, for every sufficiently large fixed \(j\), the common-trajectory receipts give

\(\displaystyle |U_i - V_{j, i} | \leqslant \frac{F_0}{G_j / 2} \quad \text{for all sufficiently large } i, \) (4.42)

and \((V_{j, i})_i\) is Cauchy at each such fixed \(j\). A sufficient exact condition for this fixed-rung Cauchyness is that its finite mode integrand is a polynomial in finitely many measurable coefficient fields that converge pointwise, these mode integrands have a common integrable dominator, and their positive partition normalizers converge to a positive limit. Dominated convergence then gives convergence of the numerator integrals, and division by the normalizers gives convergence of \(V_{j, i}\). Under these hypotheses \((U_i)_i\) is Cauchy: given \(\varepsilon > 0\), first choose \(j\) so that \(F_0 / (G_j / 2) < \varepsilon / 4\), then take the maximum of the threshold in (4.42) and the fixed-\(j\) Cauchy threshold for error \(\varepsilon / 4\). For later \(i, i'\),

\(\displaystyle |U_i - U_{i'} | \leqslant |U_i - V_{j, i} | + |V_{j, i} - V_{j, i'} | + |V_{j, i'} - U_{i'} | < 3 \varepsilon / 4 < \varepsilon .\)

Lemma 4.44. (Holomorphic insertion estimate) Let \(S > 2\) and let \(\Phi\) be holomorphic on \(B (0, S)\). If \(| \Phi (z) | \leqslant F_0\) for all \(|z| < S\), then

\(\displaystyle | \Phi (1) - \Phi (0) | \leqslant F_0 / (S / 2) . \) (4.43)

Proof. Put \(r = S / 2\). For every \(w\) with \(|w| \leqslant 1\), the closed disk \(\bar{B} (w, r)\) lies in \(B (0, S)\) since \(1 + S / 2 < S\). The Cauchy integral formula on its boundary gives \(| \Phi' (w) | \leqslant F_0 / r\). Integrating the derivative on the real segment from \(0\) to \(1\) proves (4.43).\(\Box\)

Lemma 4.45. (Uniform confluence supplies the fixed-rung vault) Let \(I\) be a finite set, let \(\mathcal{B}\) be a finite set, and let \(\mathcal{X}\) be a nonempty set. Suppose \(F_{n, i} : \mathcal{X} \to \mathbb{C}\) and \(G_{n, i, b} : \mathcal{X} \to \mathbb{C}\) have uniform eventual bounds: for each \(i \in I\) there is \(c_i \geqslant 0\) such that eventually \(\sup_{x \in \mathcal{X}} |F_{n, i} (x) | \leqslant c_i\), and for each \(i, b\) there is \(d_{i, b} \geqslant 0\) such that eventually \(\sup_{x \in \mathcal{X}} |G_{n, i, b} (x) | \leqslant d_{i, b}\). For any weights \(w_i > 0\), there are finite \(x_0, y_0\) such that, eventually in \(n\),

\(\displaystyle w_i |F_{n, i} (x) | \leqslant x_0, \qquad \sum_{b \in \mathcal{B}} w_i |G_{n, i, b} (x) | \leqslant y_0 \quad (i \in I, x \in \mathcal{X}),\)

where one may take

\(\displaystyle x_0 = \sum_{i \in I} w_i c_i, \qquad y_0 = \sum_{i \in I} \sum_{b \in \mathcal{B}} w_i d_{i, b} .\)

Proof. There are only finitely many bounds. Intersect their eventual tails to get one index \(n_0\) after which all hold at once. For each fixed \(i\), positivity of the weights and nonnegativity of every summand give \(w_i |F_{n, i} (x) | \leqslant w_i c_i \leqslant x_0\). Likewise \(\sum_b w_i |G_{n, i, b} (x) | \leqslant \sum_b w_i d_{i, b} \leqslant y_0\). No compactness or extraction is needed.\(\Box\)

This is the two-part vault bound used for the scalar and box-marked fields. In the application, the eventual uniform bound over configurations for each fixed rung and each field is the eventual uniform boundedness supplied by the fixed-level comparison of Subsection 10.13, as in (4.23); it is an input here. Since the label set and the box set of a fixed finite torus are finite, their thresholds can be intersected once.

The fixed-rung vaults in this argument are produced by the same history, not by an additional choice of subsequence. At a fixed rung \(j\), the retained history interpolation gives uniform confluence of each scalar insertion and each box-marked insertion. Applying Lemma 4.45 to the finite label set, with weights \(w_i\), gives the constants \(t, v\) in the later-rung vault. Applying it at the opened rung \(k\) gives \(x, y\) in the opened-rung vault. The constants may depend on \(k, j\), the finite labels, and the weights, but the opened-rung constants used in the Cauchy comparison are held fixed as \(j\) varies. The positive weights are obtained from the paid allowances \(B_i\) by \(w_i = \zeta_i / (|B_i | + 1)\); indeed \(w_i |B_i | \leqslant \zeta_i\) and \(w_i > 0\) when \(\zeta_i > 0\).

In the public same-run application, these are actual receipts of the selected history. Its branch-stability estimate gives uniform confluence at every fixed rung for the scalar and box-marked fields, and its credit estimate gives the finite constants \(B_i\). The preceding finite sum therefore supplies the two vaults required at the opened rung and at each later rung. The history's scale profile supplies the positive weights and their march relation. Its selected position map supplies the remaining geometry: ancestor-point separation gives injectivity at the chosen rung, hence occupancy one, and continues to give the required Drake inequalities at later rungs. These properties are intersected with the eventual pond, positive-reserve, and delay tails before fixing the rung; thus the estimates are simultaneous for the finite label family and hold along the original volume sequence.

To apply Lemma 4.44, let \(P_z (U)\) be the selected census-mode coefficient of the restored field at rung \(j\), with its principal and forced field families multiplied by \(z\), and put

\(\displaystyle \Phi (z) = Z_{N, p}^{- 1} \int P_z (U) \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_j (U) .\)

The finite restored polynomial depends polynomially on \(z\). The quantitative majorant establishing integrability of its coefficients is given below.

Here is the selected-field verification of its uniform bound, conditional on the served-slab and uniform bound receipts at rung \(j\). Write \(B_j\) for its Drake separation scale and \(E_j\) for the common support exponent. Assume \(B_j \geqslant 8\), \(E_j \geqslant 2\), and \(S_j = e^{B_j / 64} > 2\). The last inequality is a separate late-scale condition; it does not follow from \(B_j \geqslant 8\) alone. For a nonzero polynomial component on a support \(X\), each nonzero mode has at least two markers, and the support-placement condition puts every named origin of those markers in \(X\). Drake separation says distinct origins of that mode are at max-distance at least \(B_j^2\). The connected-halo tour estimate in Subsection 4.2 therefore gives

\(\displaystyle B_j \hspace{0.17em} |I| \leqslant 64 \text{cover} (B_j, X), \qquad \text{cover} (B_j, X) \geqslant B_j / 64,\)

for its marker set \(I\). Thus \(|z| < e^{B_j / 64} \leqslant e^{\text{cover} (B_j, X)}\). Scaling the component by \(z\) multiplies its envelope by \(|z|\). Its original weighted support bound contains the factor \(e^{E_j \text{cover} (B_j, X)}\); since \(|z| \leqslant e^{2 \text{cover} (B_j, X)}\), the same bound pays the scaled envelope at exponent \(E_j - 2\). This argument applies to every active support in both the principal and forced families; zero components cost nothing. The correction families are scaled, while the exponential-entry and marked activity carriers are fixed. The support, gauge, and minimum-degree conditions remain true under scalar multiplication; the envelopes in the support ledger scale by \(|z|\). Hence the same served decomposition with those scaled correction families witnesses servedness of the restored field at exponent \(E_j - 2\). This does not assert that the restore operation itself is linear. For integrability, the vault bounds the finitely many scalar and marked fields and the served ledger bounds the finitely many correction coefficients. The selected lawfulness upper bound for the action and the vacuum-activity envelope give the pointwise exponential majorant (4.9); all coefficient fields are measurable. Expanding the finite restored polynomial therefore gives a measurable, integrable majorant for each coefficient of \(P_z\), uniform for \(z\) in the disk. The prior is a probability measure. Thus the integral defining \(\Phi\) is holomorphic on \(B (0, S_j)\).

To pass to the opened rung, the served witness at the lowered exponent is propagated by the downward servedness induction: at each intervening scale the retained matchup and wake data give the transition hypotheses, and the exact push identity fixes each marked coefficient. The induction's integrability step follows from the transition's finite probability mass. This is the served-transfer result of Subsection 4.2, with its displayed one-step response and payment hypotheses; Fubini supplies the mode-integral identity below, but by itself does not imply servedness. The backward transition from rung \(j\) to \(k\) is finite, and at each transition, if \(K_m (U, V)\) denotes its kernel from finer configuration \(U\) to coarser configuration \(V\), its normalization is \(\int K_m (U, V) \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_m (V) = 1\) for every \(U\). For each integrable coefficient \(q\), the push identity is

\(\displaystyle q_m (V) = \int K_m (U, V) q_{m + 1} (U) \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_{m + 1} (U) .\)

Moreover, nonnegativity and normalization give

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} & \int \hspace{-0.17em} \int K_m (U, V) |q_{m + 1} (U) | \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_{m + 1} (U) d \mu_m (V)\\ & \qquad = \int |q_{m + 1} (U) | \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_{m + 1} (U) < \infty . \end{array}\)

Fubini therefore proves

\(\displaystyle \int q_m (V) \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_m (V) = \int q_{m + 1} (U) \left( \int K_m (U, V) \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_m (V) \right) d \mu_{m + 1} (U) = \int q_{m + 1} (U) \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_{m + 1} (U) .\)

This proves the mode-integral preservation at each transition. The opened-rung vaults and finite-volume mode estimate (4.10) then give exactly

\(\displaystyle | \int P_z \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_j | \hspace{0.17em} \omega_w (A) \leqslant Z_{N, p} \exp (| \text{Site}_k |\mathcal{T}_k + |A| (x + y + 2)),\)
\(\displaystyle | \Phi (z) | \leqslant F_0 := \frac{\exp (| \text{Site}_k |\mathcal{T}_k + |A| (x + y + 2))}{\omega_w (A)} .\)

All weights, the opened rung, and its vault budgets are fixed as \(j\) varies, so \(F_0\) is independent of \(j\). This checks the bound hypothesis of the holomorphic estimate from the explicit scaling, backward transfer, and integrated-mode receipts.

In the public pilot comparison, the finite transition recurrence identifies \(U_i = \Phi (1)\) and \(V_{j, i} = \Phi (0)\), with the same terminal law and normalization. The terminal birth identity supplies the value at \(z = 1\); the later-rung restoration with its correction families set to zero supplies the value at \(z = 0\). The opened rung \(k\) is used to bound this value after transfer. The transition preserves each mode integral, so this identification introduces no change of partition function. Thus (4.43) is precisely (4.42), not an additional compactness input.

There is an exact finite identity connecting this pilot observable to the ordinary finite-volume Wilson observable. For the census mode containing all \(n\) labels, all copy switches are the identity. The product of the local insertions is therefore exactly the finite-volume numeric observable, at the same address tuple \(o_{f_i} (x)\). Thus Cauchyness of the pilot sequence is Cauchyness of \(U_i (x)\) itself; this identity introduces no error term and no subsequence.

To pass from ancestor points to the full positional function, use that ancestor points are dense. The full-mesh local Lipschitz receipt on a compact subset of the non-hit set, together with \(a_i \to 0\), gives asymptotic uniform equicontinuity there: if the continuum points are within \(\delta / 2\), their lattice addresses are within \(\delta\) once \(2 c_n a_i < \delta / 2\). For compact \(K \subset U = \text{NoHit}\), choose a compact neighborhood \(K \Subset K \prime \Subset U\) and a common asymptotic modulus on \(K \prime\). A finite \(\delta / 3\)-net for \(K\) can be chosen with centers in the dense ancestor set intersected with \(U\). Pointwise Cauchyness at these finitely many centers gives one threshold. For any two later indices and any \(x \in K\), compare their values at \(x\) to the value at a net center: the two modulus errors are each below \(\varepsilon / 3\), and the center values differ by less than \(\varepsilon / 3\). Hence the functions are uniformly Cauchy on \(K\); completeness gives a uniform limit, and the same modulus makes it continuous. The generic finite-difference bounds in Subsection 4.11 provide the required bounds and local Lipschitz receipts from the same run. The application is conditional on the following same-run receipts: uniform branch confluence and a paid credit for each fixed finite label set; the pond, migration, occupancy, and Drake data needed to construct the transition fields; a served witness at each chosen later rung with its displayed support-estimates; the finite downward service-transfer hypotheses; and a fixed-rung Cauchy sequence. It also uses the full-mesh local Lipschitz bound on each compact subset. Given these receipts, (4.42) is the quantitative insertion estimate just proved, and the pointwise Cauchy and compact-uniform limits required by the full-mesh positioned construction follow for every period, length, and label word. Together with the same-run finite-difference bounds in Subsection 4.11, and with the specialisation \(f_i = i\), \(C_i = C\) fixed above, these receipts yield the smooth positioned correlation along the full mesh, without further extraction. The same conclusion, for the cutoff sequence itself, is stated in Subsection 4.8. The per-label transition fields used in the served-slab step are among these inputs. The marked coefficients and one-step comparisons are constructed in Subsection 13.10, using the component merger of Lemma 13.33. Subsection 4.2 gives the centered estimates from these transition data.
